### [1] [libNLPBE: An Open-Source Python Package for Solving the Non-Linear Poisson-Boltzmann Equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28908)
*Jun-Hyeong Kim,Weitao Yang*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: libNLPBE is an open-source Python library that solves the non-linear Poisson-Boltzmann equation (NLPBE) for molecular systems in combination with density functional calculations, providing electrostatic correction terms for the Fock matrix. It employs density fitting approximation, a modified Damped Inexact Newton Multigrid method with symmetric preconditioning, algebraic multigrid, and GPU acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ionic effects in electrolyte solutions significantly alter solute chemical properties, and the NLPBE is an efficient implicit solvent model for electronic structure calculations. However, limited availability of NLPBE solvers for molecular electronic structure calculations hinders theoretical investigation into ionic effects.

Method: The library uses density fitting approximation for efficient solute electrostatic potential calculation. A modified Damped Inexact Newton Multigrid method (mDINMH) is developed, featuring a symmetric preconditioner for solving the Newton equation, enabling robust multigrid methods for symmetric linear operators. An algebraic multigrid method supports a broad range of grid points, and a GPU-accelerated version leverages parallelization efficiency.

Result: The paper presents libNLPBE as an accessible open-source Python library that solves NLPBE for molecular systems combined with density functional calculations, specifically obtaining electrostatic correction terms for the Fock matrix arising from electrolyte solution environments.

Conclusion: libNLPBE improves accessibility to NLPBE solvers for molecular electronic structure calculations, enabling theoretical investigations into ionic effects in electrolyte solutions through an efficient, open-source implementation with GPU support.

Abstract: Solvent environments surrounding a solute can significantly alter its chemical properties. These changes become more prominent in electrolyte solutions, where mobile ions largely influence the solute through electrostatic interactions. A theoretical description of these ionic effects will benefit the design of chemistry performed in electrolyte solutions. As a result, the non-linear Poisson-Boltzmann equation (NLPBE) has emerged as an efficient implicit solvent model for electronic structure calculations to address such effects. However, the limited availability of NLPBE solvers for molecular electronic structure calculations greatly hinders theoretical investigation into the ionic effects. To improve accessibility of the NLPBE, we present libNLPBE, an open-source Python library, that solves the NLPBE for molecular systems in combination with density functional calculations, specifically to obtain electrostatic correction terms arising from the electrolyte solution environment for the Fock matrix. The library employs the density fitting (DF) approximation to efficiently calculate solute electrostatic potentials. Furthermore, we develop the modified Damped Inexact Newton Multigrid developed by Holst (mDINMH) method for solving the NLPBE. The mDINMH features a symmetric preconditioner for solving the Newton equation, which enables the use of robust multigrid methods designed for symmetric linear operators. In addition, an algebraic multigrid method has been incorporated into the mDINMH to support a broad range of grid points. We also present a GPU-accelerated version of libNLPBE to leverage parallelization efficiency of GPUs.

</details>


### [2] [Simulating spectrally resolved exciton--exciton-interaction pump--probe spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29041)
*Kateřina Charvátová,Matteo Bruschi,Pavel Malý*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a microscopic theoretical framework for spectrally resolved exciton-exciton-interaction pump-probe (EEI-PP) spectroscopy in large molecular systems, demonstrating that spectral resolution is essential to disentangle single-exciton relaxation from multi-exciton annihilation dynamics, with applicability to squaraine polymers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While spectrally resolved fifth-order signals have been explored in simplified cases, larger molecular systems have only been analyzed with spectrally integrated signals. This limits the ability to distinguish intertwined single- and multi-exciton dynamics such as relaxation and annihilation.

Method: The authors develop a microscopic theoretical description for spectrally resolved EEI-PP response incorporating realistic lineshapes. They decompose the total signal into individual excitation pathways to reveal how single- and multi-exciton state structures shape spectral features and dynamics, applicable to large molecular systems like squaraine polymers.

Result: The decomposition into individual excitation pathways demonstrates that spectral resolution is needed to distinguish otherwise intertwined single-exciton relaxation and exciton-exciton annihilation dynamics, enabling direct comparison with experimental spectra.

Conclusion: Spectrally resolved EEI-PP spectroscopy, supported by the presented microscopic theory, provides a direct probe of exciton-exciton annihilation and excitation energy transport in extended, nearly isoenergetic molecular landscapes.

Abstract: Exciton--exciton-interaction pump--probe spectroscopy (EEI--PP) captures the fifth-order signal of light-matter interaction, directly probing exciton--exciton annihilation. This allows studying excitation energy transport even in extended, nearly isoenergetic landscapes. Although spectrally resolved fifth-order signals have already been explored in simplified cases, in larger systems only spectrally integrated signals have been analyzed. We present a microscopic theoretical description for spectrally resolved EEI--PP response with realistic lineshapes, revealing how single- and multi-exciton state structure shape the spectral features and dynamics. The description is applicable to large molecular systems such as squaraine polymers, facilitating a direct comparison with experimental spectra. By a decomposition of the total signal into individual excitation pathways, we demonstrate that the spectral resolution is needed to distinguish otherwise intertwined single- and multi-exciton dynamics, such as single-exciton relaxation and exciton--exciton annihilation.

</details>


### [3] [Transition states and dynamical bottlenecks in the Li-NC <-> Li-CN isomerizing reaction with the Lagrangian betweenness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29344)
*P. García-Cuadrillero,F. Revuelta,R. M. Benito,F. Borondo*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper applies the Lagrangian betweenness, a tool originally developed for oceanographic transport, to study the vibrational dynamics of the Li-NC ↔ Li-CN isomerization reaction, demonstrating its ability to identify transition states and other phase-space invariant structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate the applicability of the Lagrangian betweenness method—originally developed for oceanographic transport—to molecular reaction dynamics, specifically for identifying dynamical bottlenecks and transition states in the Li-NC ↔ Li-CN isomerization reaction.

Method: The Lagrangian betweenness, adapted from betweenness-centrality in network theory, is applied to the vibrational dynamics of the Li-NC ↔ Li-CN isomerization reaction to identify dynamical bottlenecks in phase space.

Result: The Lagrangian betweenness successfully identifies transition states at the top of the energetic barrier, as well as other phase-space invariant structures including manifolds, chains of islands, and invariant tori that govern the intricate vibrational dynamics of the system.

Conclusion: The Lagrangian betweenness is a powerful tool for identifying not only transition states but also a broader set of dynamical bottlenecks and invariant structures in molecular reaction dynamics, extending its utility beyond its original oceanographic context.

Abstract: We study the vibrational dynamics that is responsible for Li-NC <-> Li-CN isomerization reaction using the Lagrangian betweenness, a tool recently developed to identify dynamical bottlenecks in the context of oceanographic transport. Following the spirit of the betweenness-centrality metric, which efficiently identifies the nodes that act as bottlenecks in complex networks, the Lagrangian betweenness is similarly capable to identify bottlenecks in dynamical systems. On the one hand, in this paper we show the ability of the Lagrangian betweenness to identify transition states, which are short-lived states that are formed at the top of the energetic barrier that the system must surpass in order to transition the reaction from the (initial) reactants to the (final) products. On the other hand, we also demonstrate that the Lagrangian betweenness is also able to identify other dynamical bottlenecks, and phase-space invariant structures such as manifolds, chains of islands and invariant tori, which are also responsible for the intricate vibrational dynamics of the system.

</details>


### [4] [Site-Specific Vibrational Dynamics to Probe Local and Global Protein Motions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29805)
*Sena Aydin,Markus Meuwly*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper investigates how site-specific vibrational spectroscopic probes (--SCN, --N3, --SNO) attached to alanine residues in lysozyme can encode information about protein dynamics, using machine-learning-based models for bonded and electrostatic interactions. The frequency fluctuation correlation functions reveal that probes can report not only local fluctuations but also collective, low-frequency protein motions, with static components consistent with experimental observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the extent to which vibrational probe labels attached to proteins can capture information about protein dynamics beyond their immediate local environment, specifically exploring whether these probes can report on low-frequency collective protein motions.

Method: Machine-learning-based models are used to describe spectroscopic probes: reproducing-kernel models for bonded interactions and fluctuating minimally distributed charges for electrostatic interactions. Spectroscopic dynamics are characterized via frequency fluctuation correlation functions (FFCFs) computed from simulations of lysozyme with --SCN, --N3, and --SNO labels at various alanine positions.

Result: FFCFs for many probe locations show a non-decaying component on the simulation timescale, indicating slow residual dynamics and incomplete conformational sampling. The magnitude of these static contributions aligns with previous experimental findings, providing qualitative validation. Probes at suitable locations can capture collective protein motions beyond local fluctuations.

Conclusion: Site-specific vibrational probes can report on protein dynamics extending beyond their immediate local environment, and when placed at appropriate locations, they can provide information on collective low-frequency protein motions, validated qualitatively by comparison with experimental observations.

Abstract: Vibrational spectroscopic probes can provide site-specific information on protein structure and dynamics. In this work, the possibility to relate protein motion with the vibrational response for --SCN, --N$_3$, and --SNO labels covalently linked to all alanine-residues in lysozyme is investigated. Depending on the position of the probe along the polypeptide chain, its local hydration, and the dynamics of its environment, the vibrational response can encode not only local fluctuations but also low-frequency, collective motions of the protein. The spectroscopic probes are described using machine-learning-based models for both bonded interactions, represented by reproducing-kernel models, and electrostatic interactions, represented by fluctuating minimally distributed charges. Spectroscopic dynamics are characterized through frequency fluctuation correlation functions (FFCFs). For many probe locations, the FFCFs contain a non-decaying component on the simulation time scale, indicating residual slow dynamics and incomplete sampling of the underlying conformational fluctuations. The magnitude of these static contributions is consistent with previous experimental observations, providing qualitative validation of the simulations. Overall, the results demonstrate that site-specific vibrational probes can report on dynamics extending beyond their immediate local environment and can, at suitable locations, provide information on collective protein motions.

</details>


### [5] [Accelerated Quantum-Assisted Selected Configuration Interaction via Fast-Annealing-Based Determinant Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30482)
*Hayun Park,Younghun Kwon,Hunpyo Lee*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents QASCI, a quantum-assisted selected configuration interaction method that combines selected CI with graph-based block diagonalization of the FCI Hamiltonian. Determinant selection is formulated as QUBO problems solved iteratively via fast annealing. The method achieves chemical accuracy on H8-H18 chains and Li2S with substantially fewer determinants than FCI, and outperforms active-space FCI for N2 when treating the full orbital space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full configuration interaction (FCI) scales exponentially with spin orbitals, and selected CI (SCI) methods reduce cost by retaining important determinants, but the repeated identification of these important determinants remains a major computational bottleneck. The authors aim to accelerate the determinant selection process by leveraging quantum-formulated optimization.

Method: QASCI combines SCI with graph-based block diagonalization (GBBD) of the FCI Hamiltonian. GBBD partitions the Hamiltonian into independent blocks, and within each block, determinant selection is cast as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem. These QUBO instances are solved iteratively using a fast annealing approach to construct the SCI space.

Result: Benchmarked on H8-H18 hydrogen chains and Li2S in STO3G basis, QASCI achieves chemical accuracy while retaining a small and decreasing fraction of determinants as chain length grows, despite exponential growth of FCI space. For N2 in 631G basis, the full-orbital QASCI calculation with 50,000 determinants obtains a lower ground state energy than an FCI calculation in an (12e, 12o) active space.

Conclusion: The combination of QASCI and GBBD substantially reduces the computational cost of determinant selection while preserving FCI-level accuracy, enabling accurate electronic structure calculations in larger orbital spaces.

Abstract: Full configuration interaction (FCI) provides exact electronic structure within a given atomic basis, but its computational cost grows exponentially with the number of spin orbitals. Selected configuration interaction (SCI) methods alleviate this limitation by retaining only the most important Slater determinants. However, the repeated identification of important determinants remains a major computational bottleneck. We present a quantum assisted selected configuration interaction (QASCI) method that combines SCI with graph based block diagonalization (GBBD) of FCI Hamiltonian. The GBBD method partitions FCI Hamiltonian into independent blocks, within which determinant selection problem is formulated as a quadratic unconstrained binary optimization (QUBO) problem. The QUBO problems for selecting determinants to construct SCI space are iteratively solved using a fast annealing approach. We benchmark method on H8-H18 hydrogen chains and Li2S in STO3G basis. For Hn chains, chemical accuracy is achieved while retaining only a small fraction of Slater determinants, and this fraction decreases with increasing n, despite the exponential growth of the FCI Hilbert space. For Li2S, QASCI results remain within chemical accuracy while retaining substantially fewer determinants than the full FCI space. We apply QASCI to N2 using the 631G basis, considering both active orbital and full orbital treatments. The full orbital QASCI calculation, using 50000 determinants, yields a lower ground state energy than an FCI calculation within an active space comprising 12 spin orbitals and 12 electrons. These results demonstrate that the combination of QASCI and the GBBD approach can substantially reduce computational cost of determinant selection while maintaining the accuracy of FCI based electronic structure calculations, thereby enabling accurate calculations in larger orbital spaces.

</details>


### [6] [Frequency-resolved ultrafast electron diffraction: visualizing vibrational dynamics in frequency- and real-space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30868)
*Rosalie Tabarie,Simon P. Neville,Michael Schuurman,Kasra Amini*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The paper introduces frequency-resolved ultrafast electron diffraction (UED), a new analytical technique that applies Fourier transformation to the time-dependent difference pair distribution function (ΔPDF), yielding a two-dimensional frequency-distance map of photoinduced structural dynamics. Applied to allene and 1,2-butadiene following S₁(ππ*) excitation at 200 nm, the method identifies specific vibrational modes (C=C stretching, CCC bending, CH₂ motions) and their internuclear distance contributions, tracks when frequency components appear during dynamics, and demonstrates that few-femtosecond temporal resolution is essential for retrieving these signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While ultrafast electron diffraction provides direct structural information about molecular changes following photoexcitation, it remains challenging to decompose these dynamics into individual vibrational motions. A method that simultaneously resolves vibrational frequencies, internuclear distances, and their time evolution would greatly enhance interpretation of UED experiments.

Method: The authors introduce frequency-resolved UED by Fourier transforming the time-dependent difference pair distribution function (ΔPDF) to create a two-dimensional frequency-distance representation. They apply this method to simulated electron scattering signals from ab initio multiple spawning (AIMS) trajectories for allene and 1,2-butadiene following 200 nm photoexcitation to the S₁(ππ*) state, and vary the pump-probe delay window to map when specific frequency components appear.

Result: The method successfully identifies C=C stretching and CCC bending motions along with their contributing internuclear distances, separates overlapping CH₂ vibrational modes, and reveals that methyl substitution lowers C=C stretching and CCC bending frequencies while adding a new frequency component in 1,2-butadiene during the first 90 fs. The analysis demonstrates that sub-20-fs, and ultimately few-femtosecond, temporal resolution is critical for recovering these frequency components.

Conclusion: Frequency-resolved UED provides a comprehensive three-fold characterization of photoinduced structural dynamics—vibrational frequencies, internuclear distances, and reaction times—offering a powerful tool for interpreting ultrafast structural dynamics experiments and highlighting the importance of attosecond-to-few-femtosecond instrumentation.

Abstract: Ultrafast electron diffraction (UED) provides direct information on changes in molecular structure following photoexcitation, but identifying the individual vibrational motions contributing to these dynamics remains challenging. Here, we introduce frequency-resolved UED, where Fourier transformation of the time-dependent difference pair distribution function ($Δ$PDF) gives a two-dimensional frequency-distance representation of the structural dynamics. We apply this approach to allene and 1,2-butadiene following photoexcitation to the $S_1(ππ^*)$ state at 200 nm, using electron scattering signals simulated from previously-published ab initio multiple spawning trajectories (S. P. Neville et al., J. Chem. Phys., 2016, 144, 014305). We identify C=C stretching and CCC bending motions and the internuclear distances over which they contribute, and separate overlapping CH$_2$ vibrational motions. By changing the pump-probe delay range used for the Fourier transform, we identify when specific frequency components contribute during the excited-state dynamics. Methyl substitution reduces the C=C stretching and CCC bending frequencies and introduces an additional frequency component in 1,2-butadiene during the first 90 fs. We further show the importance of sub-20-fs, and ultimately few-femtosecond, temporal resolution for retrieving these frequency components. Frequency-resolved UED therefore provides the vibrational frequencies, internuclear distances, and reaction times associated with photoinduced structural dynamics.

</details>

### [7] [Direct Evidence of Unconventional Superconductivity in Doped Kagome system RV$_3$Sb$_5$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28848)
*Avior Almoalem,Yuqing Xing,Iksu Jang,Daniel J. Schultz,Grgur Palle,Andrea N. Capa Salinas,Stephen D. Wilson,Rafael M. Fernandes,Jörg Schmalian,Vidya Madhavan*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Using impurity-bound state spectroscopy via scanning tunneling microscopy, the authors investigate optimally doped RbV3Sb5 where the charge-density wave is suppressed, and find fully gapped, multiband superconductivity. The observed particle-hole asymmetric bound states around nonmagnetic defects are incompatible with conventional s-wave and s± pairing, but fully consistent with a chiral order parameter, providing strong evidence for a fully gapped chiral superconducting state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The superconducting pairing symmetry of kagome metals is unresolved because phase-sensitive experiments capable of distinguishing order parameters have been difficult to implement. The authors aim to resolve this by using impurity-bound state spectroscopy as a phase-sensitive probe.

Method: Spectroscopic-imaging scanning tunneling microscopy (SI-STM) is used to measure spatial and energetic characteristics of impurity bound states. Atomic-scale spectroscopy around nonmagnetic defects is performed on optimally doped RbV3Sb5, and the results are compared with theoretical calculations for various pairing symmetries.

Result: The superconducting state is fully gapped with two distinct energy scales, consistent with a multiband order parameter. Pronounced particle-hole asymmetric bound states are observed around nonmagnetic defects. Theoretical comparison shows these bound states are incompatible with conventional s-wave and sign-changing s± pairing but fully consistent with a chiral order parameter.

Conclusion: Impurity-bound-state spectroscopy is established as a powerful phase-sensitive probe of superconductivity in kagome materials, and the results provide strong evidence that optimally doped RbV3Sb5 realizes a fully gapped chiral superconducting state.

Abstract: The superconducting pairing symmetry of kagome metals remains a central unresolved question largely because phase-sensitive experiments capable of distinguishing between different possible order parameters have been difficult to implement. The spatial and energetic characteristics of impurity bound states measured by spectroscopic-imaging scanning tunneling microscopy encode information on the superconducting order parameter. Here, we use impurity-bound state spectroscopy to investigate optimally doped RbV$_3$Sb$_{5}$ where the charge-density wave instability is fully suppressed. We find that the superconducting state is fully gapped and exhibits two distinct energy scales, consistent with a multiband order parameter. Atomic-scale spectroscopy around nonmagnetic defects reveals pronounced particle-hole asymmetric bound states. Comparison with theoretical calculations demonstrates that these bound states are incompatible with conventional $s$-wave and sign-changing $s^{\pm}$ pairing. Our calculations also show that the data are fully consistent with a chiral order parameter. Our results establish impurity-bound-state spectroscopy as a powerful phase-sensitive probe of superconductivity in kagome materials and provide strong evidence that optimally doped RbV$_3$Sb$_{5}$ realizes a fully gapped chiral superconducting state.

</details>


### [8] [Superconductivity and Magnetism in Bi-Ni System: From Bulk to Heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28855)
*Gabriel Sant'Ana,Yutao Xing,Milton A. Tumelero*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper reviews the Bi-Ni system, which comprises two distinct schemes: the NiBi3 intermetallic compound (a 4 K superconductor with complex normal-phase magnetism) and a Bi-Ni bilayer heterostructure (showing strong evidence of time-reversal breaking and triplet pairing). The review covers electronic properties, key results, and open questions in unconventional superconductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unconventional superconductivity involving magnetism—such as time-reversal symmetry breaking, triplet pairing, and proximity effects—has long been pursued for its potential to drive new physics and technologies. The Bi-Ni system offers a convenient and easily preparable platform for investigating such phenomena compared to other candidates like uranium-based compounds.

Method: The authors conducted a detailed review of the main experimental and theoretical results concerning the electronic properties of both Bi-Ni systems: the NiBi3 intermetallic compound and the Bi-Ni bilayer heterostructure.

Result: In NiBi3, complex magnetism influences normal-state electrical properties but does not affect superconducting properties. In the Bi-Ni bilayer heterostructure, strong evidence of time-reversal symmetry breaking and triplet pairing indicates a complex unconventional superconducting state. The ease of sample preparation has accelerated research in this system.

Conclusion: The review summarizes the current understanding of the Bi-Ni system's electronic properties and outlines the main open questions that must be addressed to achieve a complete understanding of unconventional superconductivity in this material system.

Abstract: Unconventional superconductivity with ingredients of magnetism such as time reversal breaking, triplet pairing and proximity effects has been a long time pursued topic in physics and material science as a new source of innovative phenomena and effects to drive further scientific advances and technologies. The Bi-Ni system has emerged as a convenient candidate for the investigation of such phenomenology. Strictly speaking, the term Bi-Ni system refers to two different schemes, first to the NiBi$_{3}$ intermetallic compound, a 4 K superconductor in which a complex magnetism seems to govern electrical properties in the normal phase, but without implication in the superconducting properties. The second Bi-Ni system is not quite a material but a heterostructure formed by a bilayer of Bi and Ni. Here the superconducting phase has to steal the spotlight by showing strong evidence of time-reversal breaking and triplet pairing featuring a complex unconventional state. The ease of preparing the samples, compared to other candidates for such unconventionality, usually Uranium-based compounds, has accelerated the research in this system. Here, we went through a detailed review of the main results about the electronic properties of these two systems as well as a discussion of the main open questions to be solved in order to reach the complete understanding in this topic.

</details>


### [9] [Electronic state of vortices at twin boundaries in a nematic superconductor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28994)
*Keita Goto,Hiroto Adachi,Masanori Ichioka*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper studies local electronic states of vortices in an s±d wave nematic superconductor using Bogoliubov-de Gennes theory for a tight-binding model, both with and without twin boundaries. A key finding is that a single vortex trapped at a twin boundary forms a bound pair of fractional vortices with weakly-induced s±id wave components, and the calculated spatial structures are compared with STM vortex images in FeSe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand vortex core structures and local electronic states in nematic superconductors with s±d wave pairing, and to explain the vortex images observed in STM experiments on the iron-based superconductor FeSe.

Method: Bogoliubov-de Gennes theory applied to a tight-binding model, where nematicity is represented by anisotropy in transfer integrals and attractive nearest-neighbor interactions. The s and d wave components of pair potentials and the local density of states are evaluated, with and without twin boundaries.

Result: A single vortex trapped at a twin boundary consists of a bound pair of fractional vortices accompanied by weakly-induced s±id wave components, even though these components do not appear in zero magnetic field. The calculated spatial structures of local electronic states are compared with STM vortex images in FeSe.

Conclusion: Nematicity and twin boundaries significantly alter vortex core structures, giving rise to fractional vortices and induced s±id pairing components near twin boundaries, which can be related to experimental STM observations in FeSe.

Abstract: Local electronic states of vortices in an s $\pm$ d wave nematic superconductor are studied both in the absence and presence of twin boundaries. The Bogoliubov-de Gennes theory for a tight-binding model is used with its nematicity represented by the anisotropy in the transfer integrals and attractive interactions between the nearest-neighbor sites. We evaluate s and d wave components of the pair potentials and the local density of states, and analyze the effects of nematicity on the vortex core structures with/without twin boundaries. We find that a single vortex trapped at the twin boundary is composed of a bound pair of fractional vortices accompanied by weakly-induced s $\pm$ id wave components, despite that such s $\pm$ id wave components do not appear in a zero magnetic field. The calculated spatial structures of the local electronic states are compared with the vortex image measured by STM experiments in an iron-based superconductor FeSe.

</details>


### [10] [Doping-driven evolution of pairing symmetry in pressurized La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29091)
*Hai-Yang Zhang,Yu-Jie Bai,Fan-Jie Kong*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper investigates the superconducting pairing symmetry in pressurized La₃Ni₂O₇ and its evolution with doping. The undoped compound exhibits s±-wave pairing originating from repulsive interactions mediated by magnetic odd modes. Hole doping drives a transition to dxy-wave pairing via enhanced intrapocket repulsions from magnetic even modes, while electron doping preserves s±-wave pairing even across a Lifshitz transition, showing that the γ Fermi pocket is not essential for superconductivity in bilayer nickelates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the pairing mechanism in pressurized La₃Ni₂O₇, a recently discovered high-temperature superconductor in bilayer nickelates, by investigating how the superconducting pairing symmetry evolves with doping and how different Fermi pockets and magnetic modes contribute to the pairing.

Method: The study analyzes the superconducting pairing symmetry and its doping evolution through a detailed analysis of pairing interactions, considering the Fermi surface topology (α, β, and γ pockets), magnetic odd/even modes, and how these mediate repulsive interactions that determine the pairing state. The doping dependence is examined via hole and electron doping, including regimes where a Lifshitz transition occurs.

Result: The undoped compound favors s±-wave pairing with sign-reversed gap functions between Fermi pockets, driven by repulsive interactions from magnetic odd modes. Hole doping expands the γ pocket, amplifying intrapocket repulsions from magnetic even modes, leading to a transition to dxy-wave pairing. Electron doping preserves s±-wave pairing even deep into the heavily electron-doped regime across a Lifshitz transition, with pairing becoming more robust without the γ pocket due to enhanced spin fluctuations from favorable α-β pocket nesting.

Conclusion: The γ Fermi pocket is not essential for superconductivity in bilayer nickelates. The s±-wave pairing is robust in the absence of this pocket due to enhanced spin fluctuations from α-β pocket nesting. Exploring doping evolution of the superconducting pairing provides a new avenue for testing the pairing mechanism in pressurized La₃Ni₂O₇.

Abstract: We investigate the superconducting pairing symmetry and its doping evolution in pressurized La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$. For the undoped compound, the most favorable pairing state is found to be $s_{\pm}$-wave, characterized by sign reversal of the gap functions between the Fermi pockets. A detailed analysis of the pairing interactions reveals that this unconventional state originates from repulsive interactions mediated by the magnetic odd modes of the bilayer nickelate. Upon hole doping, the $γ$ Fermi pocket expands, which amplifies the intrapocket repulsions on this pocket. These repulsions, driven by the magnetic even modes, gradually dominates the pairing interactions and ultimately drive a transition in pairing symmetry from $s_{\pm}$-wave to $d_{xy}$-wave in the heavily hole-doped regime. In stark contrast, the $s_{\pm}$-wave pairing persists under electron doping, even deep into the heavily electron-doped regime where a Lifshitz transition occurs. This finding suggests that the $γ$ Fermi pocket is not essential for the emergence of superconductivity in bilayer nickelates. In fact, the $s_{\pm}$-wave pairing becomes more robust in the absence of the $γ$ pocket, as spin fluctuations are strongly enhanced by the favorable nesting between the $α$ and $β$ pockets --- a condition guaranteed by Luttinger's theorem and the Fermi surface topology. We believe that exploring the doping evolution of superconducting pairing will open a new realm for testing the pairing mechanism in pressurized La$_3$Ni$_2$O$_7$.

</details>


### [11] [Topological pair density waves in kagome superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29156)
*Jia-Xin Yin,Xianxin Wu,Mark H. Fischer,Xiao-Yu Yan,Yigui Zhong,Kozo Okazaki*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This review paper explores topological pair density waves (TPDWs), an unconventional superconducting state with periodic pairing modulations from finite-momentum pairing, and their potential realization in kagome superconductors such as AV3Sb5 (A = Cs, Rb, K).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To review progress on topological pair density waves, which break time-reversal symmetry in two dimensions via non-trivial relative phases between multiple Q components, and assess their potential realization in kagome superconductors.

Method: The paper introduces the concept of a TPDW starting from the FFLO state, discusses challenges to its realization, examines TPDWs in the kagome lattice intertwined with chiral superconductivity and loop currents (connecting to quantum anomalous Hall effect models), and reviews experimental signatures in AV3Sb5 superconductors including switchable chiral pairing modulations, Bogoliubov Fermi states, the superconducting diode effect, and the anomalous thermal Hall effect.

Result: The review consolidates theoretical and experimental evidence for TPDWs in kagome superconductors, highlighting multiple quantum effects observed in AV3Sb5 materials and establishing connections between finite-momentum pairing, topological matter, and chiral superconductivity.

Conclusion: The paper projects future research opportunities for this correlated topological quantum phase and discusses its broad implications for finite-momentum pairing, topological matter, and chiral superconductivity.

Abstract: The pair density wave (PDW) is an unconventional superconducting state exhibiting periodic pairing modulations due to pairing with finite momentum Q. In two dimensions, multiple Q components of the PDW can have a non-trivial relative phase, breaking time-reversal symmetry and resulting in a topological electronic structure. Here we review progress on exploring such topological PDWs (TPDWs) and discuss their potential realization in kagome superconductors. We first introduce the concept of a TPDW starting from the Fulde-Ferrell-Larkin-Ovchinnikov (FFLO) state and examine the challenges toward its realization. We then discuss the possibility of a TPDW in the kagome lattice, which intertwines with chiral superconductivity and loop currents, thus connecting to models describing the quantum anomalous Hall effect. Furthermore, we review the experimental signatures of a TPDW in AV3Sb5 (A = Cs, Rb, K) superconductors and highlight related quantum effects, including switchable chiral pairing modulations, Bogoliubov Fermi states, the superconducting diode effect, and the anomalous thermal Hall effect. Finally, we project the future research opportunities of this correlated topological quantum phase and discuss its broad implications for finite-momentum pairing, topological matter, and chiral superconductivity.

</details>


### [12] [Controlling Intertwined Electronic Orders in FeSe with Exfoliation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29407)
*Wenyao Liu,Gabriel Natale,Kyung-Mo Kim,Birender Singh,Piyush Sakrikar,Stephen D. Funni,Leo Kondo,Augustin Davignon,Antoine de Lagrave,Kota Ishihara,Michael Geiwitz,Maia G. Vergniory,Takasada Shibauchi,Jun Sung Kim,Michał Papaj,Judy J. Cha,Kenneth S. Burch†*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Exfoliating the unconventional superconductor FeSe into thin flakes suppresses bulk electronic nematic order and switches the superconducting pairing symmetry from s-wave to d-wave, providing a pristine, chemistry-free route to controlling competing electronic orders in 2D superconductors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Controlling intertwined electronic orders in two-dimensional superconductors is key to answering fundamental questions and engineering quantum devices, but existing tuning methods (chemistry, strain, interface engineering) are complex. A pristine alternative for tuning the balance between competing orders is needed.

Method: FeSe is exfoliated into thin flakes. Transport, electron microscopy, and Raman spectroscopy characterize the weakening of nematic order. Angle-dependent Andreev reflection spectroscopy on pristine crystal edges is used, with spectra evolving from zero-energy bound states to coherence peaks as junction orientation is rotated. Injection angle, field, and temperature dependence plus theoretical modeling confirm the superconducting symmetry switch.

Result: Dimensional reduction via exfoliation substantially suppresses bulk electronic nematic response and switches the superconducting symmetry from bulk s-wave to d-wave-dominant, as confirmed by transport, microscopy, Raman, and angle-dependent Andreev reflection spectroscopy.

Conclusion: Exfoliation offers a versatile, pristine platform for engineering quantum orders in 2D superconductors and provides fresh insights into the underlying pairing mechanisms of FeSe.

Abstract: Controlling intertwined electronic orders in two-dimensional superconductors offers an effective route to answering fundamental questions and engineering new quantum devices. However, tuning the balance between competing orders typically requires complex chemistry, strain, or interface engineering. Here, we show that a pristine alternative is the dimensional reduction of the unconventional superconductor FeSe. Exfoliation suppresses the bulk electronic nematic response and switches the superconducting symmetry from bulk s-wave to d-wave-dominant. Transport, electron microscopy, and Raman spectroscopy establish the substantial weakening of nematic order in thin flakes. To probe superconductivity, we perform angle-dependent Andreev reflection spectroscopy on pristine crystal edges. As the junction's orientation is rotated, the spectra evolve from zero-energy bound states to coherence peaks. The injection angle, field, and temperature dependence, along with theoretical modeling, confirm that exfoliation switches the superconducting symmetry. Our results suggest a versatile superconducting platform for engineering quantum orders and provide fresh insights into the underlying pairing mechanisms.

</details>


### [13] [Competition between on-site and off-site pairing phases and surface spectra in superconducting wallpaper fermion systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29691)
*Kaito Yoda,Ai Yamakage*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper theoretically investigates pairing competition in superconducting topological crystalline insulators with wallpaper fermions, showing that in-plane nearest-neighbor pairing stabilizes the A1u phase while off-site pairing channels yield dominant B2u and Eu phases. The B2u pairing hybridizes wallpaper-fermion spectra, while the nematic Eu state hybridizes wallpaper fermions with double Majorana Kramers pairs, producing twisted surface energy spectra that persist under off-site pairing interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how different pairing channels (on-site vs. off-site) compete and affect the surface states—specifically wallpaper fermions protected by nonsymmorphic symmetries—in superconducting topological crystalline insulators, and to determine whether twisted dispersion phenomena from hybridization persist under various pairing interactions.

Method: Theoretical analysis of pairing competition in superconducting topological crystalline insulators hosting wallpaper fermions, examining on-site and off-site pairing channels, their symmetry representations (A1u, B2u, Eu), and the resulting hybridization effects on surface energy spectra.

Result: In-plane nearest-neighbor pair hopping stabilizes the A1u phase, while off-site pairing channels yield additional dominant B2u and Eu phases. The B2u pairing state supports hybridization between wallpaper-fermion spectra, while the time-reversal-invariant nematic Eu state exhibits spectral hybridization between wallpaper fermions and double Majorana Kramers pairs, resulting in twisted surface energy spectra.

Conclusion: Twisted dispersions arising from hybridization phenomena persist in the presence of off-site pairing interactions, demonstrating that these effects survive beyond on-site pairing scenarios.

Abstract: We theoretically investigate the competition between on-site and off-site pairings in superconducting topological crystalline insulators hosting wallpaper fermions, a class of surface states protected by nonsymmorphic symmetries. In-plane nearest-neighbor pair hopping stabilizes the $\mathrm{A_{1u}}$ phase, while off-site pairing channels yield additional dominant phases with $\mathrm{B_{2u}}$ and $\mathrm{E_u}$ representations. We further show that the $\mathrm{B_{2u}}$ pairing state supports the hybridization between wallpaper-fermion spectra themselves, whereas a representative time-reversal-invariant nematic $\mathrm{E_u}$ state exhibits spectral hybridization between wallpaper fermion and double Majorana Kramers pairs, yielding twisted surface energy spectra. These results demonstrate that the twisted dispersions arising from hybridization phenomena persist in the presence of off-site pairing interactions.

</details>


### [14] [Magnetic contacts on freestanding superconducting LaAlO$_3$/SrTiO$_3$ micromembranes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29771)
*Thies Jansen,Pinelopi Konstantinopoulou,Niklas Martin,Fabio Miletto Granozio,Alessia Sambri,Rasmus Bjørk,Felix Trier,Thomas S. Jespersen*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The authors realize superconducting LAO-STO micromembrane devices with ferromagnetic nickel side contacts, demonstrating preserved superconductivity and a magnetic-history-tunable effective field in the 2DEG, establishing a platform for superconducting spintronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The superconducting 2DEG at the LAO-STO interface is promising for superconducting spintronics, but integrating ferromagnetic contacts with the superconducting 2DEG has remained challenging.

Method: Ferromagnetic nickel contacts are integrated with LAO-STO superconducting micromembranes via a side-contact geometry. Low-temperature transport measurements probe superconductivity in the presence of the ferromagnetic contacts, and an effective field model infers contact magnetization from the maximum superconducting response.

Result: Superconductivity is preserved with ferromagnetic contacts, and the superconducting state is strongly modulated by the magnetic history of the nickel contacts, generating a tunable effective magnetic field in the 2DEG. The effective field model allows magnetization of the contacts to be inferred from superconducting response.

Conclusion: Ferromagnetically contacted LAO-STO is established as a viable platform for investigating spin injection into oxide superconductors and for developing superconducting spintronic devices based on complex oxide interfaces.

Abstract: The superconducting two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG) at the LaAlO$_3$/SrTiO$_3$ (LAO-STO) interface is a promising platform for superconducting spintronics, however, integrating ferromagnetic contacts with the superconducting 2DEG remains challenging. Here, we realize superconducting LAO-STO micromembrane devices contacted by ferromagnetic nickel contacts through a side-contact geometry. Low-temperature transport measurements demonstrate that superconductivity is preserved in the presence of the ferromagnetic contacts. We show that the superconducting state is strongly influenced by the magnetic history of the nickel contacts, which generates a tunable effective magnetic field in the 2DEG. Through an effective field model, the magnetization of the contacts can be inferred from the maximum superconducting response. Our results establish ferromagnetically contacted LAO-STO as a platform for future investigations of spin injection into oxide superconductors and provide a route towards superconducting spintronic devices based on complex oxide interfaces.

</details>


### [15] [Interplay of Excitonic Charge Density Wave and Superconductivity in Transition Metal Dichalcogenides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29822)
*Ayussh Anubhav Patel,Amit Ghosal*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper investigates the interplay between excitonic charge density wave (CDW) and superconductivity (SC) in 1T-TiSe2 using a minimal two-orbital two-dimensional triangular lattice model analyzed within a mean-field framework. The model reproduces key experimental features including the gradual suppression of CDW with tuning parameters, the emergence of SC, and the transition from commensurate to incommensurate CDW ordering, with results qualitatively insensitive to band structure details.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the complex and generic interplay between excitonic CDW and superconductivity observed in 1T-TiSe2 and related transition-metal dichalcogenides under various tuning parameters (intercalation, pressure, substitution, gating), by constructing a minimal theoretical model that captures the salient experimental features.

Method: A minimal two-orbital model on a two-dimensional triangular lattice is constructed based on observed excitonic CDW and electron/hole pockets in the Fermi surface. The model is analyzed within a simple mean-field framework to examine the competition between CDW and SC order parameters.

Result: The mean-field analysis reproduces key experimental observations: CDW is gradually suppressed by tuning parameters while SC emerges; CDW transitions from commensurate to incommensurate ordering coinciding with SC onset; and the qualitative phase diagram is robust against variations in band structure details.

Conclusion: The minimal model provides crucial insights into the nature of the CDW-SC interplay in transition-metal dichalcogenides, demonstrating that the generic experimental phase diagram can be understood within a simple mean-field framework, independent of specific band structure details.

Abstract: Motivated by the unique characteristics of the phase diagram of 1T-TiSe2, we investigated the complex interplay of excitonic charge density wave (CDW) and superconductivity (SC) on a two-dimensional triangular lattice, each site accommodating two orbitals. In response to various tuning parameters, such as intercalation, pressure, substitution, and gating, these materials exhibit a generic and gradual waning of CDW, followed by the emergence of SC. Intriguingly, the nature of CDW changes from commensurate to incommensurate with the appearance of SC. Setting up a minimal model based on observed excitonic CDW and electron and hole pockets in the Fermi surface, and analyzing it within a simple mean-field framework, we can comprehend some salient experimental features. We also found that the qualitative phase diagram remains insensitive to details of the band structure. These results offer crucial insights into the nature of the interplay between CDW and SC in transition-metal dichalcogenides.

</details>


### [16] [Ballistic-to-Localized Dynamics as Signature of Quantum Phase Transition in Josephson Junction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29830)
*F. G. Capone,A. de Candia,G. Di Bello,V. Cataudella,R. Fazio,N. Nagaosa,C. A. Perroni,G. De Filippis*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Using numerical techniques, this paper investigates how quantum phase fluctuations and quasiparticle tunneling affect a small-capacitance Josephson junction across Ohmic, sub-Ohmic, and super-Ohmic dissipation regimes. Ohmic dissipation drives a BKT quantum phase transition, super-Ohmic shows none, and sub-Ohmic exhibits a continuous second-order transition. In the Ohmic regime, the phase particle shows ballistic-to-localized dynamics rather than diffusive-to-localized, and finite-frequency charge response reveals a long-lived excitation that becomes a resonance as resistances are reduced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how dissipation type (Ohmic, sub-Ohmic, super-Ohmic) shapes the quantum phase transition and dynamics of a small-capacitance Josephson junction, beyond common assumptions such as the diffusive-to-localized crossover.

Method: State-of-the-art numerical techniques including real-frequency linear-response calculations to study quantum phase fluctuations and quasiparticle tunneling across different dissipation regimes (Ohmic, sub-Ohmic, super-Ohmic) in a small-capacitance Josephson junction.

Result: Ohmic dissipation drives a BKT quantum phase transition at equilibrium; super-Ohmic shows no transition; sub-Ohmic shows a continuous second-order transition consistent with spin-boson universality. In the Ohmic regime, the response is ballistic-to-localized (not diffusive-to-localized), with finite resistance suppressing the singular zero-frequency response. Finite-frequency coupling produces a long-lived excitation in the charge response that becomes a resonance at reduced subgap and shunt resistances.

Conclusion: The dissipation spectral function fundamentally dictates the phase transition and dynamics of the Josephson junction. The previously assumed diffusive-to-localized crossover is incorrect; the actual behavior is ballistic-to-localized. The environment-induced long-lived charge excitation may have experimentally observable signatures.

Abstract: Using state-of-the-art numerical techniques, we investigate how quantum phase fluctuations and quasiparticle tunneling shape the behavior of a small-capacitance Josephson junction across Ohmic, sub-Ohmic, and super-Ohmic dissipation regimes. We show that increasing the Ohmic dissipation strength drives a Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless quantum phase transition at thermodynamic equilibrium. Deviations from Ohmic behavior profoundly alter this scenario: the super-Ohmic regime exhibits no phase transition, whereas the sub-Ohmic regime displays a continuous second-order transition, consistent with the universality classes of the spin-boson model. Within the Ohmic regime, real-frequency linear-response calculations reveal that the phase particle does not undergo the commonly assumed diffusive-to-localized crossover. Instead, finite resistance progressively suppresses the singular zero-frequency response, producing a ballistic-to-localized change in the dynamics. At finite frequencies, coupling to the environment generates a long-lived excitation in the charge response, which evolves into a resonance as the subgap and shunt resistances are reduced.

</details>


### [17] [Superheating Field of Extreme Type-II Superconductors Calculated from the Eliashberg Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29842)
*Ramesh Paudel,Alex Gurevich*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper calculates the superheating field Hs of type-II superconductors with large Ginzburg-Landau parameter using isotropic Eliashberg theory, determining its temperature dependence for varying nonmagnetic impurity scattering rates, and showing that Hs/Hc exceeds weak-coupling BCS predictions, with a maximum at intermediate impurity scattering for Nb3Sn.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To accurately compute the superheating field of type-II superconductors beyond the weak-coupling BCS approximation, accounting for strong-coupling effects and nonmagnetic impurity scattering, with implications for superconducting RF cavity applications.

Method: The isotropic Eliashberg theory is used with a thermodynamic approach to compute Hs(T) across a wide range of nonmagnetic impurity scattering parameters p, using realistic electron-phonon spectral functions from tunneling measurements for Nb and Nb3Sn.

Result: The ratio Hs(T,p)/Hc(T) from Eliashberg theory exceeds the weak-coupling BCS value. For Nb3Sn at T=0, Hs reaches a maximum at p≈0.2 where Hs/Hc is about 6% higher than in the BCS clean limit, despite nonmagnetic impurities acting as pairbreakers in the current-carrying state.

Conclusion: Strong-coupling effects enhance the superheating field beyond BCS predictions, and optimal impurity scattering (p≈0.2) can further boost Hs for certain materials like Nb3Sn, which is relevant for optimizing superconducting radio-frequency cavities.

Abstract: We calculate the superheating field $H_s$ of a type-II superconductor with a large Ginzburg-Landau parameter from the isotropic Eliashberg theory. It is shown that the temperature dependencies of $H_s(T)$ can be obtained from a thermodynamic approach for different scattering parameters $p=\hbar/2πτ_Nk_BT_c$ in a wide range of the nonmagnetic impurity scattering times $τ_N$ ranging from the clean $(p\ll 1)$ to the dirty $(p\gg 1)$ limits. Specific calculations were done for Nb and Nb$_3$Sn using their electron-phonon spectral functions extracted from tunneling measurements. We show that the ratio $H_s(T,p)/H_c(T)$ calculated from the Eliashberg theory exceeds that of the weak coupling BCS model, where $H_c$ is the thermodynamic magnetic field. We also show that, even though nonmagnetic impurities are pairbreakers in the current-carrying state, $H_s(0,p)$ for Nb$_3$Sn at $T=0$ has a maximum at $p\approx 0.2$ at which the ratio $H_s/H_c$ is about $6\%$ higher than that in the BCS clean limit.

</details>


### [18] [Chemical Perspectives on Cuprate Superconductivity: Hole-Protected Spin Dimers as d Wave Cooper Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30001)
*Myung-Hwan Whangbo,Reinhard K. Kremer*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper investigates the real-space structure of Cooper pairs in cuprate superconductors, proposing that each d-wave Cooper pair is an antiferromagnetically coupled spin dimer protected within an antiferromagnetic hole dimer (AHD), with chains of AHDs enabling collective motion of these spin dimers. The authors validate their proposal by showing it naturally explains several puzzling experimental observations in cuprates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The extremely small coherence lengths in cuprate superconductors suggest that the superconducting pairs have a well-defined real-space structure, motivating a search for this structure by analyzing the consequences of hole-doping in CuO4 units within CuO2 layers.

Method: The authors analyze aggregates of hole-doped CuO4 units that phase-separate from hole-doped CuO2 layers using segregation-pressure indices defined in the work, and examine the implications of the proposed AHD structure for explaining existing experimental observations.

Result: The analysis suggests that d-wave Cooper pairs are antiferromagnetically coupled spin dimers protected in antiferromagnetic hole dimers (AHDs), with antiferromagnetic AHD chains providing pathways for collective unperturbed spin-dimer motion. This model successfully explains several previously puzzling observations on cuprate superconductors.

Conclusion: The proposed AHD-based real-space structure of Cooper pairs in cuprates provides a coherent framework that naturally resolves puzzling experimental observations, supporting the validity of this microscopic picture.

Abstract: The observation that the coherence lengths in cuprate superconductors are extremely small motivated us to search for its real-space structure by examining the consequence of hole-doping in CuO4 units in cuprates. We analyzed aggregates of hole-doped CuO4 units that phase-separate from hole-doped CuO2 layers in terms of their segregation-pressure indices defined in this work. Our search suggests that each d-wave Cooper pair is an antiferromagnetically coupled spin dimer protected in an antiferromagnetic hole dimer (AHD), and that the antiferromagnetic chains of AHDs provide channels through which such spin dimers can move collectively without perturbation. Based on these suggestions and their implications, we examined the causes for several seemingly puzzling observations on cuprate superconductors to find that they are naturally explained by the suggestions.

</details>


### [19] [Twist-Tunable Paramagnetic Superconductivity in $d$-wave Altermagnet/Superconductor Heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30010)
*Narges Kia,Saeed H. Abedinpour,Zahra Faraei*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper theoretically investigates twisted heterostructures of d-wave superconductors proximitized by d-wave altermagnets, revealing that twist-angle control generates tunable odd-frequency spin-triplet pairing and enables switching between diamagnetic Meissner and paramagnetic Meissner electromagnetic responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The interplay between twist-angle engineering and unconventional magnetism offers a new route to control quantum phenomena. The authors seek to understand how momentum-space mismatch between superconducting gap nodes and altermagnetic spin-splitting nodes can generate and control odd-frequency pairing and macroscopic superconducting responses.

Method: The authors perform a theoretical investigation of a heterostructure consisting of a d-wave superconductor proximitized by a two-dimensional d-wave altermagnet, analyzing the twist-angle-dependent interplay between the nodal structures of the superconductor and altermagnet, and computing the resulting electromagnetic response.

Result: The momentum-space node mismatch generates robust, twist-tunable odd-frequency spin-triplet pairing. The electromagnetic response can be tuned from conventional diamagnetic Meissner to anomalous paramagnetic Meissner response purely via twist angle. For d_{x^2-y^2} altermagnets, paramagnetic response peaks at φ=0 and vanishes at φ=π/4; d_{xy} altermagnets show complementary behavior.

Conclusion: Twisted altermagnetic heterostructures provide a versatile platform for engineering odd-frequency pairing and macroscopic superconducting phases, with twist angle serving as a control knob for the macroscopic electromagnetic response.

Abstract: The interplay between twist-angle engineering and unconventional magnetism provides a powerful new route to control quantum phenomena. We theoretically investigate a heterostructure comprising a $d$-wave superconductor proximitized by a two-dimensional $d$-wave altermagnet. We reveal that the momentum-space mismatch between the superconducting gap nodes and the altermagnetic spin-splitting nodes generates a robust, twist-tunable odd-frequency spin-triplet pairing. Consequently, the macroscopic electromagnetic response of the system can be tuned from a conventional diamagnetic Meissner state to an anomalous paramagnetic Meissner effect driven entirely by the interfacial twist angle. For a $d_{x^2-y^2}$ altermagnet, the paramagnetic response is maximized at perfect alignment ($φ=0$) and completely suppressed at a maximal twist of $φ=π/4$, while a $d_{xy}$ altermagnet exhibits the exact complementary behavior. Our results establish twisted altermagnetic heterostructures as a versatile platform for engineering odd-frequency pairing and macroscopic superconducting phases.

</details>


### [20] [Superconductivity of Tellurium Polyhydride with Tc above 90K](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30139)
*Jinfu Zhu,Guiqi Liu,Yuanhao Su,Hongyu Liu,Sijia Zhang,Panpan Kong,Qingqing Liu,Jianfa Zhao,Shaomin Feng,Jun Zhang,Haoyu Zheng,Jing Song,Luhong Wang,Fuyang Liu,Haozhe Liu,M. Bykov,Xiancheng Wang,Changqing Jin*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper reports the experimental discovery of superconductivity in tellurium polyhydride, specifically TeH4, synthesized under high pressure/temperature conditions. The compound exhibits a critical temperature of ~91 K at 263 GPa, featured by face-shared TeH12 cages and quasi-molecular H2 units, with a Ginzburg-Landau coherence length of ~47 Å.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The discovery aims to extend the family of chalcogen polyhydride high-Tc superconductors beyond the landmark SH3, exploring new hydrogen-rich compounds that may exhibit high-temperature superconductivity under extreme pressures.

Method: The compound was synthesized using a diamond anvil cell combined with a laser heating system at high pressure and high temperature. In situ transport measurements were performed as a function of temperature and applied magnetic field, while the crystal structure was characterized via synchrotron x-ray diffraction.

Result: A superconducting transition with Tc ≈ 91 K was observed at 263 GPa. The superconducting phase was identified as TeH4 with face-shared TeH12 cages forming quasi-molecular H2 units. GL coherence length was determined to be approximately 47 Å, establishing tellurium polyhydride as another chalcogen polyhydride superconductor.

Conclusion: Tellurium polyhydride (TeH4) is a new addition to the family of high-Tc polyhydride superconductors, following the landmark discovery of SH3, reinforcing the importance of hydrogen-rich compounds in high-pressure superconductivity research.

Abstract: We report experimental diacovery of superconductivity (SC) in tellurium (Te) polyhydride. The compound was synthesized at high pressure and high temperature conditions using a diamond anvil cell combined with a laser heating system. Subsequent in situ transport measurements at high pressures, performed as a function of temperature and applied magnetic field, revealed a superconducting transition with a critical temperature Tc about 91 K at 263 GPa. The superconducting phase is assigned to TeH4 with characterized face shared TeH12 cage forming quasi molecular H2 units based on synchrotron x-ray diffraction experiments. Analysis of the SC behavior at magnetic fields yielded a Ginzburg Landau (GL) coherence length of approximately 47 angstroms. Tellurium polyhydride thus becomes another chalcogen polyhydride superconductor in addition to the landmark discovery of the first polyhydride high Tc SC SH3.

</details>


### [21] [Machine learning reveals common features of unconventional superconductors with high transition temperatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30588)
*Haosheng Xu,Dongheng Qian,Yijun Yu,Jing Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper uses interpretable machine learning to uncover a common materials-space signature for high-Tc unconventional superconductors, enabling a data-driven discovery strategy that successfully recovers nickelate superconductors and identifies new candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unconventional superconductors with high critical temperatures lack a broadly applicable predictive theory for discovery, unlike conventional phonon-mediated superconductors. The authors aim to address this gap by developing a data-driven strategy to guide materials discovery.

Method: The approach integrates compositional statistics, structural information, and latent representations from property-prediction models into a unified feature representation. Unsupervised analysis is applied to an experimentally established superconducting dataset with structure-aware filtering, without using transition-temperature information. A supervised Tc model is also built to identify dominant descriptors independently.

Result: Cuprate and iron-based superconductors occupy a common region of materials space characterized by large electronegativity deviation and intermediate mean valence-electron number. Both unsupervised and supervised analyses consistently identify these descriptors. The model successfully recovers recently discovered nickelate superconductors and identifies chemically distinct candidates for future investigation.

Conclusion: A discoverable, empirical materials-space prior combined with a Tc model provides a predictive framework for finding high-Tc unconventional superconductors, validated by recovering known nickelates and proposing new candidate materials.

Abstract: Superconductors with high critical temperatures that emerges beyond the phonon-mediated regime are usually considered unconventional in nature, yet unlike conventional superconductors, no broadly applicable predictive theory currently guides their discovery. Here, we use interpretable machine learning to uncover a common materials-space signature of high-$T_{\mathrm{c}}$ unconventional superconductors and develop a data-driven strategy for materials discovery. We construct a unified feature representation for each material by integrating compositional statistics, structural information, and latent representations from trained property-prediction models, followed by structure-aware filtering of an experimentally established superconducting dataset. Without using transition-temperature information, unsupervised analysis shows that cuprate and iron-based superconductors occupy a common region of materials space, characterized primarily by large electronegativity deviation and intermediate mean valence-electron number. A supervised $T_{\text{c}}$ model independently identifies the same descriptors as dominant features, providing complementary evidence for their relevance. Using this empirical materials-space prior together with the $T_{\text{c}}$ model, we prioritize candidate materials, recover recently discovered nickelate superconductors, and identify chemically distinct candidates for future investigation.

</details>


### [22] [Quadratic pair-breaking absorption edge from Anderson localization in a superconducting wire](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30598)
*Bahruz Suleymanli,B. Tanatar*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Anderson localization fundamentally alters dissipative superconducting electrodynamics, replacing the linear Mattis-Bardeen pair-breaking onset with a quadratic Mott edge while preserving the quasiparticle spectrum. A Nambu-space Berezinskii diagram technique is used to derive this for weakly disordered single-channel wires, showing the localization length equals its normal-state value while localization time diverges at the gap edge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how Anderson localization affects dissipative superconducting electrodynamics in strongly confined geometries, where standard perturbative approaches fail to capture the interplay between disorder-induced localization and superconductivity.

Method: A Nambu-space extension of the Berezinskii diagram technique that resums elastic impurity scattering to all orders. The conservation of the Bogoliubov branch collapses the Nambu diagram hierarchy onto an exactly solvable localization problem.

Result: The linear Mattis-Bardeen pair-breaking onset is replaced by a parametrically weaker quadratic Mott edge. The localization length equals its normal-state value, while localization time diverges at the gap edge. The new absorption edge arises from the product of the superconducting pair-creation coherence factor and Mott-suppressed current matrix elements, generating a characteristic double logarithm. Localization also suppresses the Hebel-Slichter coherence peak.

Conclusion: The theory provides an all-orders benchmark for microwave and spin-relaxation experiments on localized superconducting nanowires.

Abstract: We show that Anderson localization fundamentally reshapes dissipative superconducting electrodynamics, replacing the linear Mattis--Bardeen pair-breaking onset with a parametrically weaker quadratic Mott edge while leaving the quasiparticle spectrum unchanged. We derive this behavior for a weakly disordered single-channel wire with a spatially uniform $s$-wave pair potential using a Nambu-space extension of the Berezinskii diagram technique that resums elastic impurity scattering to all orders. Conservation of the Bogoliubov branch collapses the Nambu diagram hierarchy onto an exactly solvable localization problem. The localization length remains equal to its normal-state value, whereas the localization time diverges at the gap edge. The new absorption edge results from the product of the superconducting pair-creation coherence factor and the Mott-suppressed current matrix elements between localized orbitals, which generates the characteristic double logarithm. Localization also reduces the Hebel--Slichter coherence peak. The resulting theory provides an all-orders benchmark for microwave and spin-relaxation experiments on localized superconducting nanowires.

</details>


### [23] [Microwave-Induced Optomagnetism in High-Temperature Superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31127)
*Anoop Dhillon,Amir Borji,Hamed Majedi*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper reports the first experimental observation of a steady-state, microwave-driven inverse Faraday effect in a high-temperature superconductor (YBCO). Circularly polarized microwaves generate a helicity-dependent optomagnetic response below Tc, with an effective conversion efficiency of 1.75 T/(W cm⁻²) — orders of magnitude higher than optical benchmarks. The signal collapses at high powers when the field exceeds Bc1, entering a vortex phase-slip regime, and re-emerges at vortex-washboard harmonics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore a new mechanism for contactless magnetic control in superconducting systems via microwave-driven optomagnetism, leveraging the inverse Faraday effect at microwave frequencies in high-temperature superconductors, which could enable novel quantum device applications and non-inductive magnetic manipulation.

Method: Epitaxial YBa₂Cu₃O₇₋δ films were irradiated with circularly polarized microwave radiation, and the helicity-dependent response was detected via homodyne Hall transport measurements. The effect was characterized as a function of temperature and microwave power, comparing against linearly polarized excitation and probing the regime where the self-generated field exceeds the lower critical field Bc1.

Result: The inverse Faraday effect was observed exclusively below Tc and vanished in the normal state. The optomagnetic conversion efficiency reached 1.75 T/(W cm⁻²), exceeding optical benchmarks by orders of magnitude. At high powers, the signal collapsed when self-generated fields surpassed Bc1 (vortex phase-slip onset), then re-emerged at mode-locked vortex-washboard harmonics.

Conclusion: Steady-state microwave optomagnetism is established as a viable route for contactless, non-inductive magnetic control and nonequilibrium vortex spectroscopy in superconducting quantum systems, opening new possibilities for manipulating superconducting states without physical contact.

Abstract: We report the first experimental observation of a steady-state, microwave-driven inverse Faraday effect in a high-temperature superconductor. Circularly polarized microwave radiation generates a helicity-dependent response in an epitaxial $\mathrm{YBa_2Cu_3O_{7-δ}}$ film, detected using homodyne Hall transport. The optomagnetic response emerges exclusively below $T_c$, vanishes in the normal state, and exhibits no power-dependent counterpart under linearly polarized excitation. The effective optomagnetic conversion reaches $1.75\,\mathrm{T}/(\mathrm{W\,cm^{-2}})$, surpassing optical benchmarks by several orders of magnitude. At higher microwave powers, the signal collapses when the self-generated field exceeds $B_{c1}$, marking the onset of a vortex phase-slip regime, and subsequently re-emerges at mode-locked vortex-washboard harmonics. These results establish steady-state microwave optomagnetism as a route to contactless, non-inductive magnetic control and nonequilibrium vortex spectroscopy in superconducting quantum systems.

</details>
