### [24] [Light-induced nonconservative static forces in many-body systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29122)
*Jinze Li,Xuemei Yang,Zhiyuan Sun*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper shows that light can generate nonconservative (dissipative) static forces on electronic order parameters in quantum many-body systems, demonstrated in excitonic insulators (causing phase acceleration and photon emission shifts) and incommensurate charge density waves (driving sliding motion and DC current via Thouless pumping).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Periodic drives in quantum many-body systems can generate effective static forces on slow collective degrees of freedom. The authors aim to understand the nature of forces generated by light on electronic order parameters in solid-state systems, specifically exploring whether these forces can have nonconservative components arising from dissipation and optical absorption.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze the static forces generated by light on electronic order parameters. They identify nonconservative force components originating from dissipation (optical absorption) and demonstrate the effect through two specific examples: (1) excitonic insulators where interband excitations generate a nonconservative force on the excitonic order parameter phase, and (2) incommensurate charge density wave materials where propagating light generates a nonconservative force driving the density wave into sliding motion.

Result: In excitonic insulators, the nonconservative force on the phase of the excitonic order parameter causes acceleration of the phase, which manifests as a shift in the photon emission peak from the exciton condensate. In incommensurate charge density wave systems, the light-induced nonconservative force drives the charge density wave into sliding motion, producing a DC electric current via topological Thouless pumping.

Conclusion: Light can generate nonconservative forces on electronic order parameters in solids due to dissipation/optical absorption, leading to observable phenomena such as photon emission peak shifts in excitonic condensates and DC currents from sliding charge density waves.

Abstract: In a quantum many-body system, a periodic drive can often generate effective static forces on the slow collective degrees of freedom. We study the static forces generated by light on electronic order parameters in solid-state systems. We show that the forces can have nonconservative components that originate from dissipation, i.e., optical absorption. This effect is demonstrated in two nontrivial examples. In excitonic insulators, via interband excitations, the light field generates a nonconservative force on the phase of the excitonic order parameter. This force leads to an acceleration of the phase, which manifests as a shift of the photon emission peak from an exciton condensate. In materials with an incommensurate charge density wave, a propagating light field generates a nonconservative force on the phase of its order parameter. It drives the charge density wave into sliding motion, leading to a DC electric current via topological Thouless pumping.

</details>


### [25] [Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Process Driven by Multiple Dichotomous Noises](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29226)
*Silvio Kalaj,Dongho Lee,Enzo Marinari,Jae-Hyung Jeon,Pascal Viot,Gleb Oshanin*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies a generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process driven by a superposition of K independent dichotomous noises with arbitrary fixed amplitudes and switching rates. Exact expressions are derived for the stationary position distribution and all cumulants, revealing a rich structure with compact support, algebraic branch-point singularities, edge divergences, and multiple extrema. A mapping to a heterogeneous random-flight process provides a probabilistic interpretation, and disorder-averaged stationary distributions are obtained for exponentially-distributed quenched amplitudes, showing exponential tails with algebraic prefactors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the classical Ornstein-Uhlenbeck framework beyond equilibrium Gaussian white noise to bounded nonequilibrium fluctuations with finite correlation times, and to understand the resulting stationary state structure.

Method: Exact analytic computation of stationary distributions and cumulants for a generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process driven by K independent dichotomous noises; a mapping to a heterogeneous random-flight process with bounded jumps; and disorder averaging over exponentially-distributed quenched amplitudes.

Result: Exact stationary position distributions and cumulants are obtained, revealing compact support, algebraic branch-point singularities, edge divergences, and multiple extrema. Large-K crossover to Gaussian statistics is shown. Disorder-averaged distributions exhibit exponential tails decorated by algebraic prefactors with non-trivial exponents.

Conclusion: The stationary state of a generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process driven by dichotomous noises possesses unexpectedly rich structure, is interpretable via a bounded-jump random-flight mapping, and is fundamentally altered by quenched disorder.

Abstract: We study a generalized Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process driven by a superposition of $K$ independent dichotomous noises with arbitrary fixed amplitudes and switching rates. Unlike the classical Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process driven by equilibrium Gaussian white noise, the present system is governed by bounded nonequilibrium fluctuations with finite correlation times. We obtain exact expressions for the stationary position distribution and all cumulants, and show that the stationary state possesses an unexpectedly rich structure, including compact support, algebraic branch-point singularities, edge divergences, and multiple extrema. We establish a mapping onto a heterogeneous random-flight process with bounded jumps, yielding a transparent probabilistic interpretation of the stationary measure. We further analyze several limiting regimes, including the crossover to Gaussian statistics for large numbers of noise sources. For ensembles with exponentially-distributed quenched amplitudes, we derive exact disorder-averaged stationary distributions and show that disorder fundamentally alters the stationary state, producing exponential tails decorated by algebraic prefactors with non-trivial exponents.

</details>


### [26] [Phase Diagram and Critical Behaviour of the Two-Dimensional Potts Model with Long-Range Quenched Disorder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29710)
*Rodolfo Rocha,Leticia F. Cugliandolo,Marco Picco*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies the q=3 and q=8 Potts models with spatially correlated disorder that decays as a power law with exponent a. Using wrapping probabilities of Fortuin-Kasteleyn clusters and magnetic observables, the authors refine the phase diagram previously proposed by Chippari et al., identify the physical origin of a double-peak structure in magnetic susceptibility, and establish the validity of the hyper-scaling relation across all studied regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To refine and clarify the phase diagram of Potts models with power-law correlated disorder, particularly the transition between finite-disorder and infinite-disorder fixed points as a function of the correlation exponent a, and to resolve questions about the double-peak structure in magnetic susceptibility and the validity of hyper-scaling.

Method: The study uses wrapping probabilities of Fortuin-Kasteleyn clusters to probe the phase diagram and critical properties, complemented by magnetic observables to analyze the susceptibility structure, for the q=3 and q=8 Potts models with power-law correlated disorder.

Result: The paper refines the Chippari et al. phase diagram by using wrapping probabilities, clarifies the physical origin of the double-peak structure in magnetic susceptibility, and confirms the validity of the hyper-scaling relation across all investigated regimes.

Conclusion: The study provides a more precise characterization of the disordered Potts model phase diagram and demonstrates universal scaling behavior through hyper-scaling validity across regimes.

Abstract: We study the phase diagram and critical properties of the $q = 3$ and $q = 8$ Potts models with spatially correlated disorder governed by a power-law decay with exponent $a$. Building on the phase diagram proposed by Chippari et al. [3], where a transition from finite-disorder to infinite-disorder fixed points was identified as a function of a, we refine this picture by using wrapping probabilities of Fortuin-Kasteleyn clusters. Furthermore, using magnetic observables, we clarify the physical origin of the double-peak structure in the magnetic susceptibility and establish the validity of the hyper-scaling relation across all investigated regimes.

</details>


### [27] [Representation Transitions Reveal Predictive Structure in Complex Systems: A Trajectory-Level Reconstruction in a Critical System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29777)
*Jiaqi Pan*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates through numerical re-simulation that in complex systems, the choice of representation—not merely correlation strength—determines whether predictive structure becomes accessible. A continuous low-dimensional compression succeeds (r=0.677–0.717) where stronger-correlating discrete features fail, because it preserves the structure relevant to prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional population-averaged scalar statistics fail to capture hierarchical structure in complex systems. The authors seek to understand why predictive approaches fail and what determines success, questioning whether correlation strength is sufficient or whether representational choice is the decisive factor.

Method: The authors reconstruct a systematic search via independent numerical re-simulation (not literature review), comparing multiple representational forms on the same trajectory ensemble in a critical complex system: single scalar statistics, two discrete multi-feature classifications, a trajectory-level structural representation with a diagnostic surrogate test, and a continuous low-dimensional compression. Performance is validated across eight independent scale-removal folds.

Result: A discrete representation's strongest feature correlated with the target at r=0.588 but failed to separate the ensemble's two most consequential cases, while the continuous representation succeeded robustly across eight folds (r=0.677–0.717, all p<0.001). Predictive failure was not due to insufficient correlation but to loss of structure-preserving capacity.

Conclusion: Representation choice is a scientific variable in its own right rather than a downstream analysis decision. The determining factor for predictive success in complex systems is whether the representation preserves the structure relevant to prediction, not raw correlation strength.

Abstract: Trajectories in complex systems often contain structure that is not captured by the population-averaged, scalar statistics traditionally used to describe them. Here we reconstruct, through independent numerical re-simulation rather than literature review, a systematic search across representational forms - from single scalar statistics, through two independent discrete multi-feature classification attempts, to a trajectory-level structural representation and a diagnostic surrogate test, to a continuous low-dimensional compression - applied to the same underlying trajectory ensemble in a critical complex system. Predictive failure is not explained by insufficient correlation: one discrete representation's own strongest individual feature correlates with the target more strongly ($r=0.588$) than the eventual successful representation's own headline statistic ($r=0.540$), yet fails to separate the ensemble's two most consequential cases - while the continuous representation succeeds, robustly, across eight independent scale-removal folds ($r=0.677$-$0.717$, all $p<0.001$). The determining factor was not correlation strength but whether the representation preserved the structure relevant to prediction. These results indicate that the choice of representation determines whether predictive structure in complex systems becomes accessible - a scientific variable in its own right, not a downstream analysis choice.

</details>


### [28] [Branching stochastic mechanics. I. Clustering and connected correlations within a branching-process representation of the Schrödinger equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29807)
*Eric Dumonteil,Benoît Bischoff,Alain Letourneau,Loïc Thulliez,Corentin Doutre*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper constructs a stochastic representation of quantum states via branching superprocesses (Φ_F and Φ_B) derived from the Schrödinger-Nagasawa transform, showing that finite-range correlations can coexist with an extended Born density, suggesting particle-like organization within extended quantum states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether finite-range correlations can be hidden within the statistics of an extended quantum state, and to propose a stochastic mechanism that gives rise to particle-like structures from continuous quantum fields.

Method: The Schrödinger-Nagasawa transform represents the wave function via forward/backward diffusion fields. These are promoted to branching superprocesses with Bohm/Fisher-controlled branching. The branching rate is then made responsive to the fluctuating product Φ_FΦ_B, leading to a pair equation split into collective and relative sectors, with spectral cancellation and anticorrelated source channel selection determining the correlation structure.

Result: The connected sector exhibits supercritical, critical, and subcritical clustering in confinement with critical dimension d_c=2 in free space. Stationary eigenmodes remain extended while subcritical clusters acquire a reduced de Broglie scale. The pair equation yields a correlation screening length ξ_FB = sqrt(D_eff/μ_FB), demonstrating coexistence of an extended Born density with finite-range correlations.

Conclusion: Extended Born densities and finite correlation ranges can coexist within a single stochastic kernel, providing a stochastic basis for particle-like organization within extended quantum states.

Abstract: Can finite-range correlations hide in the statistics of an extended quantum state? The Schrödinger-Nagasawa transform represents the wave function by positive forward and backward diffusion fields whose product is the Born density. We promote them to branching superprocesses, \(Φ_F\) and \(Φ_B\): diffusion samples stochastic paths, whereas Bohm/Fisher-controlled branching generates genealogies of alternative continuations. With rates evaluated on the prescribed Born density, their means reproduce Schrödinger dynamics exactly. The connected sector exhibits supercritical, critical, and subcritical clustering in confinement and has critical dimension \(d_c=2\) in free space. Stationary eigenmodes remain extended; subcritical clusters acquire the reduced de~Broglie scale. We then let the branching rate respond to the fluctuating product \(Φ_FΦ_B\). In the reciprocal basis, the fields equal a smooth reference \(R\) plus centered fluctuations \(ψ_F,ψ_B\), defining the signed kernel \(C_{\rm FB}(x,y)=\mathbb E_ω[ψ_F(x)ψ_B(y)]\). On the anticorrelated branch, \(ρ_{\rm BSM}(x)=-C_{\rm FB}(x,x)\) is the positive paired density. Stationary recovery requires its diagonal to match the Born profile, while off-diagonal decay defines the correlation range. The pair equation splits into collective and relative sectors: spectral cancellation selects the Born collective mode, while suppression of the leading density fluctuation selects the anticorrelated source channel. With relative diffusivity \(D_{\rm eff}\) and positive relaxation rate \(μ_{\rm FB}\), correlations have screening length \(ξ_{\rm FB}=\sqrt{D_{\rm eff}/μ_{\rm FB}}\). Thus an extended Born density and a finite correlation range can coexist in one stochastic kernel, suggesting particle-like organization.

</details>


### [29] [Asymmetry-controlled resonant transport in a Brownian flashing ratchet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29991)
*Sudipta Mandal,Dipanjan Chakraborty,Debasish Chaudhuri*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies directed transport in a 1D Brownian flashing ratchet with a piecewise-linear asymmetric periodic potential. It finds nonmonotonic (resonant) dependence of stationary current on switching frequency, reveals a scaling form enabling data collapse for moderate asymmetry (|δ|<0.5), shows current amplitude varies linearly with asymmetry δ while resonance frequency scales as (1-b₀δ²)⁻¹, and interprets this via coupled relaxation along the two branches of the asymmetric potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the physical mechanisms governing directed transport in flashing ratchets, specifically how the asymmetry of the piecewise-linear potential and barrier height affect the transport properties such as stationary current and resonance frequency.

Method: Numerical solutions of the Fokker-Planck equation combined with Brownian dynamics simulations for a one-dimensional Brownian flashing ratchet with a piecewise-linear asymmetric periodic potential. Analysis of scaling behavior and data collapse for moderate asymmetries.

Result: Stationary current exhibits nonmonotonic dependence on switching frequency with a resonant maximum whose position depends on potential asymmetry δ and barrier height Δ. For |δ|<0.5, a scaling form separates potential parameter dependence from frequency dependence, yielding data collapse. Current amplitude scales linearly with δ, and resonance frequency obeys ν(δ,Δ)=ν₀(Δ)/[1-b₀δ²].

Conclusion: The (1-δ²)⁻¹ scaling of resonance frequency is interpreted via coupled relaxation along the two branches of the asymmetric potential, providing a physical basis for the dependence of resonant frequency on potential asymmetry.

Abstract: We investigate directed transport in a one-dimensional Brownian flashing ratchet with a piecewise-linear asymmetric periodic potential. Numerical solutions of the Fokker--Planck equation and Brownian dynamics simulations reveal a nonmonotonic dependence of the stationary current on the switching frequency, with a resonant maximum whose position depends on the potential asymmetry $δ$ and barrier height $Δ$. For moderate asymmetry, $|δ|<0.5$, the current obeys a scaling form that separates the dependence on the potential parameters from a common frequency dependence, resulting in a data collapse upon appropriate scaling of the current and frequency. The current amplitude varies linearly with $δ$, while the resonance frequency follows $ν(δ,Δ)=ν_0(Δ)/ [1-b_0\, δ^2]$. We interpret the resulting $(1-δ^2)^{-1}$ scaling in terms of coupled relaxation along the two branches of the asymmetric potential, which provides a physical basis for the observed dependence of the resonant frequency on the potential asymmetry.

</details>


### [30] [Chemical potentials from structure factors: II. Charged multi-component mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30060)
*Xiaoyu Wang,Roya Savoj,Musahid Ahmed,Bingqing Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper extends the S0 method for computing chemical potentials from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations to charged multi-component mixtures, combining composition-space frameworks with Coulombic treatment of small-wavenumber static structure factor limits. The method separates neutral composition fluctuations from forbidden macroscopic charge fluctuations, accounts for charge-neutrality constraints, and is validated on multi-halide aqueous salt solutions and molten salt mixtures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chemical potentials of charged multi-component mixtures are central to electrolyte thermodynamics but remain difficult to compute from atomistic simulations. The existing S0 method handles neutral mixtures but needs extension to treat charged systems where long-wavelength electrostatic correlations and charge-neutrality constraints complicate the calculation.

Method: The authors extend the S0 method to charged mixtures by combining the composition-space framework from Part I (for neutral mixtures) with a Coulombic treatment of small-wavenumber limits of static structure factors. This separates thermodynamically relevant neutral composition fluctuations from forbidden macroscopic charge fluctuations and incorporates charge-neutrality constraints.

Result: The method was used to compute chemical potentials of multiple-halide aqueous salt solutions, elucidating ion-specific thermodynamic effects. It also calculated mixing free energies of molten salt mixtures, demonstrating the importance of correctly describing long-wavelength electrostatic correlations.

Conclusion: The extended S0 method provides a robust approach for computing chemical potentials in charged multi-component mixtures from equilibrium molecular dynamics, with correct treatment of electrostatic long-wavelength correlations being critical for accurate results, as demonstrated in both aqueous salt solutions and molten salt mixtures.

Abstract: The chemical potentials of charged multi-component mixtures are central to electrolyte thermo- dynamics, but remain difficult to compute from atomistic simulations. The S0 method enables computing chemical potentials of mixtures from equilibrium molecular dynamics simulations. Here we extend the S0 method to charged mixtures by combining the composition-space framework developed in Part I: Neutral Multi-component Mixtures with a Coulombic treatment of the small- wavenumber limits of static structure factors. This approach separates thermodynamically relevant neutral composition fluctuations from forbidden macroscopic charge fluctuations, and further ac- counts for charge-neutrality constraints. We use the method to compute the chemical potentials of multiple-halide aqueous salt solutions, elucidating the ion-specific thermodynamic effects. We also calculate the mixing free energies of molten salt mixtures, demonstrating the importance of correctly describing long-wavelength electrostatic correlations.

</details>


### [31] [d'Q = TdS for Infinitesimal Irreversible Processes: On the Differential Form of the Clausius Inequality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30421)
*Hiroshi Shimahara*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper corrects the Clausius inequality for infinitesimal quasi-static irreversible processes, showing the correct first-order form is d'Q = TdS, not d'Q < TdS. This proves internal entropy production is second-order or higher and confirms entropy remains a well-defined state function without using the Boltzmann relation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve a subtle but fundamental issue in thermodynamics regarding the correct differential form of the Clausius inequality for infinitesimal irreversible processes, and to provide a purely thermodynamic justification that entropy remains a well-defined state quantity even when quasi-static irreversible processes are included.

Method: A purely thermodynamic analysis of the Clausius inequality, demonstrating that for infinitesimal quasi-static irreversible processes the first-order exact relation d'Q = TdS holds, implying that any internal entropy production must be of second-order or higher in the infinitesimal displacement.

Result: The authors show that the differential form d'Q < TdS is not the correct first-order form for infinitesimal quasi-static irreversible processes; the correct form is d'Q = TdS. This establishes that internal entropy production is of second or higher order.

Conclusion: Entropy remains a well-defined state function even when quasi-static irreversible processes are included, and this can be established through purely thermodynamic arguments without invoking the Boltzmann relation.

Abstract: Conventionally, the differential form of the Clausius inequality is adopted for infinitesimal irreversible processes. We show that the correct first-order form for infinitesimal quasi-static irreversible processes is the equality d'Q=TdS rather than d'Q < TdS. This provides a purely thermodynamic proof that internal entropy production is second-order or higher and confirms that entropy remains a well-defined state quantity even when quasi-static irreversible processes are included, without resorting to the Boltzmann relation.

</details>


### [32] [Time-delayed feedback turns Arrhenius escape logarithmic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30624)
*Roy Podgaetsky,Vishwajeet Kumar Arnab Pal,Ohad Shpielberg*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that time-delayed feedback in a confining force removes the exponential Arrhenius scaling of thermal escape, replacing it with logarithmic escape times via slingshot trajectories seeded by thermal noise and amplified deterministically. Escape time fluctuations follow a Gumbel law, and optimal delay can enable escape faster than free diffusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional thermal escape follows the Arrhenius law, where mean escape time scales exponentially with barrier height, severely limiting activated processes. The authors seek a mechanism to circumvent this exponential scaling and accelerate escape using non-Markovian effects from time-delayed feedback.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze the effects of time-delayed feedback on a confining force in a thermal escape problem. They identify a destabilization threshold set by the curvature of the potential minimum, beyond which thermal noise seeds a deterministic instability that is amplified, producing slingshot escape trajectories. They characterize the escape-time statistics analytically.

Result: Beyond the delay-induced destabilization threshold, the escape time becomes logarithmic in the barrier height rather than exponential. The fluctuations of escape times follow a Gumbel extreme-value distribution, and an optimal delay value allows escape that is faster than free diffusion.

Conclusion: Time delay is proposed as a tunable and experimentally feasible control parameter for accelerating activated processes, fundamentally changing the physics of thermal escape from exponential to logarithmic scaling with the barrier.

Abstract: Thermal escape is governed by the Arrhenius law, where the mean escape time scales exponentially with the barrier height. We show that the non-Markovianity induced by time-delayed feedback in the confining force removes this exponential scaling. Beyond a threshold set by the curvature of the minimum, the delay destabilizes the well, and the thermal noise seeds an instability that is subsequently amplified deterministically to the boundary leading to \textit{slingshot} escape trajectories. The escape time becomes logarithmic in the barrier, its fluctuations follow a Gumbel law, and an optimal delay enables escape faster than free diffusion. Our results propose time delay as a tunable and experimentally feasible control parameter for accelerating activated processes.

</details>


### [33] [Effective Hardcore Exclusion Without Exclusion: Two-Species Pair Annihilation Revisited](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30630)
*Su-Chan Park,Foster Thompson*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper constructs a model of particles without hardcore exclusion that reproduces the t^{-1/3} density decay scaling in A+B→∅ reaction-diffusion systems, previously observed only in hardcore exclusion systems. The model uses asymmetric diffusion with a nonlinear local density-dependent rate, showing that the decay exponent depends on microscopic transport details (pair vs. triplet processes), not just on the isolated species' transport properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In two-species annihilation systems (A+B→∅), the total density decays as a power law with exponents that depend on dimensionality and diffusion symmetry. The t^{-1/3} decay under asymmetric diffusion has only been observed in hardcore exclusion systems. The authors aim to determine whether this scaling is exclusive to hardcore exclusion or can arise from other microscopic mechanisms.

Method: The authors construct a model of particles without hardcore exclusion where both species undergo asymmetric diffusion with a rate that depends nonlinearly on the local density. This transport, in isolation, is superdiffusive and belongs to the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class. They analyze how the density decay exponent depends on the microscopic processes driving asymmetric diffusion—specifically whether they involve pairs or triplets of particles.

Result: The t^{-1/3} scaling occurs when at least one species undergoes asymmetric diffusion induced by microscopic processes involving pairs of particles. Asymmetric processes involving groups of three particles instead lead to t^{-1/4} scaling, the same as symmetric diffusion.

Conclusion: The pair annihilation density decay exponent is not determined exclusively by the transport properties of the isolated particle species; the nature of the underlying microscopic processes (pair vs. triplet interactions) plays a crucial role in setting the anomalous decay exponent.

Abstract: Reaction-diffusion systems with two species that mutually annihilate through $A+B\to\emptyset$ reactions display a slow decay of the total density $ρ$ which follows an anomalous power law in low dimensions. For hardcore particles in one dimension, this decay follows $ρ\sim t^{-1/4}$ under symmetric diffusion and $ρ\sim t^{-1/3}$ under asymmetric diffusion without relative bias between the two species. The $t^{-1/3}$ behavior, in particular, has so far been observed exclusively in systems with hardcore exclusion. Here we construct a model of particles without hardcore exclusion that reproduces this same $t^{-1/3}$ scaling. In our model, both species undergo asymmetric diffusion with a rate that depends nonlinearly on the local density, a form of transport that, in the absence of the other species, is superdiffusive and falls into the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang universality class. The scaling behavior of $t^{-1/3}$ occurs when at least one of the two species undergoes asymmetric diffusion induced by microscopic processes involving pairs of particles, while asymmetric processes involving groups of three particles lead to $t^{-1/4}$ scaling associated with symmetric diffusion. This demonstrates that the pair annihilation density decay exponent is not determined exclusively by the transport properties of the isolated particle species.

</details>


### [34] [Kinetic temperatures and inertial effects in a nonequilibrium bead-spring model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30809)
*Jetin E Thomas,Ramandeep S. Johal*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper investigates a nonequilibrium steady-state model of two coupled beads with arbitrary masses connected to thermal baths at different temperatures. Using covariance-matrix analysis and numerical simulations, the authors show that irreversibility measures (entropy production, heat current) are invariant under mass exchange, while energy observables depend on mass arrangement. Kinetic temperatures emerge as the fundamental organizing variables unifying energy, transport, and irreversibility, and an effective temperature defining an equilibrium-like canonical distribution is derived.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the role of mass asymmetry in nonequilibrium steady-state systems and to identify the fundamental thermodynamic variables that govern energy storage, heat transport, and entropy production in coupled stochastic systems. The work aims to resolve whether mass arrangement affects irreversibility measures and to unify thermodynamic descriptions under a consistent framework.

Method: The authors employ a covariance-matrix approach combined with numerical simulations of underdamped Langevin dynamics for two coupled beads with arbitrary masses in contact with two thermal baths at different temperatures. They analyze steady-state probability distributions, heat transport, entropy production, and derive relationships in terms of kinetic temperatures. They also study singular mass limits and ergodicity properties comparing time-averaged versus ensemble-averaged observables.

Result: Key findings: (1) Entropy production and heat current are invariant under exchange of bead masses, whereas energy-storage observables depend on mass arrangement. (2) Kinetic temperatures are the natural variables: their difference controls transport and entropy production, their sum determines mean energy via a generalized equipartition relation. (3) In the infinite-mass limit, only constitutive relations expressed via kinetic temperatures remain meaningful. (4) An effective temperature defining an equilibrium-like canonical distribution is derived. (5) Time-averaged observables converge significantly faster than ensemble averages.

Conclusion: The paper establishes kinetic temperatures as the organizing variables that unify energy, transport, and irreversibility in coupled nonequilibrium systems with arbitrary masses. It reveals a fundamental distinction between energy observables (which exhibit path dependence in singular mass limits) and transport/irreversibility measures (which remain well defined). The findings provide a coherent thermodynamic framework and highlight advantages of time-averaged sampling for numerical studies.

Abstract: We investigate a nonequilibrium steady-state model consisting of two coupled beads with arbitrary masses in contact with two thermal baths at different temperatures. Using a covariance-matrix approach together with numerical simulations of the underdamped Langevin dynamics, we characterize steady-state probability distributions, heat transport, and entropy production. We show that irreversibility measures such as entropy production and heat current are invariant under an exchange of the bead masses, whereas energy-storage observables depend explicitly on the mass arrangement in a symmetrical set up. This reveals a fundamental distinction: energy observables exhibit path dependence in singular mass limits, while transport and irreversibility remain well defined. We show that kinetic temperatures provide the natural variables governing the thermodynamics of the system: their difference controls transport and entropy production, while their sum determines the mean energy via a model specific generalized equipartition relation. In the infinite-mass limit, only constitutive relations expressed in terms of kinetic temperatures remain meaningful. Thus, energy, transport, and irreversibility are unified through kinetic temperatures as the organizing variables. We also derive an effective temperature that defines an equilibrium-like canonical distribution. Finally, we analyze the notion of ergodicity and show that the time-averaged observables converge significantly faster than the ensemble averages.

</details>


### [35] [Inertia-Driven Information Flow and Symmetry Breaking in a Nonequilibrium Two-Bead System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30849)
*Jetin E. Thomas,Ramandeep S. Johal*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A nonequilibrium two-bead system coupled to two heat baths acts as an information-flow generator in both overdamped and underdamped regimes. Underdamped dynamics reveal a divergence of scaled information flow along specific paths in the thermal asymmetry–inertia parameter space. Symmetry-perturbation analysis reveals a Ginzburg–Landau-like geometric structure, with mass asymmetry shifting maxima and Hessian analysis revealing level touching and bimodality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how inertia and microscopic heterogeneity shape information-flow landscapes in nonequilibrium systems, and to explore routes toward information engines and demon-like mechanisms in nanomachines.

Method: The study employs a two-bead system coupled to two heat baths, analyzed in both overdamped and underdamped regimes. Methodological tools include symmetry-perturbation analysis of the information-flow response landscape, Hessian eigenvalue/eigenvector analysis, and exploration of the thermal asymmetry–inertia parameter space.

Result: The system generates information flow in both dynamical regimes. Underdamped dynamics exhibit a divergence of scaled information flow along specific parameter-space paths hidden in the overdamped limit. The response landscape shows a Ginzburg–Landau-like structure where the symmetric reference state is a minimum or maximum depending on perturbation direction; the overdamped flat landscape gains finite curvature under inertia. Mass asymmetry shifts maxima, and Hessian analysis reveals level touching of principal modes and bimodality along a constant-diffusion path.

Conclusion: The results establish a minimal framework connecting inertia and microscopic heterogeneity to information landscapes in nonequilibrium systems, with potential extensions to more complex heterogeneous networks and implications for information-driven nanomachines.

Abstract: We investigate information-flow generation in a nonequilibrium two-bead system coupled to two heat baths. We show that the system acts as an information-flow generator in both overdamped and underdamped regimes, with the underdamped dynamics revealing a divergence of the scaled information flow along specific paths in the thermal asymmetry--inertia parameter space that is hidden in the overdamped limit. This information generation suggests a possible route toward information engines and demon-like mechanisms in nanomachines. A symmetry-perturbation analysis of the response landscape of information flow reveals a geometric structure reminiscent of a Ginzburg--Landau framework: the symmetric reference state can correspond to a minimum or maximum depending on the perturbation direction, while the flat overdamped landscape develops a finite curvature under inertia. Mass asymmetry shifts the resulting maxima, and Hessian eigenvalue and eigenvector analysis reveals level touching of principal modes and bimodality along a constant-diffusion path. These results establish a minimal framework for understanding how inertia and microscopic heterogeneity shape information landscapes in nonequilibrium systems, with potential extensions to more complex heterogeneous networks.

</details>


### [36] [On the relaxation problem in statistical mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30871)
*Giuseppe Del Vecchio Del Vecchio*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper reformulates relaxation in statistical mechanics in terms of operational objects—local time statistics of a recorded signal Z(t)—which are essentially histograms of observations at uniformly random times, rather than underlying microstates. Relaxation is reframed as a prediction problem about fresh out-of-sample measurements, with irreversible global relaxation possible in finite bounded systems (except for special initial conditions), predictions depending on observer-chosen loss functions, and entropy reinterpreted as mutual information between observer and the unknown past.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify what actually relaxes in statistical mechanics by grounding the concept in operational observables (local time statistics of signals) rather than abstract microstate evolution, and to connect relaxation, prediction, and entropy to information available to an observer.

Method: The authors make explicit the operational objects (local time statistics / histograms from clockless random-time sampling), frame prediction as inference about a future fresh out-of-sample measurement from a believed mathematical model, analyze finite bounded systems of N>=1 degrees of freedom, incorporate observer-specific loss functions, and reinterpret entropy as mutual information between observer and unknown past.

Result: Global irreversible relaxation of predictions can occur in finite bounded systems for N>=1 degrees of freedom, though special initial conditions exist. Prediction forms depend on observer-chosen loss functions. Entropy's stationary value depends on the information available to the observer and admits a learning (mutual information) interpretation.

Conclusion: Relaxation is an observer- and information-dependent phenomenon tied to prediction of out-of-sample measurements rather than an intrinsic property of the system. Entropy, similarly, is information-relative, and its stationarity depends on available information.

Abstract: We reformulate the relaxation problem in statistical mechanics by making explicit what are the \emph{operational} objects subject to relaxation: the local time statistics of the recorded signal $Z(t)$. These local time statistics are simply the estimated histograms of observations $\{Z(t_i)\}_{i=1}^M$ performed at uniformly random times $\{t_i\}_{i=1}^M$ by a clockless observer. The subject of prediction is a belief about a future fresh out-of-sample reading of a measurement outcome whose distribution is inferred from the mathematical model believed to be true. For finite bounded systems of $N\ge 1$ degrees of freedom global irreversible relaxation of predictions can occur but special initial conditions exist. The form of the predictions depends on certain loss functions whose choice is up to the particular observer. Finally, entropy is given a learning interpretation as mutual information between the observer and the unknown past of the system under consideration and, in complete generality, its stationary value depends on the information available.

</details>


### [37] [Compensation in continuous symmetric trilayered planar ferrimagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30941)
*Olivia Mallick,Muktish Acharyya*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper studies a trilayered (A-B-A type) anisotropic XY magnetic model with intra-plane ferromagnetic and inter-plane antiferromagnetic interactions using Monte Carlo simulations. The system exhibits both high-temperature critical behavior (vanishing magnetizations) and low-temperature compensation behavior (vanishing total magnetization with non-zero sublattice magnetizations). Both compensation and critical temperatures increase with single-site anisotropy. A comprehensive phase diagram is presented, and finite-size analysis shows correlation growth near criticality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the equilibrium properties of a trilayered anisotropic XY magnetic system with competing ferromagnetic/antiferromagnetic interactions, focusing on critical and compensation behaviors which are important for potential applications such as spintronics and magnetic recording.

Method: Monte Carlo simulation using the Metropolis single-spin (randomly chosen) update algorithm applied to a trilayered (A-B-A type) anisotropic XY model with intra-plane ferromagnetic and inter-plane antiferromagnetic interactions.

Result: For a certain range of interaction parameters, the system exhibits both high-temperature critical behavior (vanishing sublattice and total magnetizations) and low-temperature compensation behavior (vanishing total magnetization despite non-vanishing sublattice magnetizations). Both the compensation temperature and critical temperature increase with increasing single-site anisotropy. A comprehensive phase diagram was constructed, and finite-size studies show growth of correlations near the critical temperature.

Conclusion: The trilayered anisotropic XY model displays rich magnetic behavior including compensation and critical phenomena controlled by anisotropy and interactions, with the phase diagram and correlation growth characteristics providing a complete picture of its equilibrium properties.

Abstract: The trilayered (A-B-A type) anisotropic (single site) XY magnetic model is considered with the intra-plane ferromagnetic and
  the inter-plane antiferromagnetic interactions. The equilibrium properties of such trilayered anisotropic XY system has been studied by
  Monte Carlo simulation with Metropolis single spin (randomly chosen) update algorithm. In a given range of interaction parameters, the system has been found to exhibit the high temperature {\it critical} (vanishing sublattice magnetisations and total magnetisation) and low temperature {\it compensation} (vanishing total magnetisation even for non-vanishing sublattice magnetisations) behaviours. The compensation temperature and the critical temperature both are found to increase with the increase of single site anisotropy. The comprehensive phase diagram is drawn. The finite size study reveals the growth of correlations near the critical temperature.

</details>


### [38] [Overcoming critical slowing down in frustrated spin systems by learned multiscale sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31114)
*Gabriele Bandini,Giulio Biroli,Patrick Charbonneau,Andrea Gambassi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper introduces the wavelet conditional renormalization group (WCRG) sampling method, which learns rather than constructs clusters to overcome critical slowing down in frustrated systems where conventional cluster algorithms (Swendsen-Wang, Wolff) fail. Tested on a frustrated 2D soft-spin model, WCRG achieves O(log2 L) sampling complexity at an Ising-like critical point, reproducing key statistical properties while exposing a tradeoff between sampling speed and the expressiveness of the underlying energy-based model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cluster algorithms are highly effective for mitigating critical slowing down but fundamentally fail when even weak frustration is present, since constructive cluster approaches rely on non-frustrated interactions. The authors aim to overcome this limitation by learning relevant collective clusters rather than constructing them directly.

Method: The WCRG method uses wavelet transforms to learn the probability distribution of collective fluctuations via energy-based models, then recursively generates configurations from coarse to fine scales by sampling conditional wavelet distributions conditioned on already-sampled coarse-scale information.

Result: WCRG reproduces the local-field distribution and structure factor across different phases of a frustrated 2D soft-spin model. At an Ising-like critical point, conditional dynamics decorrelates within O(1) sweeps per scale, giving an overall sampling complexity of O(log2 L), far more efficient than local MCMC methods. The accuracy depends on the expressiveness of the energy-based model used for wavelet conditional estimation.

Conclusion: Learned multiscale sampling can overcome critical slowing down in frustrated systems where conventional cluster algorithms fail. The key tradeoff is that the fast sampling accuracy hinges on the representational capacity of the energy-based model estimating the wavelet conditional distributions, clarifying the main limitation and design consideration for the WCRG approach.

Abstract: Cluster algorithms, such as the Swendsen--Wang and Wolff methods, are among the most successful MCMC methods for mitigating critical slowing down in statistical systems. These constructive cluster algorithms, however, fail in the presence of even extremely weak frustration. Here, we sidestep this fundamental limitation by learning rather than constructing the relevant clusters. Specifically, we use the wavelet conditional renormalization group (WCRG) sampling method to learn the probability distribution of collective fluctuations of a frustrated two-dimensional soft-spin model. Configurations are then generated recursively from coarse to fine scales by sampling conditional wavelet distributions. The WCRG method reproduces the main statistical properties of the system across different phases, including the local-field distribution and the structure factor. At an Ising-like critical point, the conditional dynamics remains decorrelated within $\mathcal{O}(1)$ sweeps at each scale, yielding an overall sampling complexity of $\mathcal{O}(\log_2 L)$, thus making WCRG much more efficient than standard local MCMC methods. These results show that learned multiscale sampling can overcome critical slowing down in frustrated systems for which conventional cluster algorithms fail. By assessing the sampling accuracy of different observables, we also clarify the main tradeoff of the WCRG method: the accuracy of the fast sampling scheme depends on the expressiveness of the energy-based model used to estimate the wavelet conditional distributions.

</details>

### [39] [Estimating the ground-state hyperfine shifts of group-1 atoms due to long-range collisions, static electric fields, and nearby surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28841)
*B. H. McGuyer,K. Choksi,N. VonHeeder,B. A. Olsen*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper extends previous work on estimating hyperfine coupling shifts in group-1 atoms to include shifts caused by nearby conductive surfaces (wall shifts), while also improving existing estimates for non-reactive collisions and static electric fields by numerically computing updated characteristic energies. The authors investigate scaling arguments for these energies and suggest adaptations for other applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperfine coupling in group-1 atoms can be perturbed by various interactions (collisions, electric fields, nearby surfaces), affecting precision measurements in devices like vapor cells and nanoscale systems. Prior work provided estimation frameworks for collision-induced and Stark shifts, but needed extension to wall shifts and refinement of the characteristic energy values used.

Method: The authors extend the existing perturbative approach to estimate hyperfine shift from a nearby conductive surface (wall shift), and numerically compute updated values for the characteristic energies used across all categories of perturbations. They also explore scaling arguments to estimate these energies and discuss how the approach can be generalized to other applications.

Result: Updated numerical values for characteristic energies improve estimates of hyperfine shifts from collisions, electric fields, and conductive surfaces. The extension to wall shifts in nanoscale devices is demonstrated, and scaling arguments provide additional means to estimate the relevant energies.

Conclusion: The extended and improved framework provides more accurate estimates of hyperfine coupling shifts across multiple perturbing environments—relevant to pressure shifts in vapor cells, Stark shifts, and wall shifts in nanoscale devices—and is adaptable to other applications.

Abstract: Many interactions perturb (or shift) the hyperfine coupling in group-1 atoms between their nuclear spin and single valence electron. Previous work provided an approach to estimate this shift for non-reactive collisions with other atoms or molecules at long range, and extended it to estimate the shift from static electric fields. We further extend this approach to estimate the shift due to a nearby conductive surface, and improve all these estimates by numerically computing updated values for the characteristic energies they use. We investigate how to estimate these energies by scaling arguments, and suggest how to adapt the approach to other applications. The results are relevant to pressure shifts in vapor cells, Stark shifts, and wall shifts in nanoscale devices.

</details>


### [40] [Controlling light shifts in chip-scale atomic beam clocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29317)
*Alexander Staron,Mingwu Lu,Ruwan Senaratne,Travis Autry,Susan Schima,John Kitching,William McGehee*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A centimeter-scale Ramsey CPT clock based on a microfabricated Cs atomic beam is demonstrated, achieving 2×10⁻¹⁰ fractional frequency stability at 1 s and sub-μs drift over nearly 17 hours, with leading CPT light systematics controlled below 10⁻¹² via a doubly-insensitive operating point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the range of clock stability achievable in low-power timing applications by developing compact atomic beam clocks.

Method: A microfabricated Cs atomic beam is used in a Ramsey CPT configuration. The interplay between light shifts and Doppler shifts is investigated, revealing competing dependencies on CPT light parameters that yield doubly-insensitive operating points where clock frequency is simultaneously insensitive to laser frequency and power. Spectroscopic signatures from the atomic beam are used to control key clock shifts in a fully-integrated-compatible manner.

Result: The clock achieves a fractional frequency stability of 2×10⁻¹⁰ at 1 s and sub-μs drift over nearly 17 hours, with leading CPT light systematics controlled below the 10⁻¹² level.

Conclusion: The demonstration shows that chip-scale atomic beam clocks can provide high stability in low-power applications, with light-shift systematics effectively managed through doubly-insensitive operating points and spectroscopically-guided shift control.

Abstract: Chip-scale atomic beam clocks are being investigated to extend the range of clock stability achievable in low-power timing applications. Here, we demonstrate a centimeter-scale, Ramsey coherent population trapping (CPT) clock based on a microfabricated Cs atomic beam device and investigate the interplay between light shifts and Doppler shifts that determines its leading clock systematics. We show that these shifts exhibit competing dependencies on CPT light parameters, leading to ``doubly-insensitive" operating points where the clock frequency is simultaneously insensitive to laser frequency and power. We further demonstrate a method for controlling key clock shifts using spectroscopic signatures from the atomic beam that is compatible with fully-integrated operation. The clock achieves a fractional frequency stability of $2 \times 10^{-10}$ at $1~\textrm{s}$ and sub-$μ$s drift over nearly $17~\textrm{hours}$, with leading CPT light systematics controlled below the $10^{-12}$ level.

</details>


### [41] [Cancellation of $D_2$ line transitions of alkali-metal atoms by magnetic-field values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29700)
*Artur Aleksanyan,Susanna Petrosyan,Emil Gazazyan*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a closed-form solution for finding magnetic-field values that cancel π, σ+, and σ− transitions of the D2 line in alkali-metal atoms, overcoming the previously perceived difficulty of 4×4 Hamiltonian blocks. By exploiting the tridiagonal structure of the Zeeman-coupled blocks and polynomial recursion properties, the authors derive a single closed-form expression for the cancellation magnetic fields, calculate 234 such values for several alkali atoms up to 20 kG, and show that—unlike the D1 line—σ± transitions do cancel for D2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work found a single closed-form expression for magnetic fields canceling π transitions of the D1 line in alkali-metal atoms. The D2 line resisted a similar treatment because its ²P3/2 manifold produces 4×4 Hamiltonian blocks, making formulas seem too complex to be useful. This work aims to show this difficulty is only apparent and to extend the closed-form treatment to the D2 line.

Method: The authors exploit two key structural properties: (1) since the Zeeman interaction couples only levels with ΔF=±1, every Hamiltonian block is tridiagonal regardless of dimension; (2) consequently, the characteristic polynomial obeys a three-term recursion, and eigenvector components are polynomials in the eigenvalue. Using these properties, eigenvalues are obtained in closed form via Ferrari's and Cardano's formulas (for quartic and cubic equations), eigenvectors are obtained without further diagonalization, and a single relation gives the magnetic-field value canceling a D2 transition as a function of the excited-state eigenvalue, nuclear spin I, ground-state hyperfine splitting, and magnetic quantum number. A necessary condition on m and polarization is derived, replacing the simpler selection rule known for D1.

Result: All magnetic-field values canceling π, σ+, and σ− transitions were calculated for ²³Na, ³⁹K, ⁴⁰K, ⁴¹K, ⁸⁵Rb, ⁸⁷Rb, and ¹³³Cs up to 20 kG, yielding 234 values with uncertainties. Contrary to the D1 line, σ± transitions of the D2 line do cancel and account for the majority of the values found. The accuracy of the calculated B values is limited only by the uncertainties of the excited-state hyperfine constants.

Conclusion: The apparent complexity of the D2 line is resolved: closed-form expressions for cancellation magnetic fields are achievable using the tridiagonal structure and recursion properties of the Hamiltonian blocks. This extends the previous D1-line result to the D2 line, providing comprehensive cancellation-field values for seven alkali-metal species and revealing that D2 σ transitions do exhibit cancellation, unlike D1.

Abstract: In a previous work the $π$ transitions of the $D_1$ line of alkali-metal atoms were shown to cancel at magnetic-field values given by a single closed-form expression. The $D_2$ line has until now resisted the same treatment, because the fixed-$m$ Hamiltonian blocks of the $^2P_{3/2}$ manifold reach the dimension $4\times4$, and the corresponding formulas were considered to be too heavy to be useful. In this work we show that this difficulty is only apparent. Since the Zeeman interaction couples only levels with $ΔF=\pm1$, every block, whatever its dimension, is tridiagonal; its characteristic polynomial therefore obeys a three-term recursion, and the components of its eigenvectors are polynomials in the eigenvalue. Using these two properties we obtain the eigenvalues in closed form by Ferrari's and Cardano's formulas, the eigenvectors without any further diagonalization, and finally a single relation which gives the magnetic-field value canceling a $D_2$ transition as an explicit function of the excited-state eigenvalue, the nuclear spin $I$, the ground-state hyperfine splitting and the magnetic quantum number. A necessary condition on $m$ and on the polarization is derived, which replaces the selection rule known for the $D_1$ line. All the magnetic-field values canceling $π$, $σ^+$ and $σ^-$ transitions of $^{23}$Na, $^{39}$K, $^{40}$K, $^{41}$K, $^{85}$Rb, $^{87}$Rb and $^{133}$Cs are calculated up to 20~kG and given with their uncertainties; there are 234 of them. Contrary to the $D_1$ line, the $σ^{\pm}$ transitions of the $D_2$ line do cancel, and they account for the majority of the values found. The accuracy of the calculated $B$ values is limited only by the uncertainties of the excited-state hyperfine constants.

</details>

### [42] [Charge-State Dependence of Electronic Excitations in keV Ions Transmitted Through Solids Probed by Ion-Photon Coincidence Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28717)
*Kevin Vomschee,Radek Holeňák,Svenja Lohmann,Eleni Ntemou,Daniel Primetzhofer*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study investigates electronic excitations in keV He projectiles transmitted through matter by measuring ion-photon coincidences. The results show that excitation of the helium projectile is largely material-independent but rises strongly with kinetic energy, in agreement with a beam-foil spectroscopy excitation model extended to higher photon energies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding electronic excitations caused by light keV ions in matter is important for materials modification (space weathering, plasma-wall interactions in fusion reactors) and analytical techniques such as low/medium energy ion scattering. A deeper insight into how these excitations depend on ion kinetic energy is needed.

Method: The authors measured photon emission at energies >10 eV from keV He projectiles transmitted through different sample systems. They separated exit charge states and performed ion-photon coincidence measurements to link emitted photons to specific ions. Charge state resolved photon yields were extracted, and a detailed analysis of the coincidence methodology and data corrections was provided.

Result: The electronic excitation of the helium projectile is largely material independent and rises strongly with projectile kinetic energy. Photon yields agree well with a common excitation model from beam-foil spectroscopy, extending the model's validity to higher photon energies and various sample materials.

Conclusion: The coincidence-based methodology provides deep insight into electronic excitations of ions in matter, has broad applicability in fundamental research and materials analysis, and validates a general excitation model for the helium projectile across different materials and energy regimes.

Abstract: Investigating the electronic excitations caused by light keV ions in matter contributes to a better understanding of materials modification such as space weathering or plasma-wall interactions in fusion reactors as well as improved materials analysis methods such as low or medium energy ion scattering. This study investigates the dependence of specific excitations in keV He projectiles, the most common projectile employed in these analytical methods, on the kinetic energy of the ion. We report on photon emission at energies >10eV emitted by projectiles excited upon transmission through different sample systems. We separate exit charge states and report on measurements of ion-photon pairs in coincidence, i.e. we can link a photon to the specific ion emitting it. From these measurements we extracted charge state resolved photon yields and obtained a deep insight into the electronic excitation occurring. Our results show that the electronic excitation of the helium projectile is largely material independent and rising strongly with the kinetic energy of the projectile. The photon yields are in good agreement with a common excitation model initially developed for beam foil spectroscopy. We hence extend the validity of this model to higher photon energies and different sample materials. We further provide a detailed analysis of the employed coincidence approach and necessary corrections to the raw data, as the methodology has a broad applicability in fundamental research and materials analysis.

</details>


### [43] [Benchtop Momentum-Resolved Phonon Spectroscopy: Unlocking Lattice Dynamics via X-ray Diffuse Scattering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28759)
*Paulo H. V. da Silva,Tarsis M. Germano,Rafaela F. S. Penacchio,Sergio L. Morelhao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that momentum-resolved phonon spectroscopy can be performed using a commercial benchtop SAXS/WAXS instrument instead of large-scale synchrotron or neutron facilities, by measuring and quantitatively analyzing Thermal Diffuse Scattering (TDS) patterns in silicon and extracting interatomic force constants with accuracy comparable to simulated data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Momentum-resolved phonon spectroscopy is critical for understanding material properties but traditionally requires access to large-scale synchrotron or neutron facilities, creating a high barrier to entry for most research laboratories.

Method: The authors used a commercially available benchtop SAXS/WAXS instrument with a low-energy (8 keV) X-ray source and custom-integrated vacuum geometry to acquire TDS patterns from silicon (111) and (100) slabs. They then extracted interatomic force constants (IFCs) from these patterns using global optimization algorithms.

Result: The benchtop instrument successfully acquired characteristic TDS patterns containing sufficient information to extract IFCs with fitting accuracy comparable to that achieved from simulated data with statistical noise, providing access to phonon information across the entire Brillouin Zone.

Conclusion: This low-barrier approach makes synchrotron-level lattice dynamics characterization accessible to virtually any research laboratory, enabling new avenues for studying intrinsic strain and anisotropic lattice dynamics in emerging material systems.

Abstract: Momentum-resolved phonon spectroscopy is essential for understanding the thermal, electronic, and structural properties of materials, yet it traditionally necessitates access to large-scale synchrotron or neutron facilities. We report a paradigm shift by demonstrating the accurate measurement and quantitative analysis of Thermal Diffuse Scattering (TDS) patterns using a commercially available benchtop Small/Wide-Angle X-ray Scattering (SAXS/WAXS) instrument. By utilizing a low-energy (8 keV) X-ray source and a custom-integrated vacuum geometry, we acquired characteristic TDS patterns in silicon (111) and (100) slabs. We demonstrate that these benchtop images contain sufficient information to extract interatomic force constants (IFCs) through global optimization algorithms, achieving a fitting accuracy comparable to those obtained from simulated data with statistical noise. Crucially, this capability provides access to phonon information across the entire Brillouin Zone, a critical component of lattice dynamics unavailable via standard laboratory techniques like first-order Raman spectroscopy. This low-barrier approach makes synchrotron-level lattice dynamics characterization accessible to virtually any research laboratory, enabling new avenues for the study of intrinsic strain and anisotropic lattice dynamics in emerging material systems.

</details>


### [44] [Engineering Excitons through Polymorphism and Dimensional Confinement in Low-Dimensional Tellurium](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28861)
*Gabriel Elyas Gama Araujo,Alexandre Cavalheiro Dias,Andreia Luisa da Rosa*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses many-body GW and Bethe-Salpeter equation calculations to investigate the quasiparticle, excitonic, and optical properties of two-dimensional tellurium polymorphs (α- and β-tellurene, hydrogen-passivated hexagonal tellurene) and one-dimensional helical nanowires, revealing that excitonic behavior is strongly governed by band-edge dispersion, crystal symmetry, and dimensional confinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While low-dimensional tellurium exhibits rich optical properties governed by the interplay of dimensionality, band-edge electronic structure, and electron-hole interactions, the microscopic origin of its excitonic behavior remains largely unexplored, motivating a detailed many-body investigation.

Method: The authors employ many-body GW calculations for quasiparticle properties and solve the Bethe-Salpeter equation to obtain excitonic and optical properties across two-dimensional tellurium polymorphs and one-dimensional helical nanowires, with momentum-resolved BSE eigenvectors and real-space exciton wave functions used to characterize excitonic localization and anisotropy.

Result: α-tellurene shows weak, spatially extended electron-hole correlations; β-tellurene, with SOC-induced quasi-flat band-edge states, exhibits a strongly bound and anisotropic near-infrared exciton; hydrogen-passivated hexagonal tellurene (a quantum spin Hall phase, Z2=1) supports an even larger direct exciton binding energy of 0.51 eV with a compact, nearly isotropic in-plane distribution; the one-dimensional helical nanowire shows high binding energy and a pronounced ultraviolet shift in its optical response.

Conclusion: Strong electron-hole correlations are fully compatible with nontrivial band topology, while exciton binding strength and spatial character depend strongly on band-edge electronic structure and crystal symmetry, with dimensional confinement driving increasingly strong excitonic effects down to the nanowire limit.

Abstract: The interplay between dimensionality, band-edge electronic structure, and electron-hole interactions governs the optical response of low-dimensional tellurium, yet the microscopic origin of its excitonic behavior remains largely unexplored. Here, we investigate the quasiparticle, excitonic, and optical properties of two-dimensional tellurium polymorphs and one-dimensional helical nanowires using many-body GW and the Bethe--Salpeter equation. Our results reveal a strong dependence of the excitonic response on band-edge dispersion, crystal symmetry, and dimensional confinement. $α$-tellurene exhibits comparatively weak and spatially extended electron-hole correlations, whereas the SOC-induced quasi-flat band-edge states of $β$-tellurene give rise to a strongly bound and anisotropic near-infrared exciton. Momentum-resolved BSE eigenvectors and real-space exciton wave functions directly reveal the contrasting localization and anisotropy of these excitonic states. Remarkably, hydrogen-passivated hexagonal tellurene, previously identified as a quantum spin Hall phase with $Z_2=1$, supports an even larger direct exciton binding energy of 0.51 eV together with a compact and nearly isotropic in-plane excitonic distribution. This demonstrates that strong electron-hole correlations are fully compatible with nontrivial band topology, while the binding strength and spatial character of the exciton remain strongly dependent on the underlying band-edge electronic structure and crystal symmetry. The one-dimensional helical nanowire represents the strong-confinement limit, exhibiting a direct high exciton binding energy and a pronounced shift of the optical response toward the ultraviolet.

</details>


### [45] [Revealing low-energy surfaces of multinary compounds by controlling surface coordination environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28903)
*Weihang Xie,Harshan Reddy Gopidi,Zhengyu Liu,Romain Claes,Alexander G. Squires,Keith T. Butler,David O. Scanlon,Pieremanuele Canepa*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: SALAMI is a Python package that generates symmetric, charge-neutral, dipole-free, and low-energy slab models for multinary compounds via combinatorial searches that preserve optimal coordination environments. Applied to Li3PS4 and ZnSb2O6, density functional theory shows that preserving coordination polyhedra (PS4 units for Li3PS4, partial Sb undercoordination for ZnSb2O6) significantly lowers surface energies and shrinks predicted Wulff shapes by ~20%, demonstrating the necessity of controlling local coordination for accurate surface energetics of multinary compounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional cleavage planes for multinary compound surfaces often cut through strongly bonded polyhedra, producing unphysical surface energies. A better approach is needed to generate physically meaningful slab models that preserve coordination environments.

Method: The authors introduce SALAMI, a Python package that performs combinatorial searches to selectively remove surface atoms and generate corrugated terminations that preserve optimal coordination environments, yielding symmetric, charge-neutral, dipole-free, low-energy slabs. They applied it to all symmetrically inequivalent orientations with Miller indices up to 2 for Li3PS4 (solid-state electrolyte) and ZnSb2O6 (transparent conducting oxide), and validated with DFT calculations.

Result: DFT reveals that Li3PS4 must preserve all PS4 units for minimum surface energy. For ZnSb2O6, low-energy surfaces require partial undercoordination of surface Sb atoms (SbO5 or SbO4 from bulk SbO6) depending on orientation. Applying coordination constraints lowers surface energies significantly compared to unconstrained models, shrinking the predicted Wulff shape volume by approximately 20%.

Conclusion: Meticulous control of local coordination environments is necessary for accurately predicting the surface energetics of multinary compounds, and SALAMI provides an effective automated workflow for generating such physically meaningful surface models.

Abstract: When modeling surfaces of multinary compounds, conventional cleavage planes often cut through strongly bonded polyhedra, resulting in unphysical surface energies. Here, we introduce SALAMI (Symmetric Atomic Layers for Arbitrary Multinary Interfaces), a Python package that generates symmetric, charge-neutral, dipole-free, and low-energy slab models for multinary compounds. SALAMI performs combinatorial searches to selectively remove surface atoms and generate corrugated terminations that preserve optimal coordination environments. We applied this workflow to all symmetrically inequivalent crystallographic orientations with Miller indices up to 2 for two prototypical structures: the solid-state electrolyte Li3PS4 and the transparent conducting oxide ZnSb2O6. Density functional theory calculations reveal that Li3PS4 must preserve all PS4 units to achieve the minimum surface energy. For ZnSb2O6, low-energy surfaces are achieved by partial undercoordination of surface Sb atoms to SbO5 or SbO4 from the bulk SbO6, depending on the surface orientations. Compared to surface models generated with unconstrained coordination, applying constraints to achieve optimal local coordination environments significantly lowers surface energies, shrinking the volume of the predicted Wulff shape by approximately 20%. Our results demonstrate that meticulous control of local coordination environments is necessary for accurately predicting the surface energetics of multinary compounds.

</details>


### [46] [Giant staggered Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya vectors emerged in synthetic antiferromagnets with spatially alternating stress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28953)
*Yuuga Iwata,Kensuke Hayashi,Reiju Furumaki,Satoshi Iihama,Masahiro Sato,Takahiro Moriyama*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper reports on a strong interlayer Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction in Co/Ru magnetic superlattices, achieved by breaking structural symmetry through alternating mechanical strains. Theoretical analysis reveals a staggered ordering of DM vectors, demonstrating that spatially varying strains can effectively control both DM interaction strength and DM vector order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Controlling the DM interaction and the ordering of DM vectors is crucial for exploring chiral magnetic physics, exotic magnetic/electronic properties (e.g., altermagnetism), and advancing new paradigms in magnetism and spintronics.

Method: The authors used a peculiar stress mechanism to apply alternating mechanical strains in Co/Ru magnetic superlattices, breaking the structural symmetry. Combined with theoretical analyses, they studied the resulting DM interaction and its vector ordering.

Result: A strong interlayer DM interaction emerges in the strained Co/Ru magnetic superlattices, and theoretical analysis reveals a staggered order of DM vectors.

Conclusion: Spatially varying strains are highly effective at breaking system symmetry, enabling control over both the DM interaction strength and the DM vector order, which has implications for magnetism and spintronics.

Abstract: Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction is a source of chiral magnetic physics, and it manifests microstructural symmetry which could link to exotic magnetic and electronic properties such as altermagnetism. For a new paradigm in magnetism and spintronics, it is crucial to have control on the DM interaction as well as ordering of the DM vectors which characterize the DM interaction. Here, we report on a strong interlayer DM interaction emerged in Co/Ru magnetic superlattices whose structural symmetry is unambiguously broken by alternating mechanical strains developed using the peculiar technique of our stress mechanism. Moreover, theoretical analyses reveal that our magnetic superlattices host a staggered order of the DM vectors. Our results indicate that the spatially varying strains with our breakthrough technique are quite effective in breaking the symmetry of the system and prove that it can control the DM interaction strength as well as the DM vector order.

</details>


### [47] [Attosecond Reconstruction of Strain Tensors via Electronic Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30133)
*Jing Li,Jiayu Yan,Guoyong Yuan,Chao Chen,Shang Wang,Fulong Dong*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors demonstrate an attosecond transient absorption spectroscopy (ATAS) scheme to reconstruct strain tensors in two-dimensional materials using strained graphene as a prototype. Fishbone structures in ATAS serve as spectral fingerprints of strain, with strain-induced shifts and splittings of van Hove singularities encoding strain magnitude and orientation. Combining density-matrix simulations with analytical modeling establishes a direct mapping between transient absorption spectra and strain tensors, enabling accurate lattice deformation retrieval from ultrafast electronic responses. This introduces an attosecond spectroscopic paradigm for ultrafast strain metrology in quantum materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional structural probes for sensing lattice deformation in quantum materials are limited, and there is a need for ultrafast, electronic-based approaches to strain metrology in two-dimensional materials that can capture strain tensors with high temporal resolution.

Method: Attosecond transient absorption spectroscopy (ATAS) on strained graphene, combined with density-matrix simulations and analytical modeling to establish a quantitative mapping between transient absorption spectra and strain tensor parameters.

Result: Fishbone structures in ATAS act as distinctive spectral fingerprints of strain; strain-induced shifts and splittings of van Hove singularities encode strain magnitude and orientation respectively, enabling accurate reconstruction of the strain tensor.

Conclusion: The work establishes an attosecond spectroscopic paradigm for ultrafast strain metrology, where electronic fingerprints replace conventional structural probes for sensing lattice deformation in quantum materials.

Abstract: We demonstrate an attosecond transient absorption spectroscopy (ATAS) scheme for reconstructing strain tensors in two-dimensional materials. Using strained graphene as a prototype system, we show that the fishbone structures in ATAS serve as distinctive spectral fingerprints of strain, where strain-induced shifts and splittings of van Hove singularities encode the magnitude and orientation of the strain tensor, respectively. By combining density-matrix simulations with analytical modeling, we establish a direct mapping between transient absorption spectra and strain tensors, enabling accurate retrieval of lattice deformation from ultrafast electronic responses. Our work introduces an attosecond spectroscopic paradigm for ultrafast strain metrology, where electronic fingerprints replace conventional structural probes for sensing lattice deformation in quantum materials.

</details>


### [48] [Energy landscape and dissipation of sliding magnetic rotor arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28915)
*Johannes Krotz,Anton Lüders*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper analytically investigates magnetic friction in a rigid magnetic rotor array sliding over a commensurate magnetic substrate.
