Using a simplified model that reduces collective rotor dynamics to coupled nonlinear differential equations, the authors map the parameter space into chambers with invariant energy-landscape structures, identify distinct dynamical regimes including a globally alternating moment-alignment regime with peak dissipation, derive leading-order friction expressions for small and large layer separations, and show that friction in the alternating regime can be computed from energy jumps between critical points. Since the model is scale-free, results apply across microscopic to macroscopic length scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how magnetically coupled rotational degrees of freedom contribute to tribological loss during sliding, by providing an analytical framework for a previously introduced simplified model of magnetic rotor arrays sliding over magnetic substrates.

Method: Analytical study of a rigid magnetic rotor array sliding over a commensurate magnetic substrate, where rotors rotate about an axis perpendicular to the sliding direction with microscopic shaft damping. The collective dynamics are reduced to coupled nonlinear differential equations and analyzed via the energy landscape in the quasi-static regime. The parameter plane (layer separation vs. time) is partitioned into chambers with invariant energy-landscape structure, and asymptotic expansions are used for small and large gaps.

Result: The parameter plane can be divided into chambers where the energy-landscape topology is invariant, allowing identification of layer-separation intervals for distinct dynamical states. A regime of globally alternating moment alignment with peak dissipation is recovered. Leading nontrivial orders of collective magnetic friction are derived for both small and large gaps. For the alternating regime, friction is computed from energy jumps between unstable and stable critical points at chamber boundaries.

Conclusion: The analytical results provide a foundation for understanding the observed dynamics and dissipation in sliding rotor arrays. Because the simplified model is scale-free, the conclusions generalize across a broad range of microscopic and macroscopic length scales.

Abstract: Magnetically coupled damped rotational degrees of freedom can give rise to tribological loss when excited by a relative sliding motion, linking friction to the dynamics of magnetic moments. We analytically investigate such magnetic friction by studying a recently introduced simplified model for a rigid magnetic rotor array sliding over a commensurate magnetic substrate. Here, the array rotors can rotate about an axis perpendicular to the sliding direction and are damped by microscopic shaft friction, while the substrate magnets have a fixed in-plane orientation. The simplified model reduces the collective rotor dynamics of this setup to a set of coupled nonlinear differential equations, which can be studied by the corresponding energy landscape in the quasi-static regime. We find that the parameter plane spanned by layer separation and time can be divided into distinct chambers, in which the structure of the energy landscape remains invariant. Using these chambers, we identify the layer separation intervals corresponding to different dynamical states and recover the emergence of a regime where the moment alignment alternates globally while exhibiting peak dissipation. Additionally, we derive the leading nontrivial orders of the collective magnetic friction in the regimes of small and large gaps between the array and the substrate. For the alternating regime, we find that friction can be computed by tracking energy jumps from unstable to stable critical points at the chamber boundaries. These results provide an analytical foundation for the observed dynamics and dissipation in sliding rotor arrays. Because the simplified model is scale-free, our conclusions transfer across a broad range of microscopic and macroscopic length scales.

</details>


### [49] [Directional commensurability stabilizes structural superlubricity in patterned mesoscale interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29141)
*Viet Hung Ho,Ge Li,Melisa M. Gianetti,Bjørn Haugen,Graham L. W. Cross,Astrid S. de Wijn*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates patterned surfaces as a route to overcome roughness and wear limitations in structural superlubricity. It shows that triangular-triangular patterns fail at elevated loads due to few load-bearing contacts causing local pressure to exceed coating strength, whereas a square-triangular patterned interface increases load-bearing contacts into continuous lines, maintaining superlubricity at higher loads and tolerating surface imperfections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structural superlubricity from lattice incommensurability could eliminate friction, but real-world systems suffer from roughness, wear, and contact size limitations. Patterned surfaces are explored as a possible route to overcome these robustness issues.

Method: The authors compare triangular-triangular patterned interfaces (shown to fail at elevated loads due to few load-bearing contacts) with a square-triangular patterned interface that increases the number of load-bearing contacts and organizes them into continuous contact lines. Sliding along specific directions relative to these lines is analyzed.

Result: The square-triangular pattern maintains superlubricity at significantly higher loads by reducing pressure-induced coating failure while remaining somewhat tolerant to surface imperfections, unlike the triangular-triangular pattern which fails due to excessive local contact pressure exceeding coating strength.

Conclusion: The findings establish a mechanism for stabilizing structural superlubricity against coating failure and provide a design principle for engineering low-friction interfaces with enhanced load-bearing capacity and defect tolerance.

Abstract: Structural superlubricity, arising from lattice incommensurability, offers a promising route to eliminate friction and associated energy losses in mechanical systems. In real-world systems, roughness and wear currently pose severe limitations on its robustness and especially the contact size. Here, we consider patterned surfaces as a possible route to overcome some of these limitations. We show that the simplest choice of patterning, contacts made up of two incommensurate triangular-triangular patterns, fails at elevated loads because of the small number of load-bearing contacts, causing the maximum local contact pressure to exceed the strength of the superlubric coating. We introduce a square-triangular patterned interface that increases the number of load-bearing contacts and organizes them into continuous contact lines. When sliding along specific directions relative to these lines, superlubricity is maintained at significantly higher loads by reducing pressure-induced coating failure while also remaining somewhat tolerant to surface imperfections. These findings establish a mechanism for stabilizing structural superlubricity against coating failure and a design principle for engineering low-friction interfaces with enhanced load-bearing capacity and defect tolerance.

</details>


### [50] [Can a Spin Liquid State Persist as the Ground State in the Presence of Competing Interactions and Disorder?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28998)
*Sumanta Mukherjee*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that competing interactions in a geometrically frustrated lattice can stabilize different magnetic phases, and that fluctuations combined with these interactions lead to unusual ordered phases via the order-by-disorder mechanism. The competition is further modified by structural disorder, which drives most samples toward glassy dynamics that mimic spin-liquid behavior and complicate experimental identification of genuine spin-liquid candidates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how competing interactions and fluctuations in frustrated lattices give rise to exotic magnetic phases, particularly the order-by-disorder phenomenon, and to address the challenge of distinguishing genuine spin-liquid behavior from glassy dynamics induced by structural disorder.

Method: The study examines a geometrically frustrated two-dimensional lattice, analyzing how underlying competing interactions stabilize different magnetic phases and how fluctuations promote unusual ordered states. It additionally investigates how inherent structural disorder modifies the competition and leads to glassy dynamics.

Result: Competing interactions in the frustrated lattice stabilize different magnetic phases, and with fluctuations they lead to fluctuation-driven order-by-disorder. However, local structural disorder drives most samples toward glassy dynamics that can mimic spin-liquid-like behavior, making genuine spin-liquid identification experimentally difficult.

Conclusion: The competition between interactions in frustrated lattices provides a pathway for understanding order-by-disorder phenomena, but structural disorder complicates the experimental identification of genuine spin-liquid candidates by producing glassy dynamics that mimic spin-liquid signatures.

Abstract: In this report, we have shown that, in an otherwise geometrically frustrated lattice, the underlying interactions compete to stabilize different magnetic phases. In conjunction with fluctuations, these interactions may lead to the formation of unusual ordered phases, providing a pathway for understanding the fluctuation-driven order-by-disorder phenomenon. This competition is further modified by the presence of inherent structural disorder in a frustrated two-dimensional lattice. Furthermore, due to the local nature of the additional structural disorder, the majority of the samples evolve toward glassy dynamics, which can not only mimic spin-liquid-like behavior but also make it difficult to identify genuine spin-liquid candidates experimentally.

</details>


### [51] [Elastic properties of amorphous LiTaCl$_6$ solid-state electrolyte](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29404)
*Xiaolin Liu,De-en Jiang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates the elastic properties of amorphous LiTaCl6, a superionic Li-ion conductor for all-solid-state batteries, using DFT-based methods. The MLFF-MD approach yields a Young's modulus of 2.84 ± 0.26 GPa, quantitatively matching the experimental 2.91 ± 0.32 GPa, while static methods significantly overestimate the modulus. The material behaves elastically like a soft polymer or gel, highlighting the necessity of dynamical treatment for amorphous solid electrolytes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Amorphous solid-state electrolytes are promising for safe, high-energy-density all-solid-state batteries, but their mechanical properties are poorly understood computationally. Understanding elastic behavior is critical for battery design and performance.

Method: The authors employ three DFT-based approaches: (1) unrelaxed static method, (2) relaxed static method, and (3) strain fluctuations from MD simulations in the NPT ensemble using DFT-trained MLFFs. These are applied to amorphous LiTaCl6 to extract elastic moduli.

Result: The unrelaxed static method overestimates Young's modulus by an order of magnitude; the relaxed static method still overestimates by >170%. The MLFF-MD approach yields Young's modulus of 2.84 ± 0.26 GPa, quantitatively matching experiment (2.91 ± 0.32 GPa). Additional predicted properties include bulk modulus (4.44 GPa), shear modulus (1.02 GPa), and Poisson's ratio (0.39).

Conclusion: Amorphous LiTaCl6 behaves elastically like a soft polymer or gel, and only the MLFF-MD approach with dynamical treatment yields quantitative agreement with experiment. This highlights that static computational methods are inadequate for simulating the elastic response of amorphous superionic conductors, consistent with recent findings for crystalline superionic conductors.

Abstract: Amorphous solid-state electrolytes are attractive candidates for safe, high-energy-density all-solid-state batteries, yet their mechanical properties remain poorly understood from a computational perspective. Here, we investigate the elastic behavior of the recently discovered amorphous superionic Li-ion conductor LiTaCl$_6$ using density-functional-theory (DFT)-based methods, including unrelaxed static, relaxed static, and strain fluctuations from molecular dynamics (MD) simulations in the isobaric--isothermal (NPT) ensemble with DFT-trained machine-learning force fields (MLFFs). While the unrelaxed static method predicts a Young's modulus an order of magnitude higher than the experiment, the relaxed static method---commonly applied to crystalline electrolytes---still overestimates the modulus by more than 170%. In contrast, the MD approach using MLFFs yields a Young's modulus of $2.84 \pm 0.26$ GPa, which quantitatively agrees with the experimental value of $2.91 \pm 0.32$ GPa. Using the MLFF-MD approach, we further predict bulk modulus (4.44 GPa), shear modulus (1.02 GPa), and Poisson's ratio (0.39) for amorphous LiTaCl$_6$ and conclude that elastically it behaves like a soft polymer or gel. These results demonstrate that amorphous superionic materials possess some unique elastic properties and that, among the methods examined, only the MLFF-MD approach yields quantitative agreement with experiment, highlighting the necessity of a dynamical treatment to simulate their elastic response, consistent with recent findings for crystalline superionic conductors.

</details>


### [52] [Evaluating LLM-based AI agents integrated with materials synthesis tools: the case of atomic layer deposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29309)
*Angel Yanguas-Gil*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reviews evaluation strategies for LLM-based AI models and agents in materials synthesis, covering knowledge/reasoning benchmarks, tool-use benchmarks, and closed-loop benchmarks involving experimental systems. It uses atomic layer deposition (ALD) as a case study and proposes a practical evaluation framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based AI agents are increasingly integrated into materials synthesis workflows, there is a need to systematically evaluate their performance across different contexts—especially those directly involving experimental tools. The authors aim to consolidate existing evaluation approaches and provide guidance for assessing these models in materials synthesis.

Method: The paper provides a structured overview of evaluation strategies for LLMs in materials synthesis, categorized into three tiers: knowledge and reasoning benchmarks, tool-use benchmarks, and closed-loop benchmarks involving real or virtual experimental systems. It uses atomic layer deposition (ALD) as a case study to illustrate how these approaches map to a specific synthesis technique and how they generalize to other methods.

Result: The authors identify and classify existing evaluation strategies in the literature, demonstrate how ALD serves as a representative case study linking general AI evaluation methods to materials-specific tasks, and derive a practical evaluation framework applicable across materials synthesis techniques.

Conclusion: A practical evaluation framework is provided for assessing LLM-based agents in materials synthesis, spanning knowledge, tool-use, and closed-loop interaction scenarios, with ALD serving as a template for generalization to other synthesis methods.

Abstract: This work provides an overview of the different strategies that can be used to evaluate the performance of AI models and agents based on large language models (LLMs) for materials synthesis. After providing a brief overview of the key technologies behind the current generation of AI agents based on LLMs, we summarize the different approaches to evaluating these models in the context of materials science and in particular on materials synthesis, with a specific emphasis on scenarios in which the models are directly integrated with experimental tools. We discuss evaluation strategies spanning knowledge and reasoning benchmarks, tool-use benchmarks, and closed loop benchmarks involving the interaction with experimental systems or realistic virtual tools. We use atomic layer deposition (ALD) as a case study, emphasizing how existing approaches in the literature both build from general approaches used beyond materials science and can be generalized to other materials synthesis techniques. Finally, we provide a practical evaluation framework to evaluate LLMs in the context of materials synthesis

</details>


### [53] [Cesium Clustering and Fluoroberyllate Network Disruption in FLiBe: A Total Scattering and Molecular Dynamics Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29898)
*Sean Fayfar,Rajni Chahal,Danny Wang,David J. Sprouster,Shravan Venugopal,Guiqiu Zheng,Dan Olds,Joerg Neuefeind,Stephen Lam,Boris Khaykovich*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper investigates the structural perturbations in FLiBe molten salt when doped with 5 mol% CsF (simulating fission product accumulation). Using X-ray and neutron diffraction with EPSR refinement and neural network molecular dynamics (NNMD), the authors find that Cs slightly disrupts the intermediate-range fluoroberyllate network by increasing free fluorine ions and clusters extensively in the melt, while local coordination remains largely unchanged. Notably, 5 mol% CsF suppresses crystalline Li2BeF4 formation at room temperature, with the phase only appearing above 180°C upon heating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Next-generation fission reactors using molten salts (e.g., FLiBe) will accumulate fission products like cesium during operation, potentially altering thermophysical properties and atomic structure. Understanding these structural perturbations is critical for predicting and modeling reactor salt behavior.

Method: Combined experimental and computational approach: (1) X-ray and neutron diffraction measurements on FLiBe with 5 mol% CsF; (2) empirical potential structure refinement (EPSR) modeling against scattering data; (3) neural network molecular dynamics (NNMD) simulations; (4) comparative analysis of EPSR and NNMD to distinguish data-constrained features from model-dependent ones.

Result: Cs-bearing correlations account for changes in the total structure factor and pair-distribution function while FLiBe correlations remain minimally altered. Cs slightly disrupts the intermediate-range fluoroberyllate network, increasing free fluorine fraction but leaving local coordination largely unchanged. Cs ions cluster extensively with BeF4^2- tetrahedra bridging neighboring Cs environments. The 5 mol% CsF suppresses crystalline Li2BeF4 formation at room temperature, with the phase appearing only above 180°C upon heating.

Conclusion: The experimentally constrained structural features provide a benchmark for atomistic models used to predict behavior and properties of fission-product-containing FLiBe, offering insight into how cesium accumulation perturbs the salt's structure and phase behavior.

Abstract: Several next-generation fission reactor designs employ molten salts such as FLiBe (2LiF-BeF$_2$), with some concepts using fuel dissolved directly in the salt. During operation, fission products such as cesium will accumulate in the salt mixture, potentially leading to an evolution of the thermophysical properties underpinned by the atomic structure. To understand the structural perturbations in FLiBe with 5 mol% CsF, we conducted X-ray and neutron diffraction measurements, refined empirical potential structure refinement (EPSR) models against the experimental data, and compared the resulting structure with neural network molecular dynamics (NNMD) simulations. Comparisons of the EPSR and NNMD structures distinguishes features constrained by the scattering data from those that remain model dependent. The new Cs-bearing correlations account for the changes in the total structure factor and pair-distribution function, while the FLiBe correlations remain minimally altered. We find that Cs slightly disrupts the intermediate-range fluoroberyllate network, increasing the fraction of free fluorine ions, while the local coordination remains largely unchanged. The Cs ions within FLiBe cluster extensively, with BeF$_4^{2-}$ tetrahedra bridging neighboring cesium environments. In contrast to the minor structural perturbations in the liquid, the addition of 5 mol% CsF suppressed the formation of the crystalline Li2BeF4 phase at room temperature, with the phase appearing only above 180C upon heating. These experimentally constrained structural features provide a benchmark for atomistic models used to predict the behavior and properties of fission-product-containing FLiBe.

</details>


### [54] [Symmetry-Preserving Phase Transitions in $AM_2$Al$_9$ Materials under Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28957)
*Jian-Feng Zhang,Sheng Xu,Zhong-Yi Lu,Tao Xiang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: First-principles calculations reveal that AM₂Al₉ compounds (A = Ba, Ca, Sr, Eu; M = Fe, Co, Ni) undergo pressure-induced isostructural phase transitions with volume collapse while preserving P6/mmm symmetry, driven by intralayer/interlayer bond redistribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isostructural phase transitions are rare and poorly understood because they preserve symmetry while being first-order. The authors aim to identify a material family exhibiting this phenomenon and elucidate its microscopic origin, providing a platform for studying symmetry-preserving structural transitions and critical phenomena under pressure.

Method: First-principles calculations on AM₂Al₉ compounds, including analysis of pressure-induced phase behavior, structural symmetry verification, and bonding analysis using integrated crystal orbital Hamilton population (ICOHP) to examine intralayer versus interlayer bond character changes.

Result: The compounds undergo pressure-induced isostructural first-order phase transitions characterized by a pronounced volume collapse while retaining the same crystal symmetry and space group (P6/mmm). ICOHP analysis shows the transition is driven by redistribution of bonding character between intralayer and interlayer atomic bonds.

Conclusion: AM₂Al₉ compounds serve as a promising platform for experimentally investigating rare isostructural phase transitions and critical phenomena under pressure, deepening understanding of symmetry-preserving structural phase transitions.

Abstract: External parameters such as temperature, pressure, and chemical doping can induce structural phase transitions in materials. Although such transitions usually involve a change in symmetry, an uncommon exception is the isostructural phase transition, which is first order yet preserves the symmetry of the parent structure. Using first-principles calculations, we show that $AM_2$Al$_9$ compounds ($A$ = Ba, Ca, Sr, or Eu; $M$ = Fe, Co, or Ni) undergo pressure-induced isostructural phase transitions. At the transition pressure, these systems exhibit a pronounced volume collapse while retaining the same crystal symmetry and space group ($P6/mmm$). Bonding analysis based on the integrated crystal orbital Hamilton population (ICOHP) shows that the transition is driven by a redistribution of bonding character between intralayer and interlayer atomic bonds. Because isostructural transitions are rare in single crystals, $AM_2$Al$_9$ provides a promising platform for investigating critical phenomena under pressure and for deepening our understanding of symmetry-preserving structural transitions.

</details>


### [55] [Orbital occupation selects structural dimensionality in binary transition-metal oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28996)
*Deping Guo,Renhong Wang,Cong Wang,Meng Gao,Wu Zhou,Yanning Zhang,Fei Pang,Zhihai Cheng,Wei Ji*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper shows that orbital occupation can select the bonding framework itself (not just modify it), determining structural dimensionality in transition-metal oxides. Using first-principles calculations on CrO, they identify two competing orbital-structure states ($d_{x^2-y^2}$ favoring a 3D covalent phase and $d_{z^2}$ stabilizing a 2D layered phase), and generalize this into a filling-dependence rule across 3d binary oxides.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Orbital-lattice coupling in transition-metal oxides is traditionally discussed within a fixed bonding framework. The authors aim to demonstrate that orbital occupation can also select the bonding framework itself, offering a mechanistic route to control structural dimensionality and enabling the design of low-dimensional oxide materials.

Method: First-principles calculations (DFT) on CrO, including constrained-occupation calculations to manipulate $d_{z^2}$ filling, stability tests against exchange-correlation functionals and on-site Hubbard $U$ variations, monolayer dynamical stability analysis, and exfoliation energy calculations. The analysis is extended across related 3d binary oxides to establish a filling-dependence relationship.

Result: CrO exhibits two competing orbital-structure states: $d_{x^2-y^2}$ occupation gives a 3D connected covalent phase, while $d_{z^2}$ gives a weakly coupled layered phase. Increasing $d_{z^2}$ filling contracts in-plane lattice and expands out-of-plane. The two phases have distinct magnetic ground states and ferroelastic responses. The layered phase is robust against functional/U variations, stable down to monolayer limit, with low exfoliation energy of 46 meV/Å².

Conclusion: Orbital occupation can act as a structural dimensionality selector in transition-metal oxides. A filling-dependence relation predicts 2D vs 3D bonding preference across 3d binary oxides, providing a microscopic framework for exploring and designing low-dimensional oxide materials.

Abstract: Orbital-lattice coupling in transition-metal oxides is usually discussed within a given bonding framework, where orbital occupation is intertwined with local coordination, strain, or symmetry breaking. Here, we show that orbital occupation can also select the bonding framework itself, thereby determining structural dimensionality. Using first-principles calculations, we identify CrO as a prototype in which the single active $3d\,e_g$ electron of high-spin Cr$^{2+}$ gives rise to two competing orbital-structure states. The $d_{x^2-y^2}$ occupation favors a three-dimensionally connected covalent phase, whereas the $d_{z^2}$ occupation stabilizes a weakly coupled layered phase. Constrained-occupation calculations show that increasing the $d_{z^2}$ filling continuously contracts the in-plane lattice while expanding the structure along the layer normal. The two phases exhibit distinct magnetic ground states and ferroelastic responses. Moreover, the layered phase is robust against exchange-correlation functional and on-site ($U$) variations, remains dynamically stable down to the monolayer limit, and has a low exfoliation energy of 46 meV/Angstrom^2. Extending the analysis across related $3d$ binary oxides reveals a filling-dependence relation between accessible orbital filling and the preference for 2D or 3D connected bonding motifs, providing a microscopic basis for exploring low-dimensional oxide materials.

</details>


### [56] [Chemically Resolved Topological Coordinates Link Structural Dynamics and Configurational Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30523)
*Ayush Kumar Pandey,Abhishek Tewari*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces chemically directed persistent homology at four resolutions to connect atomic connectivity across length scales to dynamics and energetic ordering. Applied to CsPbI3 AIMD trajectories, it reveals that γ-phase Pb networks retain topological memory longer and show slower iodide correlation decay despite softer Pb–I restoring forces. It identifies rare δ-like Pb environments predicting subsequent large displacements, resolves a δ|γ boundary, shows compact dopant arrangements are energetically favored, and demonstrates that machine-learned potentials with low energy errors can still reverse DFT configurational ordering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic coordinates alone do not reveal how chemical connectivity across length scales relates to atomic motion and configurational energy ordering. The authors aim to provide a chemically informed topological framework to bridge structure, dynamics, and energy ordering.

Method: They formulate chemically directed persistent homology at four resolutions—complete networks, individual sites, spatial fields, and substitutional arrangements—preserving chemical identity and length scale. They apply it to AIMD trajectories of δ- and γ-CsPbI3 at five temperatures from 500–700 K, to a 2560-atom δ|γ interface, and to substituted CsPbI3, and compare learned energy models (SchNet, Allegro).

Result: γ-CsPbI3 has lower Pb–I restoring stiffness and larger iodide excursions but 2.27× slower iodide correlation decay and 1.63× longer Pb-network memory than δ. Rare γ-phase Pb sites with δ-like Cs-cage connectivity precede 0.17 Å greater Pb displacement. Compact dopant arrangements are lower in DFT energy than dispersed ones. 61 of 247 ML models with energy errors below 1 meV/atom reverse the DFT ordering.

Conclusion: Chemical network topology provides a key link between structure and physical response, and serves as a test for whether learned energy models preserve DFT configurational ordering even when average errors are small, since relative configurational energies set Boltzmann populations.

Abstract: Atomic coordinates specify a structure, but they do not reveal how chemical connectivity across several length scales relates to atomic motion and configurational energy ordering. We formulate chemically directed persistent homology at four resolutions---complete networks, individual sites, spatial fields, and substitutional arrangements---while retaining the chemical identity and length scale of each connectivity feature. In \textit{ab initio} molecular dynamics (AIMD) trajectories of $δ$- and $γ$-CsPbI$_3$ at five temperatures spanning 500--700 K, the corner-sharing $γ$ network has a lower Pb--I restoring stiffness and permits larger iodide excursions, yet iodide positional correlations decay 2.27 times more slowly and Pb-network topology retains memory 1.63 times longer than in the edge-sharing $δ$ phase. Local softness and loss of network memory are therefore distinct. At individual sites, rare $γ$-phase Pb environments with a $δ$-like Cs-cage connectivity precede 0.17 $\mathring{\mathrm{A}}$ greater Pb displacement over the subsequent 0.5 ps. The same Pb-network coordinate resolves disruption of corner-sharing connectivity across a 2560-atom $δ|γ$ boundary. In substituted CsPbI$_3$, compact dopant arrangements undergo greater cooperative host relaxation and lie lower in density-functional-theory (DFT) energy than dispersed arrangements of the same composition. SchNet and Allegro model families with comparable energy errors encode opposite ordering along this coordinate, and 61 of 247 supplied models with errors below 1 meV atom$^{-1}$ on separate test structures reverse the DFT relation. Because relative configurational energies set Boltzmann populations, chemical network topology links structure to physical response and tests whether learned energy models preserve DFT configurational ordering even when their average errors are small.

</details>


### [57] [Emergence and suppression of phonon vortices in two-dimensional crystals: Interplay of lattice symmetry, heavy impurities, and shear](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30645)
*Yu-Tian Zhang,Deng Pan,Yuliang Jin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Phonon vortices in 2D materials arise as symmetry-adapted linear combinations of degenerate planar phonon modes, with coefficients fixed by the lattice point group. They are intrinsic standing waves in perfect crystals. Heavy impurities favor vortex modes via resonance-induced frequency softening, while shear deformation suppresses them by breaking rotational symmetry, producing a predictable strain threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the fundamental origin of phonon vortices (vortex-like displacement fields in vibrational modes of 2D materials) and to establish a general symmetry-based framework that explains how they form, how they can be tuned via impurities and strain, and how they can be predicted.

Method: A symmetry-based framework grounded in lattice point group theory and energy minimization. The authors show that phonon vortices are symmetry-adapted linear combinations of degenerate planar modes, and analyze the competing effects of mass impurities (which favor vortices via resonance softening) and shear deformation (which suppress vortices by breaking rotational symmetry) to derive a strain threshold.

Result: The authors demonstrate that phonon vortices are intrinsic standing-wave solutions in perfect crystals, requiring no defects. They show that a heavy mass impurity preferentially stabilizes vortex modes through resonance-induced frequency softening, while shear deformation suppresses them, yielding a precisely predictable strain threshold governing the transition.

Conclusion: The symmetry and energy-minimization framework provides a general basis for understanding vibrational topological defects induced by various impurities or defects. Defect and shear engineering emerges as an effective strategy for tuning vibrational modes and thereby controlling thermal and mechanical properties of 2D crystals.

Abstract: Phonon vortices are vortex-like displacement fields that appear in the vibrational modes of two- dimensional materials. Here, we demonstrate that these vortices arise as symmetry-adapted linear combinations of degenerate planar phonon modes, with the superposition coefficients uniquely de- termined by the lattice point group. This symmetry principle establishes that vortices are intrinsic standing-wave solutions in perfect crystals, requiring neither impurities nor disorder. A heavy mass impurity favors vortex modes over planar modes through stronger resonance-induced frequency soft- ening, whereas shear deformation suppresses vortex modes by breaking rotational symmetry. The competition between these two effects gives rise to a precisely predictable strain threshold. The proposed framework, grounded in symmetry and energy-minimization, provides a useful basis for understanding vibrational topological defects induced by other types of impurities or defects. This study further suggests that defect and shear engineering constitutes an effective tuning strategy for controlling vibrational modes and the associated thermal and mechanical properties of crystals.

</details>


### [58] [Dark exciton signatures in the infrared transient absorption of MoS$_2$ monolayer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30504)
*Tian-Xiang Qian,Marco D'Alessandro,Claudio Attaccalite,Tian-Yi Cai,Sheng Ju,Davide Sangalli*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a fully ab initio scheme (on top of GW+BSE) for computing transient-absorption spectra in the exciton-exciton transition energy range, enabling detection of dark excitons of all classes (symmetry-forbidden, spin-forbidden, finite-momentum) in 2D semiconductors. Applied to monolayer MoS2, the signal arises from contributions across Γ, K, M, and Q valleys, with peaks assigned to 1s→2p and 1s→3p transitions from A and B excitons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dark excitons play a central role in nonequilibrium dynamics of 2D semiconductors but are notoriously difficult to characterize experimentally and computationally. Existing methods fail to capture the full spectrum of dark excitonic states, motivating the need for a unified ab initio framework.

Method: The authors develop an ab initio scheme built on GW+BSE for computing exciton-exciton transient-absorption spectra from arbitrary populated exciton distributions. All dark exciton classes are treated on equal footing via exciton-exciton dipole matrix elements evaluated in a locally smooth gauge, incorporating both intra-band and inter-band contributions. Applied to monolayer MoS2 with valley-resolved analyses.

Result: The exc-tr-abs signal in monolayer MoS2 emerges from contributions of four valleys (Γ, K, M, Q), not solely from q=Γ. Exciton-exciton dipoles have similar intensity across valleys, with relative weight governed by initial excitonic population. Main peaks correspond to 1s→2p and 1s→3p transitions from A and B excitons, involving superpositions of spin-flip and spin-conserving excitons across valleys with momentum-averaged contributions.

Conclusion: Transient-absorption in the exciton-exciton transition range provides a viable experimental route to probe dark excitonic states of all classes in 2D semiconductors. The interpretation of the signal must account for multi-valley contributions, as restricting to q=Γ alone leads to substantially different conclusions.

Abstract: Dark excitons play a central role in the nonequilibrium dynamics of two-dimensional semiconductors, but remain difficult to characterize. In this work we show that transient-absorption experiments, with probe pulses tuned in the exciton-exciton transitions energy range (exc-tr-abs), can be used to detect excitations from any populated dark excitonic state, including symmetry-forbidden, spin-forbidden, and finite momentum ones. We develop a fully ab initio scheme, on top of $GW$+BSE, for computing exc-tr-abs spectra from arbitrary populated exciton distributions. The different classes of dark excitons are included on an equal footing, by evaluating exciton-exciton dipole matrix elements in a locally smooth gauge, which accounts for both intra-band and inter-band contributions. As a test case, we consider monolayer MoS$_2$, for which experimental data are available. The exc-tr-abs signal emerges from a sum of contributions from four different valleys in the exciton dispersion, namely $Γ$, $K$, $M$, and $Q$ valleys. The interpretation of the final signal is substantially different from the one based on transitions at $\mathbf{q}=Γ$ alone. The exciton-exciton dipoles show similar intensity among these valleys, while their relative weight is mostly dictated by the initial excitonic population. State- and spin-resolved analyses show that the main peaks can be described as $1s \rightarrow 2p$ and $1s \rightarrow 3p$ transitions from A and B excitons, but with a superposition of transitions from both spin-flip and spin-conserving excitons across the valleys, and with contributions averaged over different momenta within the individual valleys.

</details>


### [59] [Single-pulse strain-induced precessional dynamics of magnetization in Co-doped iron garnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29245)
*Héloïse Damas,Carl S. Davies,Tomasz Zalewski,Petr M. Vetoshko,Vladimir I. Belotelov,Andrzej Stupakiewicz,Andrei Kirilyuk*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that transient lattice deformation from mid-infrared pulses drives reversible precessional magnetization dynamics in Co-doped yttrium iron garnet, as confirmed by single-shot pump-probe magneto-optical microscopy and micromagnetic simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While ultrafast control of magnetization through lattice excitation offers a promising route to manipulate magnetic order on short timescales, the specific role of transient strain in driving magnetization dynamics has been poorly understood.

Method: Single-shot pump-probe magneto-optical microscopy resolves structural and magnetic responses of Co-doped YIG to individual 5-ps mid-infrared pulses. Micromagnetic simulations incorporating transient strain are used to reproduce the observed magnetization dynamics.

Result: The excitation generates a localized strain field with an outward-propagating elastic wave. Magnetic contrast decreases then reverses on a ~1.5-2 ns timescale before recovering. Structural and magnetic responses show correlated wavelength and pulse-energy dependences. Simulations reproduce precessional reorientation within individual domains while preserving labyrinthine domain morphology.

Conclusion: Transient lattice deformation is identified as the link between single-pulse mid-infrared excitation and reversible precessional magnetization dynamics in Co-doped yttrium iron garnet.

Abstract: Ultrafast control of magnetization through lattice excitation provides a route to manipulating magnetic order on short timescales, yet the role of transient strain in driving magnetization dynamics remains poorly understood. Here, single-shot pump-probe magneto-optical microscopy is used to resolve the structural and magnetic responses of cobalt-doped yttrium iron garnet to individual 5-ps mid-infrared pulses. The excitation generates a localized strain field together with an outward-propagating elastic wave. Concurrently, the magnetic contrast decreases following excitation and subsequently reverses on a timescale of approximately 1.5-2 ns before recovering to its initial state. The structural and magnetic responses exhibit closely correlated wavelength and pulse-energy dependences, indicating a common excitation pathway. Micromagnetic simulations incorporating transient strain reproduce the precessional reorientation of the magnetization within individual domains while largely preserving the labyrinthine domain morphology. These results identify transient lattice deformation as the link between single-pulse mid-infrared excitation and reversible precessional magnetization dynamics in Co-doped yttrium iron garnet.

</details>


### [60] [Analogue Phase Change Computational Memory with High Precision Reads and Energy Efficient Writes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29338)
*Ghazi Sarwat Syed,Loris Coccia,Vara Prasad Jonnalagadda,Antonio Massimiliano Mio,Asit Ray,Matthew BrightSky,Abu Sebastian*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper introduces a compact phase-change memory device architecture that combines an ultra-confined active switching volume with a non-insulating thin film to overcome trade-offs among computational precision, efficiency, and density in resistive memory for in-memory computing. The architecture achieves ~6-bit precision, conductance below 50 μS, and sub-100 μA programming currents using conventional undoped phase-change materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Resistive memory is promising for in-memory computing, but existing device architectures cannot simultaneously achieve high computational precision, efficiency, and density due to inherent trade-offs among these metrics. A new device architecture is needed to overcome this fundamental limitation.

Method: The authors introduce a compact phase-change memory device architecture featuring (1) an ultra-confined active switching volume to enhance electro-thermal efficiency and (2) a non-insulating thin film to suppress temporal conductance fluctuations. They analytically model the devices and back-end integrate them into crossbar arrays.

Result: The architecture enables computational precision approaching 6 bits, low conductance values below 50 μS with adequate conductance window, and a viable pathway toward sub-100 μA programming currents under nominal operating voltages, even using conventional undoped phase-change materials.

Conclusion: The proposed phase-change memory device architecture successfully resolves the precision-efficiency-density trade-off in resistive memory, offering a practical pathway for high-performance in-memory computing with conventional materials.

Abstract: Resistive memory technologies offer a compelling advantage for in-memory computing. However, realizing a device architecture that simultaneously achieves high computational precision, efficiency, and density has remained elusive due to inherent trade-offs among these performance metrics. Here, we introduce a com- pact phase-change memory device architecture that combines an ultra-confined active switching volume for enhanced electro-thermal efficiency with a non-insulating thin film that suppresses temporal conductance fluctuations. We analytically model and back-end integrate these devices into crossbar arrays. Even with conventional, undoped phase-change materials, the device architecture enables a computational precision approaching 6 bits, low conductance values below 50 μS with an adequate conductance window, and a viable pathway toward sub-100 μA programming currents under nominal operating voltages.

</details>


### [61] [Strain-Driven Electronic and Catalytic Modulation of g-C3N4/GeS van der Waals heterostructure for Photocatalytic Water Splitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29340)
*Soumendra Kumar Das,Smruti Ranjan Parida,Tapas Kumbhakar,Prasanjit Samal,Sridhar Sahu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A first-principles DFT study reveals that a g-C3N4/GeS heterostructure with type-II band alignment and a 2.17 eV indirect band gap exhibits strain-tunable photocatalytic activity for water splitting, with improved HER kinetics and significantly reduced OER overpotential under biaxial tensile strain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore sustainable hydrogen production via photocatalytic water splitting, and to investigate whether strain engineering can optimize the photocatalytic performance of a two-dimensional g-C3N4/GeS heterostructure.

Method: First-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations, including band structure analysis, band edge alignment with water redox potentials, Gibbs free energy calculations for HER (ΔGHER), OER overpotential evaluation, Ab initio molecular dynamics (AIMD) simulations, and optical absorption analysis under varying biaxial tensile strain (0-3%).

Result: The heterostructure exhibits a type-II band alignment with an indirect band gap of 2.17 eV, smaller than the individual monolayers (g-C3N4: 2.81 eV, GeS: 3.31 eV). Biaxial tensile strain up to 3% modulates the band gap and edge positions for proper alignment with water redox potentials. ΔGHER is 0.2 eV for pristine and near-thermoneutral (within ±0.1 eV) at +1% and +2% strain. The OER overpotential drops from 2.17 V to 0.97 V with increasing strain. AIMD and visible-region optical absorption confirm stability and photocatalytic viability.

Conclusion: Strain engineering is an effective strategy to enhance the photocatalytic performance of the g-C3N4/GeS heterostructure for hydrogen generation, achieving favorable band alignment, near-thermoneutral HER kinetics, reduced OER overpotential, and thermodynamic stability under applied biaxial tensile strain.

Abstract: Photocatalytic water splitting offers a viable pathway for sustainable hydrogen production. In this study, first-principles density functional theory calculations were performed to explore the strain-dependent photocatalytic behaviour of a two-dimensional g-C3N4/GeS heterostructure. The heterostructure shows type-II band alignment with an indirect band gap of 2.17 eV, smaller than those of the individual g-C3N4 (2.81 eV) and GeS (3.31 eV) monolayers. Biaxial tensile strain up to 3% effectively modulates the band gap and band edge positions, allowing suitable alignment with water redox potentials. The calculated Gibbs free energy for the hydrogen evolution reaction (ΔGHER) is 0.2 eV for the pristine heterostructure and approaches near-thermoneutral values (-/+ 0.1 eV) under +1% and +2% strain. Meanwhile, the OER overpotential decreases from 2.17 V to 0.97 V with increasing strain. AIMD simulations and optical absorption in the visible region confirm the thermodynamic stability and promising photocatalytic potential of the heterostructure for hydrogen generation.

</details>


### [62] [PINN-Phase: A physics-informed neural network for curvature-driven multiphase-field evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29413)
*Seifallah Elfetni,Peter H. Seeberger,Theodore Tyrikos-Ergas*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: PINN-Phase is a physics-informed neural time integrator for multiphase-field simulations that advances polycrystalline microstructures from initial conditions without case-specific tuning, enforcing phase bounds and unit sum at each step. It achieves 0.94-3.71% grain-label disagreement across ten 2D test cases, reaches nearly 3x the training time horizon, runs ~5.2 min per 12,000-step rollout on a single GPU, and generalizes to pre-registered 64-grain and 3D 16-grain models with strong survivor/extinction recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase-field simulation of polycrystalline microstructures is computationally costly when many related cases must be evolved over long times, motivating the need for a faster surrogate integrator that can generalize across initial conditions without retraining per case.

Method: A physics-informed neural time integrator (PINN-Phase) advances the full multiphase field from its initial condition, enforcing phase bounds and unit sum at every step. Post-initial-condition reference states are only used for evaluation, and a single trained model is applied to unseen microstructures within a fixed benchmark family without case-specific tuning.

Result: A single trained 25-grain model meets all predefined criteria in seven of eight unseen 2D microstructures and both stress cases, with 0.94-3.71% terminal grain-label disagreement across ten cases. A 12,000-step rollout takes ~5.2 min on a single GPU, reaching nearly 3x the training horizon. A pre-registered 64-grain model achieves 6.09% disagreement (retaining all 21 reference survivors plus one); a continuation with doubled horizon reaches 3.97% and the exact survivor set. In 3D, a 16-grain 96^3 model recovers the exact terminal active set and all three extinction identities in six of six unseen microstructures, five passing the complete qualification.

Conclusion: PINN-Phase demonstrates structurally admissible long-horizon prediction and prospective initial-condition transfer within fixed benchmark families, providing an efficient and generalizable surrogate for polycrystalline multiphase-field evolution.

Abstract: Phase-field simulation of polycrystalline microstructures becomes costly when many related cases must be evolved over long times. We introduce PINN-Phase, a physics-informed neural time integrator that advances the full multiphase field from its initial condition and enforces phase bounds and unit sum at every step; on the reported explicit multiphase-field benchmarks, post-initial-condition reference states serve only for evaluation. Without case-specific tuning, a single trained 25-grain model meets all predefined criteria in seven of eight unseen microstructures fixed before evaluation and in both stress cases, with 0.94-3.71% terminal grain-label disagreement across the ten cases. Each 12,000-step rollout takes about 5.2 min on a single GPU and reaches nearly three times the temporal horizon represented during training. A pre-registered 64-grain model reaches 6.09% disagreement, retaining all 21 reference survivors plus one additional grain; a post-evaluation continuation with a doubled training horizon and 25 additional epochs reaches 3.97% and the exact survivor set. In three dimensions, one trained 16-grain 96^3 model recovers the exact terminal active set and all three extinction identities in six of six unseen microstructures, five of which meet the complete predefined qualification. These results demonstrate structurally admissible long-horizon prediction and prospective initial-condition transfer within fixed benchmark families.

</details>


### [63] [Vibrational Origin of the Barocaloric Effect in the Spin-Crossover Complex Fe(pap-5NO2)2: A Combined DFT and Mean-Field Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29447)
*Alan de Souza,Antonio M. dos Santos,Yongqiang Cheng,Vinícius S. R. de Sousa,Mario Reis*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a theoretical framework combining DFT calculations with an effective mean-field Hamiltonian to explain the microscopic origin of the barocaloric effect in the spin-crossover complex Fe(pap-5NO2)2. The model quantitatively reproduces the experimental barocaloric entropy change and identifies low and mid-frequency metal-ligand vibrations as the primary source of vibrational entropy change, with lattice entropy accounting for ~84% of the total response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While barocaloric performance has been experimentally reported for Fe(pap-5NO2)2, the microscopic origin of its pressure-induced entropy change—specifically the relative contributions of vibrational, configurational, and spin degrees of freedom—has not been quantitatively established.

Method: The authors combine density functional theory (DFT) calculations of vibrational frequencies for both low-spin (S=0) and high-spin (S=2) states with an effective mean-field Hamiltonian. Displacement-vector overlap analysis establishes correspondence between vibrational spectra of the two spin states, and a thermodynamic model explicitly accounts for configurational, spin, and lattice entropy contributions.

Result: The model quantitatively reproduces the experimental barocaloric entropy change (~70 J kg-1 K-1 maximum reversible for 2 kbar pressure change) and predicts a spin-crossover temperature of T_1/2 = 313 K, in excellent agreement with the experimental value of 308 K. Lattice entropy (molecular vibrations) accounts for ~84% of the total barocaloric response, with configurational and spin contributions being comparatively small.

Conclusion: Low and mid-frequency metal-ligand vibrations are the primary microscopic origin of the vibrational entropy change driving the barocaloric effect in Fe(pap-5NO2)2, and lattice (vibrational) entropy dominates the response over configurational and spin entropy contributions.

Abstract: Despite the barocaloric performance recently reported for the spin-crossover complex Fe(pap-5NO2)2, the microscopic origin of its pressure-induced entropy change remains poorly understood. In particular, the relative contributions of vibrational, configurational, and spin degrees of freedom to the barocaloric response have not yet been quantitatively established. Here, we develop a theoretical framework that combines density functional theory (DFT) calculations with an effective mean field Hamiltonian to investigate the barocaloric effect in this spin-crossover complex. Vibrational frequencies of the low-spin (S = 0) and high-spin (S = 2) states obtained from first-principles calculations are incorporated into a thermodynamic model that explicitly accounts for configurational, spin, and lattice entropy contributions. By establishing a correspondence between the vibrational spectra of both spin states through displacement-vector overlap analysis, we identify low and mid frequency metal-ligand vibrations as the primary microscopic origin of the vibrational entropy change. The proposed model quantitatively reproduces the experimentally reported barocaloric entropy change for a pressure variation of 2 kbar, corresponding to a maximum reversible entropy change of approximately 70 J kg-1 K-1, and predicts a spin-crossover temperature of T_1/2 = 313 K under ambient pressure, in excellent agreement with the experimental value of 308 K. Analysis of the entropy components reveals that lattice entropy associated with molecular vibrations accounts for approximately 84% of the total barocaloric response, whereas configurational and spin contributions are comparatively small.

</details>


### [64] [Solid and Quasi-Solid Electrolytes for Zinc Batteries: Balancing Water Activity, Ion Transport, and Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29586)
*Souvik Naskar,Jiaqian Qin,Eric Jianfeng Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper critically examines zinc electrolytes across a continuum from water-rich hydrogels to dry polymers, solvated crystals, and inorganic conductors, showing that neither high bulk ionic conductivity nor nominally water-free composition reliably predicts cell performance. It proposes a classification scheme based on phase state, mobile-solvent fraction, and dominant transport mechanism, and argues that controlled-solvation and hybrid electrolytes offer the most credible near-term path for zinc batteries, while truly solvent-free Zn2+ conductors remain a longer-term scientific target.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zinc batteries promise safe, low-cost energy storage, but aqueous electrolytes cause hydrogen evolution, corrosion, cathode dissolution, and non-uniform zinc deposition. Replacing water with solid or quasi-solid electrolytes suppresses these issues but removes the medium enabling rapid Zn2+ transport and conformal contact. This tension has been obscured by inconsistent use of "solid state" and conductivity-based comparisons, motivating a rigorous, critical framework for evaluating zinc electrolytes.

Method: The authors conduct a critical examination of zinc electrolytes across a continuum (water-rich hydrogels to dry polymers, solvated crystals, and inorganic conductors). They distinguish water content from thermodynamic water activity, classify materials by phase state, mobile-solvent fraction, and dominant transport mechanism, and analyze how practical factors—electrolyte thickness, Zn2+ transference, interfacial resistance, and evolving contact—determine real cell performance rather than bulk conductivity alone.

Result: The analysis shows that high bulk ionic conductivity and nominally water-free composition do not reliably predict cell performance. Instead, electrolyte thickness, Zn2+ transference number, interfacial resistance, and evolving contact are often decisive. Controlled-solvation and hybrid electrolytes emerge as the most promising near-term options, whereas genuinely solvent-free Zn2+ conductors remain a long-term research target.

Conclusion: Progress in solid-state zinc batteries requires transparent reporting of solvent state and validation under realistic conditions: thin electrolytes, realistic electrode loadings, limited zinc excess, and calendar-life testing. The field should move beyond conductivity-centric comparisons toward a more nuanced classification and evaluation framework for zinc electrolytes.

Abstract: Zinc batteries offer a compelling route to safe and low-cost energy storage, yet their reliance on aqueous electrolytes promotes hydrogen evolution, corrosion, cathode dissolution, and non-uniform zinc deposition. Replacing the liquid with a solid or quasi-solid electrolyte can suppress these processes, but it also removes the medium that enables rapid Zn2+ transport and conformal electrode contact. This tension, the price of removing water, has been obscured by inconsistent use of the term solid state and by comparisons based largely on bulk ionic conductivity. Here we critically examine zinc electrolytes across a continuum from water-rich hydrogels to dry polymers, solvated crystals, and inorganic conductors. We distinguish water content from thermodynamic water activity and classify these materials according to phase state, mobile-solvent fraction, and dominant transport mechanism. We show that neither high conductivity nor nominally water-free composition reliably predicts cell performance: electrolyte thickness, Zn2+ transference, interfacial resistance, and evolving contact often determine the practical outcome. Controlled-solvation and hybrid electrolytes therefore provide the most credible near-term path, whereas genuinely solvent-free Zn2+ conductors remain a longer-term scientific target. Progress will require transparent reporting of solvent state and validation using thin electrolytes, realistic electrode loadings, limited zinc excess, and calendar-life testing.

</details>


### [65] [Evaluating a 4B open-weights local LLM for agentic DFT workflows: a literature reproducibility audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29665)
*Shambhu Bhandari Sharma*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work evaluates an open-weights Qwen3:4B model running a fully local autonomous scientific pipeline for materials science, applied to pentagonal 2D materials. The neurosymbolic architecture combines agentic text extraction with deterministic DFT code gates. The extractor achieves 95.7% precision but only 67.3% recall, complete GPU residency outperforms weight/cache precision for quality, and the workflow reproduces lattice constants with 2.3% mean absolute relative error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hosted commercial models in agentic materials science workflows suffer from reproducibility, economic, and data-privacy constraints. This work seeks to determine whether lightweight open-weights models can viably execute autonomous scientific workflows entirely on local hardware.

Method: An open-weights Qwen3:4B model runs a neurosymbolic pipeline: agents extract parameters from unstructured text and propose, while deterministic code disposes (generates DFT inputs and drives simulations). The workflow is guarded by verbatim text grounding and multi-pass inference unions to counteract hardware-induced structural collapse. Evaluated across three hardware configurations with varying GPU residency, weight precision, and cache precision, against 201 expert judgements.

Result: Extractor achieves 95.7% precision (95% CI 90.3-98.1%) and 67.3% recall (59.8-74.0%) against expert judgements. Precision for absent parameters does not exceed 47.0%, making the omission rate a loose upper bound on literature incompleteness. Complete GPU residency improves Matthews correlation from 0.414 to 0.530 at fixed quantisation and to 0.560 with unquantised cache. Only 33.3% (19/57) of studies are reproducible in principle; convergence reproduces lattice constants with 2.3% mean absolute relative error where prototypes are retained.

Conclusion: Lightweight open-weights models can reliably drive autonomous agentic workflows in materials science when bounded by deterministic code gates. Full GPU residency is more critical to extraction quality than weight or cache precision, and verbatim-grounding constraints ensure extracted parameters are strictly factual.

Abstract: Agentic workflows in materials science relying on hosted commercial models face severe reproducibility, economic, and data-privacy constraints. To explore fully local agentic science, this work evaluates an open-weights Qwen3:4B model executing an autonomous scientific pipeline across varying hardware constraints. Applied to pentagonal two-dimensional materials, the system extracts parameters from unstructured text, translates them into density functional theory (DFT) inputs, and drives simulations to convergence under a strict neurosymbolic architecture where agents propose and deterministic code disposes. The workflow is guarded by verbatim text grounding and multi-pass inference unions to counteract hardware-induced structural collapse. Evaluated against 201 expert judgements, the extractor achieves 95.7% precision (95% CI 90.3-98.1%) and 67.3% recall (59.8-74.0%), ensuring extracted parameters are strictly factual. However, precision identifying absent parameters does not exceed 47.0%, establishing that the measured omission rate constitutes a loose upper bound on true literature incompleteness. Across three hardware configurations, complete GPU residency governs extraction quality more fundamentally than weight or cache precision, raising Matthews correlation from 0.414 to 0.530 at fixed quantisation and to 0.560 with an unquantised cache. A corpus-scale audit indicates only 19 (33.3%) of the 57 studies are reproducible in principle, reporting every method parameter needed to re-initialise the calculation. Driven to convergence, the workflow reproduces published lattice constants with a mean absolute relative error of 2.3% where the relaxed structure retains its prototype, establishing that lightweight open-weights models can reliably drive autonomous agentic workflows when bounded by deterministic code gates.

</details>


### [66] [High-Field Terahertz Spin Resonance in Cr$_2$O$_3$ above the Spin-Flop Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29712)
*Kaiyang Huang,Yuto Kinoshita,Natsuki Kanda,Takuya Matsuda,Masashi Tokunaga,Ryusuke Matsunaga,Yasuhiro H. Matsuda*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Single-shot terahertz time-domain spectroscopy of Cr₂O₃ in pulsed magnetic fields up to 30 T reveals a nearly linear field dependence of the resonance frequency (~22 GHz/T) in the 20–30 T range, which is smaller than the 28 GHz/T observed at low fields. This reduction is robust against temperature and field tilt, indicating an intrinsic high-field property.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend terahertz spectroscopy of the antiferromagnet Cr₂O₃ into high magnetic field regimes (up to 30 T) where previous studies were limited to low fields, and to investigate whether the resonance frequency's field dependence deviates from low-field behavior, which could reveal intrinsic high-field physics.

Method: Single-shot terahertz time-domain spectroscopy was performed on Cr₂O₃ in pulsed magnetic fields up to 30 T. The resonance frequency was measured well above the spin-flop field (in the 20–30 T range) and its field slope was extracted and compared to low-field results. Control experiments varied temperature and field tilt angle (15°) to test the robustness of the observed slope reduction.

Result: Above the spin-flop field, the resonance frequency shows a nearly linear field dependence with a slope of ~22 GHz/T, which is smaller than the 28 GHz/T reported from low-field measurements. The reduced slope is insensitive to temperature and to a 15° field tilt.

Conclusion: The reduction in the resonance frequency field slope at high fields is an intrinsic property of Cr₂O₃ in the high-field regime rather than an extrinsic effect related to temperature or field alignment, suggesting new physics emerges well above the spin-flop field.

Abstract: We report single-shot terahertz time-domain spectroscopy of Cr$_2$O$_3$ in pulsed magnetic fields up to 30 T. Well above the spin-flop field, in the 20-30 T range, the resonance frequency exhibits a nearly linear field dependence with a slope of ~22 GHz/T, smaller than the 28 GHz/T reported from low-field measurements. This reduction is insensitive to temperature and to a 15° field tilt, suggesting an intrinsic high-field property.

</details>


### [67] [Phonon-Localization-Driven Decoupling of Dual-Channel Transport for Record-Low Intrinsic Lattice Thermal Conductivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29778)
*Zhunyun Tang,Xiaoxia Wang,Jin Li,Chaoyu He,Chao Tang,Mingxing Chen,Tao Ouyang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that phonon localization decouples the dual-channel (particle-like and wave-like) thermal transport channels in quasi-1D ternary helical crystals, achieving record-low lattice thermal conductivity (e.g., 0.086 W/mK for GaSeI at room temperature) by compressing phonon branches into localized flat bands that suppress both group velocity components simultaneously.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The intrinsic lattice thermal conductivity of crystalline solids is fundamentally limited by the competition between particle-like phonon propagation and wave-like phonon tunneling channels. The authors aim to break this inherent trade-off by identifying a pathway to simultaneously suppress both transport channels to reach the lowest possible thermal conductivity limit.

Method: The authors propose phonon localization as a strategy to decouple the dual-channel thermal transport. In quasi-1D ternary helical crystals (InSeI, GaSeI, AlSeI), weak interchain interactions and heavy elements cause numerous phonon branches to become highly localized, nearly dispersionless flat bands. This localization suppresses both the diagonal and off-diagonal components of the group velocity, simultaneously reducing both the particle-like (κL_P) and wave-like/coherent (κL_C) contributions to lattice thermal conductivity.

Result: For InSeI, interchain room-temperature κL_P and κL_C are 0.145 and 0.053 W/mK, respectively, yielding a total ultralow κL of 0.198 W/mK. GaSeI and AlSeI achieve record lows of 0.086 and 0.089 W/mK at room temperature, dropping further to 0.058 and 0.059 W/mK at 900 K.

Conclusion: Phonon localization provides a robust and general pathway to decouple and simultaneously suppress both particle-like and wave-like thermal transport channels, offering useful insights for exploring and achieving the ultimate thermal conductivity limits in crystalline materials.

Abstract: A fundamental bottleneck in pushing the intrinsic lattice thermal conductivity of inorganic crystalline solids to its lowest limit arises from the inherent competition between the particle-like propagation (\(κ_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{P}}\)) and wave-like tunneling (\(κ_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{C}}\)) channels. Herein, we demonstrate that phonon localization provides a robust pathway to decouple the dual-channel transport, achieving record-low \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}\) in quasi-1D ternary helical crystals. Despite the structural complexity leading to densely populated phonon branches and thus inducing abundant coherent phonons, the weak interchain interactions and heavy elements compress numerous branches into highly localized, nearly dispersionless flat bands. Such strong localization simultaneously suppresses both the diagonal and off-diagonal components of the group velocity, thereby synergistically suppressing \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{P}}\) and \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{C}}\). Taking InSeI as an example, the interchain room-temperature \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{P}}\) and \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{C}}\) are 0.145 and 0.053 W/mK, respectively, yielding an ultralow total \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}\) of 0.198 W/mK. Weaker interchain interactions further drive the room-temperature \(κ_{\mathrm{L}}\) of GaSeI and AlSeI to record lows of 0.086 and 0.089 W/mK, respectively; these values even drop to 0.058 and 0.059 W/mK at 900 K. These findings provide useful insights into exploring the thermal conductivity limit in crystals.

</details>


### [68] [Valley-Enabled Intrinsic Dresselhaus Spin-Orbit Coupling in Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29785)
*Johannes L. P. Steinschuld,Hendrik J. Bluhm,Lars R. Schreiber,Seyed Akbar Jafari*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a symmetry-based theory of spin-orbit-valley coupling in silicon, revealing an intrinsic bulk Dresselhaus spin-orbit coupling (independent of interfaces or electric fields) that is valley off-diagonal and proportional to the valley Pauli matrix τ₁. Tight-binding calculations show this bulk coupling is more than an order of magnitude larger than typical interface-induced spin-orbit coupling, equivalent to needing electric fields ~50× larger for the interface mechanism. The theory also derives symmetry-allowed spin-valley couplings from magnetic-field gradients explaining observed valley-dependent Zeeman splitting in micromagnet experiments, predicts an isotropic B_z-linear contribution from out-of-plane magnetic field tilt, and predicts a disorder-robust spin-independent micromagnet-induced valley splitting in the τ₃ channel.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish valley symmetry as a fundamental ingredient in silicon spin-orbit physics and provide new mechanisms for controlling and probing spin and valley degrees of freedom in silicon quantum devices, addressing gaps in understanding intrinsic (non-interface) spin-orbit coupling sources.

Method: Symmetry-based group-theoretic analysis treating the valley degree of freedom as a symmetry-carrying quantum degree of freedom, combined with tight-binding calculations for quantitative validation and derivation of symmetry-allowed coupling terms.

Result: Discovery of an intrinsic bulk Dresselhaus spin-orbit coupling in silicon that is valley off-diagonal (proportional to τ₁), more than an order of magnitude larger than interface-induced coupling (requiring ~50× larger electric fields to match via interface mechanism). Derivation of magnetic-field-gradient-induced spin-valley couplings explaining micromagnet Zeeman splitting observations, plus a predicted isotropic B_z-linear contribution and a disorder-robust τ₃-channel micromagnet-induced valley splitting.

Conclusion: Valley symmetry is a fundamental ingredient in silicon spin-orbit physics; the bulk Dresselhaus coupling and micromagnet-induced couplings provide new, experimentally accessible mechanisms for controlling spin and valley degrees of freedom in silicon quantum devices.

Abstract: We develop a symmetry-based theory of spin-orbit-valley coupling in silicon that reveals an intrinsic source of Dresselhaus spin-orbit coupling independent of interfaces or external electric fields. Treating the valley degree of freedom as a symmetry-carrying quantum degree of freedom, we show that the Dresselhaus interaction is necessarily valley off-diagonal and that a bulk contribution proportional to the valley Pauli matrix $τ_1$ is symmetry allowed. Tight-binding calculations yield a bulk coupling more than an order of magnitude larger than typical interface-induced spin-orbit coupling; achieving the same energy scale through the interface-induced mechanism would require electric fields roughly 50 times larger than typical fields. We further derive the symmetry-allowed spin-valley couplings generated by magnetic-field gradients and show how they account for the valley-dependent Zeeman splitting observed in micromagnet experiments. A slight tilt of the background magnetic field out of the plane produces an additional isotropic contribution linear in $B_z$, providing an experimentally accessible signature of the corresponding coupling constant. Finally, we predict a spin-independent micromagnet-induced valley splitting in the $τ_3$ channel, which is distinct from the $τ_{1,2}$ channels generated by alloy disorder and therefore remains robust against disorder-induced cancellation. These results establish valley symmetry as a fundamental ingredient in the spin-orbit physics of silicon and provide new mechanisms for controlling and probing spin and valley degrees of freedom in silicon quantum devices.

</details>


### [69] [Floquet Topological Spin-Valley-Layertronics on a Layered Dice Lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29833)
*Jianqi Zhong,Teng-Fei Ying,Jinyu Zou,Benjamin T. Zhou*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper predicts a rich set of correlated topological phases in a layered dice lattice system at band filling ν=4 under Hubbard interactions, showing an intrinsic sublattice antiferromagnetic quantum spin-valley Hall insulator that can be driven by circularly polarized light into a Floquet f-wave altermagnet insulator, and by a vertical displacement field into a spin-valley-layer-polarized Chern insulator with electrically controllable quantum numbers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The recent discovery of the dice flat band in layered YCl electride offers a real material platform for exploring correlation and topological physics in dice lattice systems, but the full phase diagram under interactions remains unexplored.

Method: The study considers a generic layered dice lattice at band filling ν=4 with on-site Hubbard interactions, and analyzes how circularly polarized light (Floquet engineering) and a vertical displacement field tune the correlated topological phases.

Result: Three distinct phases are predicted: (i) an intrinsic sublattice antiferromagnetic quantum spin-valley Hall insulator; (ii) a Floquet odd-parity f-wave altermagnet insulator driven by CPL; and (iii) a spin-valley-layer-polarized Chern insulator induced by a vertical displacement field, where the signs of spin, valley, and Chern numbers are all controlled by the field direction.

Conclusion: The layered dice lattice is established as a versatile platform for electrically switchable magnetic and topological phases, offering an all-electrical scheme for integrated spin-valley-layertronics for non-volatile information storage and processing.

Abstract: The recent discovery of long-sought dice flat band in layered YCl electride has opened up rich possibilities of correlation and topological physics in dice lattice systems [Nature Communications 17, 2213 (2026), arXiv:2509.05958]. Here, we reveal a plethora of distinctive correlated topological phases in a generic layered dice lattice system at band filling of $ν=4$ under on-site Hubbard interactions: (i) the system is an intrinsic sublattice anti-ferromagnetic (AFM) quantum spin-valley Hall insulator; (ii) a circularly polarized light (CPL) drives the AFM spin-valley insulator into a Floquet odd-parity $f$-wave altermagnet(AM) insulator; (iii) a vertical displacement field turns the Floquet $f$-wave AM insulator into a spin-valley-layer-polarized Chern insulator, with the sign of spin, valley and Chern number all controlled by the direction of the displacement field. Our results not only establish the layered dice lattice as a versatile platform for electrically switchable magnetic and topological phases, but also provide an all-electrical scheme for integrated spin-valley-layertronics for non-volatile information storage and processing.

</details>


### [70] [Van Hove singularities at the $L$-face of the lutetium nitride phonon dispersion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29994)
*M. Markwitz,K. Kneisel,R. G. Buckley,K. C. Rule,M. Chegeni,H. J. Trodahl,W. F. Holmes-Hewett,J. D. Miller,F. Natali,M. Maddah,B. J. Ruck,S. Granville*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reports the structural and vibrational properties of LuN, a 4f-filled nonmagnetic lanthanide nitride, using elastic and inelastic neutron scattering at 4 K. A peak in the generalized density of states is attributed to a van Hove singularity on the fourfold-degenerate L-face of the Brillouin zone, supported by DFT+U calculations, extending phonon understanding beyond the Γ-point.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To advance understanding of phonon dynamics in lanthanide nitrides (LnN) beyond the Γ-point, using LuN as a prototypical 4f-filled nonmagnetic member due to its simple electronic structure.

Method: Elastic and inelastic neutron scattering measurements at 4 K combined with DFT+U computational input to interpret the generalized phonon density of states.

Result: A peak in the generalized density of states identified, attributed to a van Hove singularity on the fourfold-degenerate L-face of the Brillouin zone.

Conclusion: This work extends the understanding of phonon dynamics in LnN compounds beyond the Γ-point, providing new insight into vibrational properties through neutron scattering and DFT+U analysis.

Abstract: We report the structural and vibrational properties of the prototypical 4$f$-filled nonmagnetic member LuN of the lanthanide nitrides, \textit{Ln}N, with elastic and inelastic neutron scattering data at $4$~K.
