We find a peak in the generalized density of states which, through input from a DFT+$U$ computation, we ascribe to a van Hove singularity on the fourfold-degenerate $L$-face of the Brillouin zone. This work advances the understanding of phonon dynamics in \textit{Ln}N beyond the $Γ$-point.

</details>


### [71] [Libron-phonon coupling and hydrogen-bond dynamics in the vacancy-ordered perovskite (NH4)2SnCl6: a temperature- and pressure-dependent Raman study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30064)
*Vasco S. Neto,Mayra A. P. Gómez,Bruno S. Araújo,Alejandro P. Ayala*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper shows that despite the NH4+ librational motion in (NH4)2SnCl6 being Raman-silent by symmetry, it can be probed via the renormalization it imposes on allowed [SnCl6]2- modes. Combining X-ray diffraction and Raman spectroscopy (10-300 K, up to 10.1 GPa), the authors find a libron-phonon renormalization below ~100 K and an N-H stretch minimum near 120 K, both tracking the classical-to-quantum crossover of the ammonium rotor. Under pressure, the cavity responds via two distinct transitions (translational F2g mode gaining Raman intensity above 1.3 GPa, N-H stretch inverting slope near 1.7 GPa) while the rigid octahedral framework remains essentially decoupled, enabling independent tuning of cation-phonon coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The coupling between molecular A-site cation dynamics and lattice phonons in vacancy-ordered perovskites under external perturbation remains largely unexplored. Since the NH4+ librational motion is strictly Raman-silent in (NH4)2SnCl6, the authors aim to demonstrate an alternative Raman-based route to access it via renormalization effects on symmetry-allowed octahedral modes, and to map the temperature- and pressure-dependent cation dynamics.

Method: Single-crystal X-ray diffraction combined with Raman scattering measurements across a temperature range of 10-300 K and pressures up to 10.1 GPa are used. The renormalization imposed by the Raman-silent NH4+ libration on allowed [SnCl6]2- modes, along with line-shape analysis and pressure-dependent intensity changes, are examined to infer cation-phonon coupling.

Result: The cubic structure evolves smoothly with temperature and pressure, showing no anomaly. Below ~100 K, [SnCl6]2- modes exhibit libron-phonon renormalization (E_eff ~ 4.7 meV) and symmetry-selective line asymmetry; the N-H stretch shows a minimum near 120 K (E_eff ~ 9.3 meV). Both track the classical-to-quantum crossover of the ammonium rotor and lie below the bare librational transition of 13.4 meV. Ammonium linewidths are governed by pure dephasing with no crossover signature. Under pressure, octahedral modes stiffen smoothly while the cation cavity responds at 1.3 and 1.7 GPa (F2g intensity onset and N-H stretch slope inversion, respectively), with full recovery on decompression.

Conclusion: The rigid [SnCl6]2- octahedral framework is essentially decoupled from the dynamically active NH4+ subsystem, which carries the response to both temperature and pressure perturbations. This offers a route to engineer and tune cation-phonon coupling independently of the octahedral network in vacancy-ordered halide perovskites.

Abstract: Vacancy-ordered perovskites R2MX6 combine a rigid inorganic framework with a molecular A-site cation, but how the cation dynamics couples to the lattice phonons under external perturbation remains largely unexplored. The librational motion of NH4+ in (NH4)2SnCl6 is strictly Raman-silent by symmetry, which is why it has been probed almost exclusively by neutron scattering, NMR and NQR. We show that it is nevertheless accessible to Raman spectroscopy, through the renormalization it imposes on the allowed [SnCl6]2- modes. Combining single-crystal X-ray diffraction with Raman scattering between 10 and 300 K and up to 10.1 GPa, we find that the average cubic structure varies smoothly with no anomaly, while below ~100 K the [SnCl6]2- modes acquire a libron-phonon renormalization (E_eff ~ 4.7 meV) and a symmetry-selective line asymmetry, and the N-H stretch passes through a minimum near 120 K (E_eff ~ 9.3 meV); both track the classical-to-quantum crossover of the ammonium rotor. The two effective energies lie below the bare librational transition of 13.4 meV measured by neutrons, as expected for a self-energy scale. The ammonium linewidths, by contrast, are governed by pure dephasing rather than by anharmonic decay and carry no crossover signature. Under pressure the octahedral modes stiffen smoothly, whereas the cavity responds twice: the translational F2g mode of NH4+ gains Raman intensity above 1.3 GPa, and the N-H stretch inverts its pressure slope near 1.7 GPa, with no change of space group and full recovery on decompression. The rigid octahedral framework is thus essentially decoupled from a dynamically active NH4+ subsystem that carries the response to both perturbations, offering a route to tune cation-phonon coupling independently of the octahedral network.

</details>


### [72] [Two-step transient liquid phase bonding of NiTi to Ti-6Al-4V through a NbZrW barrier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30117)
*Zhaoxi Cao,Samuel Price,John P. Reidy,Ian McCue*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A two-step transient liquid phase (TLP) joining route using a refractory NbZrW diffusion barrier enables successful dissimilar joining of NiTi to Ti-6Al-4V by converting the incompatible couple into two independently bondable interfaces. The resulting joints are fully dense, free of continuous intermetallic layers, and exhibit superelastic behavior with stable load-unload cycles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dissimilar joining of NiTi shape memory alloys to Ti-6Al-4V is fundamentally limited by the formation of brittle Ti2Ni intermetallic compounds and degradation of the NiTi functional properties at the joint interface. A new joining strategy is needed to overcome this incompatibility.

Method: A two-step transient liquid phase (TLP) joining route was developed using a refractory NbZrW diffusion barrier. This barrier serves dual roles: (1) as a reactive substrate for NiTi, dissolving to form a Ti-rich Ni-Ti-Nb liquid that infiltrates grain boundaries, terminated by selective Ti absorption into the barrier; and (2) as an inert substrate for Ti-64, joined via contact melting of a sacrificial Cu foil. This converts the incompatible couple into two independently bondable interfaces.

Result: Both interfaces were fully dense and free of continuous intermetallic layers. In tension, joints began transforming at 350 MPa, exhibited a stress plateau to 2.5% global strain (consistent with stress-induced transformation of the NiTi half), and failed at 380-410 MPa beyond the plateau. Five load-unload cycles to 460 MPa showed stable superelastic loops with minor ratcheting.

Conclusion: A refractory diffusion barrier enables dissimilar TLP joining of otherwise incompatible alloys, preserving the superelastic functionality of NiTi while achieving strong, intermetallic-free joints to Ti-6Al-4V.

Abstract: Dissimilar joining of NiTi to Ti-6Al-4V (Ti-64) is limited by brittle Ti2Ni formation and degradation of NiTi functionality. A two-step transient liquid phase (TLP) route was developed in which a refractory NbZrW diffusion barrier converts the incompatible couple into two independently bondable interfaces. The barrier plays a different role for each alloy: a reactive substrate for NiTi, dissolving to form a Ti-rich Ni-Ti-Nb liquid that infiltrates its own grain boundaries and is terminated by selective Ti absorption into the barrier; and an inert substrate for Ti-64, joined through contact melting of a sacrificial Cu foil. Both interfaces are fully dense and free of continuous intermetallic layers. In tension, joints spanning both interfaces began transforming at 350 MPa, exhibited a stress plateau to 2.5 global strain (consistent with stress-induced transformation of the NiTi half of the gauge) and failed beyond the plateau at 380-410 MPa. Five load-unload cycles to 460 MPa showed stable superelastic loops with minor ratcheting. These results demonstrate that a diffusion barrier enables dissimilar TLP joining of otherwise incompatible alloys.

</details>


### [73] [Coexisting Large and Small Polarons in Photoexcited CeO$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30164)
*Valentina Mazzotti,Eleonora Spurio,Nicolas Delnour,Michael Hilke,Kirk H. Bevan,Alexander Shluger,Paola Luches,David G. Cooke*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Time-resolved THz spectroscopy reveals dual polaron formation in CeO₂: delocalized large hole polarons of Fröhlich type form on an ~820 fs timescale with Drude-like conductivity and 100 cm²/Vs mobility, while small electron polarons form via localized coupling to Ce lattice sites causing transient phonon softening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding polaron formation in CeO₂ is essential for explaining charge transfer dynamics in metal oxide photocatalysts, where polaron behavior governs carrier transport and recombination.

Method: Time-resolved terahertz (THz) spectroscopy was used to probe ultrafast photoconductivity in CeO₂, examining contributions from hole and electron polarons and tracking lattice responses via Born effective charge changes.

Result: Large Fröhlich hole polarons form on 820±50 fs timescale with Drude-like optical conductivity and transient hole mobility of 100 cm²/Vs; small electron polarons form via localized Ce lattice coupling, evidenced by reduced Born effective charge and transient phonon softening.

Conclusion: CeO₂ exhibits a dual polaron nature involving both large hole and small electron polarons, providing a foundation for understanding charge transfer in metal oxide photocatalysts.

Abstract: Time-resolved terahertz (THz) spectroscopy is used to probe polaron formation in CeO$_2$. The ultrafast THz photoconductivity is dominated by contributions from large hole and small electron polarons. Delocalized large hole polarons are formed on a $820 \pm 50$ fs time scale and are of Fröhlich type, with optical conductivity well described by a simple Drude model and a transient hole mobility of $100$ cm$^2$/Vs. Small electron polarons form via localized coupling to Ce lattice sites, revealed by reduction in the lattice Born effective charge causing a transient phonon softening. These results establish the dual polaron nature of CeO$_2$ and provide a basis for understanding charge transfer dynamics in metal oxide photocatalysts.

</details>


### [74] [Covariant formula for the driving force for interface migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30296)
*Adam Morawiec*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper derives an explicit, coordinate-free component-wise expression for the contraction of the interface stiffness tensor with the curvature tensor, which determines the driving force for interface migration in crystalline materials. The formulation uses a covariant approach where the stiffness-related tensor defined in 3D Euclidean space is pulled back onto the interface manifold before contraction with the curvature tensor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The interface stiffness tensor governs the response of interface energy to orientation changes and is central to determining the driving force for interface migration via contraction with the curvature tensor. However, practical tensor computations require explicit component-wise expressions valid in arbitrary coordinate systems, which were lacking.

Method: The authors derive a covariant formulation in which the curvature tensor is contracted with the pullback of a stiffness-related tensor (originally defined in three-dimensional Euclidean space) onto the interface manifold. This provides an explicit component-wise expression valid in arbitrary coordinate systems.

Result: An explicit component-wise expression is obtained for the contraction of the stiffness and curvature tensors, valid in arbitrary coordinate systems, establishing a physically consistent foundation for three-dimensional computational models involving interface stiffness.

Conclusion: The covariant treatment provides a physically consistent and practical basis for three-dimensional computational models of interface migration involving interface stiffness anisotropy.

Abstract: Interfaces of crystalline materials are strongly affected by the anisotropy of interface energy. A key quantity in this context is the interface stiffness tensor, which characterizes the response of the interface energy to changes in interface orientation and plays a role in determining the driving force for interface migration. The latter is expressible via contraction of the stiffness tensor and interface curvature tensor. Practical computations involving tensors necessarily rely on their individual components. In this work, an explicit component-wise expression is derived for the contraction of the stiffness and curvature tensors, valid in arbitrary coordinate systems. Within this formulation, to get the driving force, the curvature tensor is contracted with the pullback of a stiffness-related tensor - originally defined in three-dimensional Euclidean space - onto the interface manifold. This covariant treatment establishes a physically consistent foundation for three-dimensional computational models involving interface stiffness.

</details>


### [75] [Real-space overlap is not enough: ambiguity in nanobeam iterative ptychography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30359)
*Stephanie M. Ribet,Mohsen Danaie,Willem P. M. de Kleijne,Arthur R. C. McCray,Frederick Allars,Christopher S. Allen,Colin Ophus,Georgios Varnavides*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates the feasibility of high-resolution iterative ptychography at small convergence angles (e.g., Lorentz mode) where no diffracted disks overlap. It shows that below this overlap threshold, reconstructions become ambiguous and degenerate, lacking phase information for each Bragg reflection, leading to lattice translations or contrast reversals that produce identical forward-model errors. An amorphous substrate can partially restore phasing but it's fragile. At fixed dose, enforcing pure-phase or adding thermal motion independently makes the solution non-unique. Critically, lattice spacing and orientation are always recovered correctly, making the non-unique reconstruction look convincing — diagnosable only by rerunning from different starting points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether high-resolution iterative ptychography can be performed at small convergence angles, which would relax the need for probe correctors and enable experiments at low accelerating voltages or with a de-excited objective lens (e.g., Lorentz mode).

Method: Combined experimental and simulation-based analysis. The authors studied reconstruction behavior when the convergence angle is small enough that no diffracted disks overlap, examined degeneracies (lattice translations and object contrast reversal), tested whether increasing real-space overlap lifts degeneracy, assessed the role of an amorphous substrate in supplying missing phase relationships, and evaluated the effects of pure-phase constraint and thermal motion in the forward model at fixed dose.

Result: Below the disk-overlap threshold, reconstructions are ambiguous due to lack of interference between Bragg disks, yielding degenerate objects (lattice translations and contrast reversals). More real-space overlap does not lift degeneracy. An amorphous substrate can supply phasing but it is fragile. At fixed dose, imposing pure-phase or adding thermal motion independently produces non-unique solutions. Lattice spacing and orientation are always recovered correctly, so the flawed reconstruction appears convincing.

Conclusion: High-resolution ptychography below the convergence-angle overlap threshold is fundamentally ambiguous. The non-uniqueness is subtle and easily overlooked since lattice parameters are correctly recovered; it can only be diagnosed by repeating reconstructions from different starting points. This highlights serious challenges for Lorentz-mode/low-voltage ptychography without the benefits of overlapping diffracted disks.

Abstract: High-resolution iterative ptychography typically relies on a well-aligned, high-convergence-angle electron probe. Here we explore whether it can instead be performed at small convergence angles, relaxing the need for probe correctors and enabling experiments at low accelerating voltages or with a de-excited objective lens, as in Lorentz mode. Through experiments and simulations, we show that once the convergence angle is small enough that no diffracted disks overlap, the resulting reconstruction is ambiguous, posing a significant challenge for robust interpretation of results. This challenge arises because of the lack of interference between Bragg disks in the recorded diffraction pattern intensity, leading to no phase information for each reflection. Reconstructions with these data lead to degenerate objects in which rigid translations of the lattice and reversals of contrast of the object produce the same error between experimental data and the ptychography forward model. Increasing the real-space overlap between probe positions does not lift this degeneracy. An amorphous substrate can supply the missing phase relationships by giving the Bragg beams support in the gaps between disks. This phasing is fragile, however, and survives only where the forward model matches the experiment. At fixed dose, either constraining the object to be a pure phase object or introducing thermal motion into the forward model is enough on its own to make the solution non-unique, highlighting why our experimental reconstructions below the overlap threshold are ambiguous despite ample dose and real-space redundancy. Most troublingly, the lattice spacing and orientation are always recovered correctly, so a non-unique reconstruction looks convincing and can be diagnosed only by repeating the reconstruction from different starting points.

</details>


### [76] [Extending the Pnictide Chemical Space for Photovoltaics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30402)
*Avaneesh Balasubramanian,Gopalakrishnan Sai Gautam*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses first-principles calculations to systematically screen 104 pnictide compositions (nitrides and phosphides) across three structural classes, identifying three promising photovoltaic candidates: NaCaInN2, NaSrInN2, and K4ZnP2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developing novel and efficient materials beyond silicon for photovoltaic applications is required to meet upcoming societal energy needs.

Method: First-principles calculations are used to systematically screen 104 pnictide compositions across three ternary/quaternary classes (ABCX2, BB'B"X2, A4BX2). The screening evaluates ground-state structures, 0 K thermodynamic stabilities, electronic structures, carrier effective masses, dynamic stabilities, and intrinsic point defect formation energies.

Result: Three promising photovoltaic candidates are identified: NaCaInN2, NaSrInN2, and K4ZnP2. All candidates are thermodynamically (meta)stable, have direct (or nearest direct) band gaps optimal for PV applications, and are resistant to forming several types of point defects.

Conclusion: The first-principles driven workflow and identified candidates will advance the development of novel PV materials and reinvigorate interest in the exploration of pnictides.

Abstract: Developing novel and efficient materials that are beyond silicon for photovoltaic (PV) applications is required to meet the upcoming energy needs of our societies. To identify novel candidate materials that can act as PVs, we use first principles calculations to perform a systematic screening of the pnictide chemical space (i.e., nitrides and phosphides). Specifically, we explore three different ternary and quaternary pnictide classes, namely, ABCX$_2$, BB'B"X$_2$, and A$_4$BX$_2$ (A = Li, Na, or K; B, B', B" = Ca, Sr, Mg, or Zn; C = Al, Ga, or In; X = N or P), leading to a set of 104 possible pnictide compositions. Based on our evaluations of ground state structures, 0 K thermodynamic stabilities, electronic structures, carrier effective masses, dynamic stabilities, and intrinsic point defect formation energies, we arrive at three promising candidates, namely, NaCaInN$_2$, NaSrInN$_2$, and K$_4$ZnP$_2$. Notably, all the identified candidates are thermodynamically (meta)stable, exhibit direct (or nearest direct) band gaps that are optimal for PV applications, and are resistant to forming several types of point defects. We hope that our first principles driven workflow and the identified candidates will advance the development of novel PV materials and reinvigorate interest in the exploration of pnictides.

</details>


### [77] [Coupling of two individual magnon resonators via a superconducting microwave resonator operating in the strong coupling regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30454)
*Anoop Kamalasanan,Georg Schmidt,Seth W. Kurfman*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates coherent coupling between ferromagnetic resonance in two permalloy stripes via a superconducting coplanar waveguide resonator, using shape anisotropy (with stripes angled at 50°) to independently tune each stripe's resonance frequency by rotating the external magnetic field, achieving strong coupling without local bias coils.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable separate tuning of multiple ferromagnetic resonators coupled to a single microwave resonator without relying on local individual bias coils, thereby simplifying integration into planar device architectures.

Method: Two permalloy stripes are placed on a superconducting coplanar waveguide resonator with a 50° angle between their long axes. The external bias magnetic field is rotated in the sample plane, exploiting shape anisotropy to independently tune each stripe's ferromagnetic resonance frequency. Both ferromagnets are individually strongly coupled to the microwave resonator.

Result: Coherent coupling of ferromagnetic resonance in two individual permalloy stripes through the superconducting resonator is demonstrated, with both stripes exhibiting strong coupling to the microwave resonator.

Conclusion: The method successfully achieves independent frequency tuning of multiple coupled ferromagnetic resonators via shape anisotropy and magnetic field rotation, avoiding local bias coils and facilitating planar device integration.

Abstract: We demonstrate coherent coupling of ferromagnetic resonance in two individual permalloy stripes via a superconducting coplanar waveguide resonator. The two stripes are placed on top of the resonator with an angle of 50$^{\circ}$ between their respective long axes. This alignment enables us to separately tune their ferromagnetic resonance frequency by exploiting their shape anisotropy by simply rotating the external bias magnetic field in the sample plane. Both individual ferromagnets exhibit strong coupling with the microwave resonator. This method avoids the use of local individual bias coils and facilitates the integration of similar experiments into planar device infrastructures.

</details>


### [78] [Heterogeneous ferroelectricity and conductivity of oxidized BaTiO$_3$ crystals: the role of nanoscale phase segregation in the surface region](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30552)
*Christian Rodenbücher,Gustav Bihlmayer,Susan Trolier-McKinstry,Jacek Szade,Franciszek Krok,Kristof Szot*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper investigates thermal oxidation effects on BaTiO₃ single crystals up to 1000 °C, revealing that oxidation drives complex Ba segregation to the surface forming BaO nanocrystals, leaving TiO₂-rich depleted regions below. This creates a sandwich-like surface structure that induces electromotive force and self-polarization, screening the material's ferroelectric response. Oxidation also transforms the crystal from metallic to semiconducting behavior irreversibly, with conductivity localized to filaments along dislocations where preferential Ba and O pipe diffusion governs the electrical properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how thermal oxidation, even at moderate temperatures (≤1000 °C), modifies the surface chemistry, structure, and electrical properties of BaTiO₃ single crystals—a widely used ferroelectric material—since these segregation and conductivity changes significantly impact device performance.

Method: The study examines thermal oxidation of BaTiO₃ single crystals using nanoscale analysis to characterize surface segregation, chemical composition changes along dislocations, and correlated electrical measurements including ferroelectric response, electromotive force, self-polarization, and conductivity behavior across different oxidation/reduction states.

Result: Oxidation at moderate temperatures induces Ba diffusion to the surface forming BaO nanocrystals, while the subsurface becomes TiO₂-rich, creating a sandwich-like structure with measurable electromotive force and self-polarization. This screens ferroelectric polarization, reducing the global response. The as-received crystal is metallic, but oxidation produces semiconducting behavior that cannot be reversed by reduction. Conductivity is confined to nanoscale filaments along dislocations, whose composition changes due to preferential Ba and O pipe diffusion.

Conclusion: Thermal oxidation at even moderate temperatures causes irreversible segregation and chemical restructuring in BaTiO₃ single crystals that fundamentally alter both ferroelectric and transport properties. The metallic-to-semiconducting transition is irreversible, and dislocation-mediated pipe diffusion of Ba and O is the key mechanism controlling local chemistry and overall electrical behavior.

Abstract: We investigate the effect of thermal oxidation on BaTiO$_3$ single crystals. Our results reveal that, even at moderate temperatures of up to 1000 °C, complex segregation mechanisms occur involving the movement of Ba-rich compounds to the upper surface, where they form inhomogeneously distributed BaO nanocrystals. As a result, deeper regions of the surface layer become depleted in Ba and become TiO$_2$-rich. A sandwich-like structure evolves in the surface layer, exhibiting a measurable electromotive force and self-polarization. This, in turn, screens the polarization and diminishes the global ferroelectric response. The conductivity is also strongly influenced by the irreversible segregation effects in the surface region. An as-received crystal can be transformed into a metallic state, while oxidation induces semiconducting properties and prevents a return to the metallic state upon subsequent reduction. Nanoscale analysis demonstrates that the conductivity is channeled to filaments forming along dislocations, whose local chemical composition changes upon reduction and oxidation even at moderate temperatures due to preferential Ba and O pipe diffusion thus determining the electric behavior of the whole sample.

</details>


### [79] [Freezing of a deformable water-saturated porous medium. Part I: THM formulation, OpenGeoSys-6 implementation and benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30591)
*Tymofiy Gerasimov,Christian B. Silbermann,Dmitri Naumov,Olaf Kolditz,Haibing Shao,Thomas Nagel*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents a coupled thermo-hydro-mechanical (THM) formulation for modeling water-to-ice phase change in deformable, fully-saturated porous media, implemented in the OpenGeoSys-6 computational platform. The authors validate the formulation through carefully designed benchmark problems and identify key ingredients affecting numerical results, including soil deformation from 9% volumetric expansion during freezing. Part II (forthcoming) will extend verification to a full-scale 3D case study of shallow subsurface ice storage with borehole heat exchangers, with a teaser result already shown.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop and rigorously verify a coupled THM numerical framework capable of simulating water-to-ice phase change in deformable saturated porous media, a complex multi-physics process relevant to subsurface ice storage and related geotechnical applications. Benchmark problems are needed to critically examine formulation components and identify factors that qualitatively and quantitatively affect simulation outcomes.

Method: The authors propose a coupled thermo-hydro-mechanical formulation for water-ice phase transition in a deformable, fully-saturated porous medium, implemented in the multi-physics platform OpenGeoSys-6. They compute a series of carefully designed benchmark problems to test each formulation component, and simulate freezing-induced soil deformation resulting from 9% volumetric expansion. A preliminary three-dimensional simulation of ice growth around borehole heat exchangers is also presented as a teaser for the forthcoming Part II.

Result: The benchmark problems critically validate the formulation and implementation, and several ingredients with qualitative and quantitative impact on numerical results are identified, dissected, and commented on. Simulations successfully capture soil deformation caused by freezing-induced volumetric expansion. A snapshot of the full-scale 3D ice storage simulation around borehole heat exchangers is provided as a preliminary result.

Conclusion: The coupled THM formulation for water-to-ice phase change in deformable saturated porous media is successfully implemented and benchmarked in OpenGeoSys-6. The work lays the foundation for code verification and validation, with Part II planned to complete the process through a real full-scale 3D case study of shallow subsurface ice storage involving borehole heat exchangers.

Abstract: In this contribution, Part I, we present a coupled thermo-hydro-mechanical formulation for modeling and analyzing water-to-ice phase change in a deformable fully-saturated porous medium. It is implemented in the multi-physics computational platform OpenGeoSys-6. We compute a series of carefully designed benchmark problems which critically examine the corresponding formulation components and the overall implementation. Several ingredients that have a qualitative and quantitative impact on the numerical results are identified, particularly dissected and commented on. Simulations also account for soil deformation induced by freezing (as a result of 9% volumetric expansion caused by water-to-ice phase transition). The forthcoming Part II will complete the code verification and validation campaign by considering a real full-scale three-dimensional case study of shallow subsurface ice storage. More specifically, we simulate the ice growth process in a fully saturated soil specimen surrounding a group of borehole heat exchangers (BHEs) that contain subzero temperature coolant fluid. A snapshot of the numerical results of this task is already depicted here in Part I as a teaser.

</details>


### [80] [Layer Axial Phonons and Dipolar Thermal Response in Centrosymmetric Thin Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30600)
*Madhubanti Mukherjee,Kapil Gope,Barun Ghosh*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Centrosymmetric layered materials can host large hidden layer-resolved phonon angular momentum despite PT symmetry forbidding total phonon AM, enabling tunable phonon thermal Edelstein-like and phonon AM dipole responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While PT symmetry in nonmagnetic centrosymmetric materials enforces zero total phonon angular momentum, the authors investigate whether such materials can still support axial phonon phenomena at a layer-resolved level.

Method: First-principles calculations on BaAgAs and representative van der Waals layered materials, analyzing layer-resolved phonon AM textures with chiral and cycloidal modes, and computing phonon thermal Edelstein-like and real-space phonon AM dipole responses as functions of strain.

Result: Centrosymmetric slabs exhibit large hidden layer-resolved phonon AM with both chiral and cycloidal mode textures, yielding layer-resolved phonon thermal Edelstein-like responses and a real-space phonon AM dipole response, both tunable by strain.

Conclusion: Centrosymmetric layered materials serve as an exciting platform for hidden axial phonons and multipolar phonon thermal responses.

Abstract: In nonmagnetic centrosymmetric materials, $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry enforces vanishing total phonon angular momentum (phonon AM) at every wave vector, making them appear unsuitable as hosts of axial phonons. Here, using first-principles calculations for BaAgAs and representative van der Waal's layered materials, we show that centrosymmetric slabs can still host large hidden layer-resolved phonon AM with both chiral and cycloidal modes. This hidden phonon AM texture produces layer resolved phonon thermal Edelstein-like responses and a real-space phonon AM dipole response, both tunable by strain. Our results establish centrosymmetric layered materials as an exciting platform for hidden axial phonon and multipolar phonon thermal response.

</details>


### [81] [Topological and spin-orbit effects on orbital moments in ultra-thin magnetic films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30642)
*Felix Nickel,Soumyajyoti Haldar,Mara Gutzeit,Stefan Heinze*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses first-principles electronic structure theory to investigate the origin of orbital moments in compensated spin structures with non-trivial topology, focusing on ultrathin magnetic films such as Pd/Mn bilayers on Re(0001). By comparing the row-wise antiferromagnetic (RW-AFM) state with the triple-Q (3Q) state, the authors disentangle topological and spin-orbit contributions to orbital moments, finding that topological orbital moments (TOMs) contribute significantly in spin-compensated systems where spin-orbit induced moments nearly cancel. The trend is confirmed in atomic-scale skyrmion lattices in Fe monolayers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Topological orbital moments (TOMs) are a direct hallmark of magnetic textures with non-trivial spin topology and could enable manipulation of magnetic structures with compensated total spin moment. While indirect evidence via Hall effect transport measurements exists, a direct observation of TOMs is missing, and distinguishing topological from spin-orbit coupling contributions remains a key complication.

Method: First-principles electronic structure theory calculations are employed. The RW-AFM state (trivial topology) in a hexagonal monolayer serves as a reference to isolate spin-orbit induced orbital moments, while the 3Q state (non-trivial spin topology, superposition of three 1Q RW-AFM states) exhibits TOMs. Comparing the two electronically similar states allows disentangling topological versus spin-orbit contributions. Calculations are extended to atomic-scale skyrmion lattices in Fe monolayers on different surfaces.

Result: TOMs provide an important contribution in spin-compensated systems, as spin-orbit coupling induced orbital moments are nearly compensated (i.e., sum to approximately zero). The same general trend found for the 3Q state is reproduced in atomic-scale skyrmion lattices in Fe monolayers on different surfaces.

Conclusion: The comparison between trivial (RW-AFM) and non-trivial (3Q) spin states enables unambiguous disentangling of topological and spin-orbit contributions to orbital moments. TOMs dominate in spin-compensated systems where spin-orbit contributions cancel, establishing ultrathin films like Pd/Mn on Re(0001) as ideal model systems for direct experimental detection of TOMs via locally-resolved techniques such as spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy.

Abstract: Topological orbital moments (TOMs) are a direct hallmark of a magnetic texture with a non-trivial spin topology. In addition to giving insight into the topology of the magnetic texture, TOMs could also be used to manipulate magnetic structures with a compensated total spin moment. Experimental evidence of TOMs has been provided via transport measurements of the Hall effect in intercalated van-der-Waals materials. However, a direct observation of TOMs is still missing. Another complication arises for the unambiguous proof of the topological origin since orbital moments can also occur due to spin-orbit coupling. Here, we use first-principles electronic structure theory to investigate the origin of orbital moments in different compensated spin structures with a non-trivial topology. We focus on ultrathin magnetic films at surfaces such as Pd/Mn bilayers on Re(0001) which represent ideal model systems for the detection of TOMs since it is possible to apply experimental techniques with local resolution of magnetic properties such as spin-polarized scanning tunneling microscopy.
  Due to its trivial topology we use the row-wise antiferromagnetic (RW-AFM) state in a hexagonal monolayer to analyze the spin-orbit induced contributions. The triple-Q (3Q) state is a superposition state of three RW-AFM (1Q) states and thus electronically similar, however, due to its non-trivial spin topology it exhibits TOMs. The comparison between these two spin states allows us to disentangle the topological and spin-orbit contributions to the orbital moments. We find that TOMs have an important contribution in spin-compensated systems, since the spin-orbit coupling induced orbital moments are nearly compensated. First-principles calculations for atomic-scale skyrmion lattices in Fe monolayers on different surfaces exhibit the same general trend found for the 3Q state.

</details>


### [82] [Terahertz Control of Optical Second-Harmonic Generation in Displacive Ferroelectrics: Electronic Bloch-State Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30665)
*Hong-Kui Liu,Zi-Chen Qin,Jun-Song Wu,Yue Yuan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a microscopic theory connecting terahertz-driven polar lattice distortions to optical second-harmonic generation (SHG) in displacive ferroelectrics, showing that the electronic SHG susceptibility is linear in the polar distortion (χ⁽²⁾∝Q) with SHG intensity quadratic in the order parameter, and that THz-coherent driving of the polar mode dynamically modulates the SHG response as I₂ω(t)∝|Q(t)|².


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Optical SHG is a standard probe of ferroelectric order but its microscopic origin is typically understood only via symmetry arguments, not the underlying electronic processes. The authors aim to establish a direct microscopic connection between THz-driven polar lattice dynamics and the resulting electronic nonlinear optical response, especially in insulating ferroelectrics where low-energy carrier dynamics are absent.

Method: Starting from a complete Bloch-band representation, the authors show that an inversion-breaking lattice distortion reconstructs electronic Bloch wave functions and modifies optical dipole matrix elements, activating nonlinear optical pathways forbidden in centrosymmetric structures. They derive the second-order susceptibility from distortion-induced reconstruction of electronic states and transition matrix elements, and analyze the time-dependent case where a THz electric field coherently drives the polar mode.

Result: The electronic SHG susceptibility is linear in the polar distortion, χ⁽²⁾(Q)∝Q, giving an SHG intensity quadratic in the inversion-breaking order parameter. Under coherent THz driving of the polar mode, the time-dependent lattice distortion modulates SHG as I₂ω(t)∝|Q(t)|² to leading order. The framework cleanly separates THz-driven lattice dynamics from the electronic interband processes responsible for SHG.

Conclusion: The theory provides a microscopic bridge between nonequilibrium polar lattice dynamics and ultrafast electronic nonlinear optics, enabling a deeper understanding of how THz control of lattice distortions translates into tunable nonlinear optical responses in insulating ferroelectrics.

Abstract: Optical second-harmonic generation (SHG) is a powerful probe of ferroelectric order, yet its microscopic origin and dynamical control are often understood primarily from symmetry considerations rather than from the underlying electronic processes. Here, we develop a microscopic theory of terahertz-controlled optical SHG in displacive ferroelectrics, establishing a direct connection between THz-driven polar lattice distortions and the resulting electronic nonlinear optical response. Starting from a complete Bloch-band representation, we show that an inversion-breaking lattice distortion reconstructs electronic Bloch wave functions, modifies optical dipole matrix elements, and activates nonlinear optical pathways that are forbidden in the centrosymmetric structure. We derive the second-order susceptibility in terms of the distortion-induced reconstruction of electronic states and optical transition matrix elements, and demonstrate that the electronic SHG susceptibility is linear in the polar distortion, $χ^{(2)}({\bf Q})\propto{\bf Q}$, leading to an SHG intensity quadratic in the inversion-breaking order parameter. When the polar mode is coherently driven by a terahertz electric field, the resulting time-dependent lattice distortion dynamically reconstructs the electronic states and thereby modulates the optical SHG response, with $I_{2ω}(t)\propto|{\bf Q}(t)|^2$ to leading order. This framework distinguishes the THz-driven lattice dynamics from the electronic interband processes responsible for optical SHG, which is particularly important in insulating ferroelectrics where low-energy carrier dynamics are absent. Our theory thus provides a microscopic bridge between nonequilibrium polar lattice dynamics and ultrafast electronic nonlinear optics.

</details>


### [83] [Halide donors in monoclinic- and corundum-phase Ga$_2$O$_3$ and Al$_2$O$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30680)
*Sai Mu,Haochen Wang,Yongjoong Shin,Zhi-Hao Wang,Chris G. Van de Walle*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A first-principles study of F and Cl halide impurities in Ga2O3 and Al2O3 (monoclinic and corundum phases) reveals that F and Cl on oxygen sites act as shallow donors in Ga2O3 but exhibit different DX-center formation behavior as Al composition increases. Cl is more resistant to DX formation and becomes a promising donor candidate in (AlxGa1-x)2O3 alloys and even pure Al2O3, though high formation energies and compensation limit true n-type conductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the donor behavior and stability of halide impurities (F and Cl) in Ga2O3 and Al2O3, and to evaluate their potential as n-type dopants in (AlxGa1-x)2O3 alloys, particularly as the band gap increases with Al composition.

Method: Systematic first-principles calculations investigating structural properties, formation energies, and charge-state transition levels of F and Cl impurities (both substitutional on oxygen sites and interstitial) in monoclinic and corundum phases of Ga2O3 and Al2O3.

Result: Both F and Cl on oxygen sites are shallow donors in Ga2O3. F shows DX-center formation at 38% Al in monoclinic and 70% Al in corundum. Cl is more resistant: DX onset at 50% Al in monoclinic and 84% Al in corundum. Interstitials act as compensating centers with low migration barriers enabling post-growth annealing removal. In Al2O3, ClO exhibits a remarkably shallow transition level at ~0.48 eV below the CBM.

Conclusion: Cl is an unusually promising donor candidate in (AlxGa1-x)2O3 alloys and pure Al2O3 due to its resistance to DX-center formation and surprisingly shallow donor level, though high formation energies and compensation make true n-type conductivity in Al2O3 challenging to achieve.

Abstract: We present a systematic first-principles investigation of halide impurities (F and Cl) in Ga$_2$O$_3$ and Al$_2$O$_3$, considering both monoclinic and corundum phases. Our study of the structural properties, formation energies, and charge-state transition levels establishes the relative stability of different atomic configurations and charge states. We find that F and Cl on oxygen sites act as shallow donors in Ga$_2$O$_3$ in both the monoclinic and corundum phases. However, their behavior differs substantially as the band gap increases with greater Al compositions. Fluorine is prone to $DX$-center formation with increased Al composition, leading to self-compensation at 38% Al concentration in monoclinic (Al$_x$Ga$_{1-x}$)$_2$O$_3$ and 70% Al concentration in corundum (Al$_x$Ga$_{1-x}$)$_2$O$_3$. Chlorine is more resistant to $DX$-center formation: in monoclinic (Al$_x$Ga$_{1-x}$)$_2$O$_3$, Cl$_\mathrm{O}$ on the lowest-energy oxygen site shows an onset of $DX$ behavior at 50% alloy composition, while in corundum (Al$_x$Ga$_{1-x}$)$_2$O$_3$ this onset for Cl$_\mathrm{O}$ occurs only at Al concentrations as high as 84%. We also study F and Cl interstitials, finding that they act as compensating centers but also exhibit migration barriers that are low enough for them to be removed by post-growth annealing. Surprisingly, in both monoclinic and corundum Al$_2$O$_3$, Cl$_\mathrm{O}$ exhibits a relatively shallow transition level located at $\sim$0.48 eV below the conduction-band minimum, much shallower than F$_\mathrm{O}$ and other donor candidates. These remarkable results identify Cl as an unusually promising donor candidate in (Al$_x$Ga$_{1-x}$)$_2$O$_3$ alloys and even pure Al$_2$O$_3$, although high formation energies and compensation will render observation of true $n$-type conductivity in Al$_2$O$_3$ difficult.

</details>


### [84] [Universal unconventional responses controlled by ferroaxial order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30706)
*Zhiren He,Guru Khalsa,Jagoda Slawinska*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper theoretically establishes that ferroaxial order universally generates unconventional components of responses — including spin Hall, magneto-Seebeck, and Faraday effects — across linear and nonlinear transport, optical, and equilibrium phenomena, providing probes for detecting ferroaxial domains and revealing a new class of ferroaxial metals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ferroaxial materials are promising for nonvolatile applications due to robustness against stray fields, but detecting the ferroaxial state is challenging since it does not manifest through measurable macroscopic polarization or magnetization, and existing probes are mostly limited to optical methods.

Method: The authors theoretically establish a general connection between ferroaxial order and unconventional components of external responses, and perform first-principles calculations for representative materials to demonstrate these responses in ferroaxial metals.

Result: Ferroaxial order universally generates unconventional response components (spin Hall, magneto-Seebeck, Faraday effects) across a broad range of phenomena, and these responses are directly coupled to ferroaxial order, enabling domain distinction. First-principles calculations confirm this for ferroaxial metals, revealing a largely unexplored material class.

Conclusion: Ferroaxiality primarily manifests through unconventional material responses rather than directly measurable order parameters, representing a fundamentally different way for ferroic order to appear and broadening possibilities for nonvolatile ferroic control and flexible device design.

Abstract: Ferroaxial materials are an emerging class of ferroic materials that, in contrast to ferromagnets and ferroelectrics, are fully robust against stray fields, making them ideally suited for data storage and other nonvolatile applications. However, detection of the ferroaxial state remains challenging, as ferroaxiality does not manifest directly through a measurable macroscopic electric polarization or magnetization, and probes are mostly limited to optical methods. Here, we theoretically establish that ferroaxial order universally generates unconventional components of responses to external stimuli, such as the spin Hall effect, magneto-Seebeck effect, or Faraday effect, across a broad range of linear and nonlinear transport, optical and equilibrium phenomena. These unconventional responses are always directly coupled to ferroaxial order and can distinguish ferroaxial domains, providing probes of ferroaxial order. This general connection also reveals a largely unexplored class of ferroaxial metals, in which unconventional transport provides a natural probe of ferroaxial order, as demonstrated by our first-principles calculations for representative materials. Our results reveal a fundamentally different way for ferroic order to manifest -- ferroaxiality primarily shows unconventional material responses rather than the directly measurable order parameters, broadening the possibilities of nonvolatile ferroic control and flexible device design.

</details>


### [85] [Fine structure of the M-center in Si](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30790)
*Aurora Teien,David R. Gongora,Arnulf Johannes Snedker-Nielsen,Viktor Bobal,Augustinas Galeckas,Peter Granum,Stefano Paesani,Marianne Etzelmüller Bathen,Lasse Vines*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper investigates photoluminescence lines near the M-center in silicon, a color center with potential for scalable quantum technologies. Three blue-shifted lines (1.1, 2.8, 3.6 meV relative to the 761 meV zero phonon line) and two red-shifted lines (4.7 and 6.4 meV) are characterized. The 2.8 meV blue-shifted line shows negative thermal quenching and is attributed to a second excited state of the M-center, while the remaining lines exhibit normal thermal quenching and are unaffected by carbon isotope and implantation fluence, suggesting they arise from other defects or perturbed M-center configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To better understand the M-center in silicon—a promising color center for scalable quantum technologies with telecom-band emission and paramagnetic ground state—by characterizing nearby photoluminescence lines and their physical origins, which is essential for accurately interpreting quantum optical measurements and defect identification.

Method: Photoluminescence spectroscopy was performed on silicon samples containing M-centers. The lines near the M-center were characterized by varying temperature, implantation fluence, carbon isotope (12C vs 13C), and annealing temperature. Emission line shifts relative to the 761 meV zero phonon line of the M-center were measured, and thermal quenching behaviors were analyzed.

Result: Three blue-shifted emission lines at 1.1, 2.8, and 3.6 meV relative to the M-center zero phonon line were observed, plus two red-shifted lines at 4.7 and 6.4 meV. The 2.8 meV line exhibits negative thermal quenching and is proposed to arise from a second excited state of the M-center. The other lines show normal thermal quenching and are insensitive to carbon isotope shifts and implantation fluence, implying they originate from other defects with similar energies or perturbed M-center configurations.

Conclusion: The line at 2.8 meV above the M-center zero phonon line is identified as originating from a second excited state of the M-center. The other observed nearby lines are most likely not intrinsic M-center transitions, but instead arise either from distinct defects with similar emission energies or from perturbed configurations of the M-center.

Abstract: Color centers in silicon offer great possibilities for scalable quantum technologies. The M-center, proposed to originate from a carbon-hydrogen complex, offers telecommunications-band emission and a paramagnetic ground state similar to the T-center. Here, we report on photoluminescence lines in the vicinity of the M-center and investigate their properties, including their dependence on temperature, implantation fluence, implantation isotope, and annealing temperature. Three emission lines are observed that are blue-shifted by 1.1, 2.8 and 3.6 meV relative to the 761 meV zero phonon line of the M center, where the 2.8 meV line exhibits negative thermal quenching and is therefore proposed to originate from a second excited state of the M-center. The remaining blue-shifted emission lines, together with two additional red-shifted (4.7 and 6.4 meV) emission lines display normal thermal quenching, and are unaffected by an isotope shift of the implanted carbon atom ($^{12}$C versus $^{13}$C implantation) and implantation fluence. Thus, they likely arise either from other defects with similar emission energies or from a perturbed configuration of the M-center.

</details>


### [86] [Role of self-coherence in single-electron phase contrast imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30833)
*Christian Kisielowski,Petra Specht,Joerg Jinschek,Stig Helveg*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper extends coherent lattice contrast into the energy-loss region in HRTEM by describing electron-sample interaction as pulse-like in the energy/time uncertainty limit, generating a self-coherence wave packet in coherent-inelastic scattering. A Rayleigh-like transfer factor P(ls, a) limits interference visibility, verified by energy-filtered HRTEM images of hexagonal BN, identifying energy-loss-induced phase noise as a distinct single-electron visibility limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify the visibility limits of lattice contrast in HRTEM when imaging with inelastically scattered (energy-loss) electrons, and to distinguish this single-electron quantum decoherence effect from other resolution-limiting factors such as ensemble-coherence or counting statistics.

Method: The authors model the coherent-inelastic scattering event as a pulse-like electron-sample interaction in the energy/time uncertainty limit, which generates a wave packet via electron self-interference. They derive a self-coherence length ls(ΔE) based on an intrinsic decoherence phase of ~1 radian, and define a Rayleigh-like transfer factor P(ls, a) for crystalline samples. The model is verified using energy-filtered HRTEM images of hexagonal boron nitride.

Result: The model successfully predicts the visibility of interference contrast from hexagonal BN in energy-filtered HRTEM. It demonstrates that energy-loss-induced phase noise constitutes a single-electron visibility limit that is distinct from resolution limitations caused by ensemble-coherence or counting-statistical noise.

Conclusion: Energy-loss-induced phase noise sets a fundamental, predictable single-electron limit on lattice contrast visibility in HRTEM, characterized by the self-coherence length and the transfer factor P(ls, a), which is separate from and additional to conventional resolution-limiting mechanisms.

Abstract: The extension of coherent lattice contrast into the energy loss region in high-resolution transmission electron microscopy (HRTEM) is described by a pulse-like electron-sample interaction in the energy/time uncertainty limit. It generates a wave packet by electron self-interference in any coherent-inelastic scattering event with energy loss. The width of this wave packet is characterized by a self-coherence length ls(ΔE) that is predictable because an intrinsic decoherence phase around one radian is set by the expectation value for phase fluctuations. In this case the visibility of interference contrast from a crystalline sample with lattice parameter a is limited by a Rayleigh-like transfer factor P(ls, a) in the self-coherently illuminated sample area. The model is verified by energy-filtered HRTEM images of hexagonal BN and identifies energy-loss-induced phase noise as a single-electron visibility limit distinct from resolution limitations caused by ensemble-coherence or counting-statistical noise.

</details>


### [87] [Stoner contributions to the magnon equation of motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30931)
*Thorbjørn Skovhus,Patrik Thunström*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper derives a Landau-damped magnon quasi-particle equation of motion from linear response theory, without requiring spin-orbit coupling. The equation yields a natural definition of exchange interaction as the restoring force near equilibrium, and includes damping and dispersive mass terms from electron-magnon coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a rigorous, minimal-assumption derivation of a magnon equation of motion directly from linear response theory, enabling a natural microscopic definition of exchange interactions and magnon damping/lifetime effects, particularly relevant for itinerant ferromagnets.

Method: Starting from linear response theory, the derivation assumes (1) the transverse magnetic susceptibility is diagonalized by one collective eigenmode per magnetic atom in the unit cell, and (2) each collective eigenmode spectrum is dominated by a single magnon resonance. The resulting equation of motion is analyzed, and wave-vector independence of the collective magnon subspace is invoked to reduce the equation to an atomistic form.

Result: The magnon equation of motion contains exchange (restoring force), a damping term, and a dispersive quasi-particle mass from electron-magnon coupling. For a prototypical itinerant ferromagnet, these terms redshift the magnon dispersion and reduce magnon lifetime as magnons enter the Stoner continuum. Magnon coupling constants can be approximated from band theory for the dynamic transverse magnetic susceptibility.

Conclusion: The derived equation of motion provides a general, first-principles-compatible framework for describing Landau-damped magnons, bridging linear response theory and atomistic magnon models, with direct applicability to realistic itinerant magnets.

Abstract: From linear response theory, we derive the equation of motion for Landau-damped magnon quasi-particles in absence of spin-orbit coupling. The derivation is based on a minimal set of assumptions, namely that the transverse magnetic susceptibility is diagonalized by one collective eigenmode per magnetic atom in the unit cell, and that the spectrum of each collective eigenmode is dominated by a single magnon resonance. The resulting equation of motion leads to a natural definition of the exchange interaction as the restoring force acting on the collective mode magnetization near equilibrium. In addition to magnetic exchange, the magnon equation of motion also contains a damping term and a dispersive quasi-particle mass, both originating from the electron-magnon coupling. The effect of these terms is illustrated for a prototypical itinerant ferromagnet, where they redshift the magnon dispersion and reduce the magnon lifetime as the magnon enters the Stoner continuum. By further assuming wave vector independence of the collective magnon subspace, the magnon equation of motion becomes atomistic with well defined magnetic sites. For real materials, one can approximate the magnon coupling constants using an arbitrary level of band theory for the dynamic transverse magnetic susceptibility.

</details>


### [88] [A MOF-reinforced self-foaming sponge for mechanically robust triboelectric membranes with improved resistance to humidity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31019)
*Tianhuai Xu,Pavel Kulyabin,Fatih Uzun,Alejandra Sophia Lozano-Pérez,Ketan Pancholi,Amit Kumar,Jin-Chong Tan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work develops a bio-derived polyamide with disulfide linkages that spontaneously forms a porous dielectric, reinforced with hydrophilic HKUST-1 MOF fillers. The MOF improves mechanical robustness by suppressing pore collapse and enhances humidity stability by confining water molecules, yielding durable and environmentally stable triboelectric nanogenerators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Porous triboelectric materials can enhance triboelectric nanogenerator performance but suffer from structural instability and humidity-induced performance degradation, limiting their practical application.

Method: A sustainable bio-derived polyamide containing disulfide linkages was developed for spontaneous porous dielectric formation without external templating. Hydrophilic HKUST-1 MOF crystals were incorporated into the porous matrix to reinforce the structure and regulate moisture effects. Mechanical characterization assessed pore collapse resistance and stress-strain behavior.

Result: HKUST-1 suppressed pore collapse and improved structural robustness under repeated deformation. It also mitigated humidity-induced charge dissipation by confining water molecules within its framework, giving enhanced triboelectric output stability and reduced performance degradation at increasing relative humidity compared to the neat porous system.

Conclusion: Integrating self-foamed porous structures, sustainable polymer design, and functional filler reinforcement provides a strategy to engineer mechanically robust and environmentally stable triboelectric systems.

Abstract: Porous triboelectric materials offer significant potential for enhancing the performance of triboelectric nanogenerators, yet their practical application is limited by structural instability and humidity-induced performance degradation. In this work, a bio-derived, sustainable polyamide containing disulfide linkages was developed to enable spontaneous formation of a porous dielectric without external templating. Hydrophilic MOF fillers comprising HKUST-1 crystals are incorporated within the porous matrix to reinforce the membrane structure and regulate moisture effects. Mechanical characterization demonstrates that HKUST-1 suppresses pore collapse and improves structural robustness under repeated deformation, while analysis of stress-strain behaviour reveals the critical role of pore stability in achieving stable triboelectric output. In addition, HKUST-1 mitigates humidity-induced charge dissipation by confining water molecules within its framework, giving enhanced triboelectric output stability and reduced performance degradation under increasing relative humidity compared to the neat porous system. This work demonstrates a strategy that integrates self-foamed porous structures, sustainable polymer design, and functional filler reinforcement to engineer mechanically robust and environmentally stable triboelectric systems.

</details>


### [89] [Orbital and Spin Edelstein Effects in KTaO$_3$(110) Two-Dimensional Electron Gases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31051)
*Hugo Witt,Aravind Raji,Srijani Mallik,Börge Göbel,Luis M. Vicente-Arche,Sara Varotto,Julien Bréhin,Gerbold Ménard,Raphaël Salazar,Julien Rault,François Bertran,Patrick Le Fèvre,Isabella Boventer,Ingrid Mertig,Agnès Barthélémy,Alexandre Gloter,Annika Johansson,Nicolas Bergeal,Manuel Bibes*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper predicts and experimentally evidences an orbital Edelstein effect in KTaO3(110) two-dimensional electron gases that largely counterbalances its spin counterpart, establishing it as a model platform for orbitronics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although signatures of the orbital Edelstein effect have been reported, its microscopic mechanisms and quantitative validation remain underexplored. The authors aim to directly link atomic structure to electron band dispersions to provide quantitative validation of the orbital Edelstein effect and its role in counterbalancing the spin response.

Method: The authors use scanning transmission electron microscopy and electron energy-loss spectroscopy to resolve the interfacial atomic configuration of KTaO3(110) two-dimensional electron gases. This structure serves as input for density-functional theory calculations, validated by angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy. The resulting band structure is fitted with a tight-binding model to compute spin and orbital Edelstein responses, and harmonic magnetotransport measurements assess the relative contributions of each.

Result: The study predicts and provides experimental evidence for an orbital Edelstein effect that largely counterbalances its spin counterpart. Harmonic magnetotransport indicates that a ~20% contribution from the orbital Edelstein response is necessary to describe the magnitude and anisotropy of the observed effect.

Conclusion: The results establish KTaO3(110) as a model platform for orbitronics, demonstrate a pathway to generate and harness orbital polarization in quantum oxide systems, and offer new insights into pairing mechanisms in their superconducting state.

Abstract: The orbital Edelstein effect converts an electric field into a non-equilibrium orbital polarization, opening new opportunities for orbitronics. Although signatures of the orbital Edelstein effect have been reported, its microscopic mechanisms and quantitative validation remain underexplored. Here, by directly linking the atomic structure of KTaO$_3$(110) two-dimensional electron gases to both their calculated and measured electronic band dispersions, we predict and provide experimental evidence for an orbital Edelstein effect that largely counterbalances its spin counterpart. Scanning transmission electron microscopy and electron energy-loss spectroscopy resolve the interfacial atomic configuration, which is used as input for density-functional calculations. Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy then confirms the resulting band structure, which is fitted by a tight-binding model enabling computation of the spin and orbital Edelstein responses. Harmonic magnetotransport indicates that a $\sim$20 \% contribution from the orbital Edelstein response is necessary to describe the magnitude and anisotropy of the effect. Our results establish KTaO$_3$(110) as a model platform for orbitronics and demonstrate a pathway to generate and harness orbital polarization in quantum oxide systems while also offering new insights into pairing mechanisms in their superconducting state.

</details>


### [90] [Strain-Tunable Spin Relaxation in Germanium from First Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31169)
*Lauren A. Tan,Shaelyn Iyer,Ivan Maliyov,Jinsoo Park,Marco Bernardi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents first-principles predictions of electronic transport and spin relaxation in bulk germanium using hybrid-functional band structures and fully relativistic electron-phonon interactions, achieving agreement with experiments from 100–400 K without empirical parameters. It identifies distinct microscopic mechanisms for spin vs. momentum relaxation and shows that compressive biaxial strain can enhance hole spin lifetimes by up to two orders of magnitude at 5% strain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Germanium is a leading platform for semiconductor spin qubits and spintronics, but its electron and hole spin dynamics are currently understood only through phenomenological models. A fully first-principles understanding is needed to accurately predict and engineer spin properties.

Method: The authors combine hybrid-functional band structures with fully relativistic electron-phonon (e-ph) interactions to predict electronic transport and spin relaxation in bulk Ge entirely from first principles, with no empirical parameters. They resolve spin-flip scattering by valley and phonon mode and analyze the effect of compressive biaxial strain on hole spin lifetimes.

Result: The first-principles approach predicts carrier mobilities, velocity-field curves, and electron and hole spin relaxation times in close agreement with experiments over 100–400 K. They identify that spin and momentum relaxation, though both mediated by e-ph scattering, are controlled by distinct processes. Compressive biaxial strain enhances hole spin lifetime by up to two orders of magnitude at 5% strain via strain-induced valence-band splitting and suppressed spin mixing.

Conclusion: Strain engineering, particularly compressive biaxial strain in Ge-on-Si devices, provides a practical route to achieving long-lived hole spins for quantum technologies, with ab initio predictions providing a validated, parameter-free framework for understanding and optimizing spin dynamics in germanium.

Abstract: Germanium is a leading platform for semiconductor spin qubits and spintronics. Yet its electron and hole spin dynamics remain understood primarily through phenomenological models. Here, we predict electronic transport and spin relaxation in bulk Ge entirely from first principles, combining hybrid-functional band structures with fully relativistic electron-phonon ($e$-ph) interactions. Without empirical parameters, we predict carrier mobilities, velocity-field curves, and electron and hole spin relaxation times in close agreement with experiments over 100$-$400 K. By resolving spin-flip scattering by valley and phonon mode, we identify the microscopic mechanisms governing spin relaxation and show that spin and momentum relaxation, although both mediated by $e$-ph scattering, are controlled by distinct processes. We further show that compressive biaxial strain enhances the hole spin lifetime by up to two orders of magnitude at 5% strain, through strain-induced valence-band splitting and suppressed spin mixing. This mechanism is directly relevant to Ge-on-Si devices, where strain provides a practical route to engineering long-lived hole spins for quantum technologies.

</details>
