Method: The study employs absolute calibration of electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) absorption to quantify the fraction of EPR-active copper spins, combined with comparison of EPR absorption against static magnetic susceptibility measurements at various temperatures.

Result: Only a fraction of copper spins in nabokoites is EPR-active, and this EPR-active fraction orders at the Néel point. The EPR-silent spin subsystem's contribution to total magnetic susceptibility decreases upon cooling, indicating the presence of two nearly decoupled spin subsystems.

Conclusion: The coexistence of magnetic order in the EPR-active subsystem and possible spin-liquid dynamics in the EPR-silent subsystem within a single compound demonstrates a dramatic splitting of the exchange network, challenging unified-exchange-network models for decorated frustrated lattices.

Abstract: The square kagomé lattice (SKL) offers a model platform for investigating geometric frustration in 2D systems. Nabokoite-family compounds \nabok{A}{X} (A=Na, K, Cs, Rb and X=Cl, Br) extend this physics to a 3D network, where 2D SKL layers are decorated by interlayer spins. Using electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR), we demonstrate a dramatic splitting of this complex exchange network into two virtually decoupled spin subsystems: absolute calibration of the electron paramagnetic resonance (EPR) absorption reveals that only a fraction of all copper spins in nabokoites is EPR-active and this fraction of the spins orders at the Néel point. Comparison of the EPR absorption and static susceptibility indicates that contribution of the EPR-silent spin subsystem to total magnetic susceptibility decreases on cooling. This direct observation of coexisting magnetic order and possible spin-liquid dynamics within a single compound challenges conventional models of unified exchange networks in decorated frustrated lattices.

</details>


### [115] [Collinear and noncollinear antiferromagnetic ordering in a highly frustrated decorated square kagomé lattice antiferromagnets of the nabokoite family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29330)
*V. N. Glazkov,Ya V. Rebrov,M. A. Dubovitskii,M. M. Markina,K. V. Zakharov,A. F. Murtazoev,P. S. Berdonosov,A. N. Vasiliev*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper investigates magnetic ordering in nabokoite family compounds ACu7(TeO4)(SO4)5X (A=Na, K, Rb, Cs; X=Cl, Br) using ESR spectroscopy and thermodynamic measurements. It reveals that light-alkali-ion (K, Na) and heavy-alkali-ion (Rb, Cs) compounds exhibit qualitatively different low-temperature ground states: heavy-alkali compounds order in conventional collinear antiferromagnetic order with easy-axis anisotropy, while light-alkali compounds undergo a ferroelectric transition (25-90K) that lifts frustration, followed by a two-step transition to an unusual noncollinear magnetic order with three zero-field magnon gaps and two spin-reorientation fields. Cluster modeling suggests the proximity of exchange parameters to a quantum phase boundary in the pyramidal building block determines the choice of ground state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the magnetic ordering and frustration in nabokoite family compounds, which host frustrated 2D square kagomé lattice layers with additional inter-layer magnetic ions, and to elucidate why different alkali-ion substitutions lead to qualitatively different ordered ground states.

Method: Multi-frequency electron spin resonance (ESR) spectroscopy, thermodynamic measurements (specific heat, magnetization, dielectric permittivity), and finite-size cluster modeling of the pyramidal structural block.

Result: Heavy-alkali-ion nabokoites (Rb, Cs) order in conventional collinear antiferromagnetic state with easy-axis anisotropy, with very similar parameter values across the subfamily. Light-alkali-ion nabokoites (K, Na) show a ferroelectric transition at 25-90K that lifts frustration, then undergo a two-step magnetic transition (Tc1~5-6K, Tc2~3-4K) into an unusual noncollinear magnetic order, evidenced by non-Larmor antiferromagnetic resonance. Light-alkali compounds exhibit three zero-field magnon gaps and two spin-reorientation fields, with values varying significantly between compounds. Cluster modeling indicates exchange parameters near a quantum ground-state boundary of the pyramidal block may determine the choice of ordered state.

Conclusion: The choice of low-temperature magnetic ground state in nabokoites depends critically on the alkali-ion species: heavy alkali ions stabilize conventional collinear antiferromagnetism, while light alkali ions enable a complex route through ferroelectric transition and two-step noncollinear magnetic ordering. The proximity of exchange coupling parameters to a quantum phase boundary in the pyramidal building block is proposed as the key factor governing this qualitatively different behavior.

Abstract: Nabokoite family compounds ACu$_7$(TeO$_4$)(SO4)$_5$X (A=Na, K, Rb, Cs; X=Cl, Br) host frustrated 2D square kagom'{e} lattice layers decorated by additional inter-layer magnetic ions. We study magnetic order in nabokoites with multi-frequency electron spin resonance spectroscopy and thermodynamic measurement (specific heat, magnetization and dielectric permittivity). Our study reveals that the choice of the low-temperature ground state is qualitatively different in light-alkali-ion (K, Na) and heavy-alkali-ion (Rb, Cs) compounds. Heavy-alkali-ion nabokoites order in conventional collinear antiferromagnetic pattern with easy-axis anisotropy. The parameters of the ordered antiferromagnetic state are very close for all heavy-alkali-ion subfamily. Light-alkali-ion members of nabokoite family demonstrate much more complicated route to the ordered state: firstly, a ferroelectric transition at 25-90K lifts the frustration and thus pre-cooks the low-temperature ordering; secondly, an unusual noncollinear magnetic order develops via two-step phase transition with first transition temperature $T_{c1}\simeq 5-6$K and the second transition at $T_{c2}\simeq 3-4$K. Noncollinear order is evidenced by observation of characteristic non-Larmor antiferromagnetic resonance mode. Spin dynamics of light-alkali-ion nabokoites is characterized by three zero-field magnon gaps and two spin-reorientation fields, the values of magnon gaps and critical fields are quite different for different compounds. The finite-size cluster modeling of pyramidal structural block of nabokoite structure combined suggests that the critical closeness of the nabokoite exchange coupling parameters to the border-line between the different quantum ground state of pyramidal building block of nabokoite structure could be the clue to the choice of qualitatively different ordered state in light- and heavy-alkali-ion nabokoites.

</details>


### [116] [Magnetic Field-Tunable Repulsive Exciton-Exciton Interaction in the van der Waals Antiferromagnet NiPS$_3$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29701)
*Kaiyang Huang,Jaena Park,Zhuo Yang,Je-Geun Park,Atsuhiko Miyata,Yoshimitsu Kohama,Yasuhiro H. Matsuda*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Ultrahigh magnetic fields (up to 178 T) induce pronounced mutual repulsion between two ultra-narrow exciton peaks near 1.5 eV in NiPS₃, accompanied by oscillator strength redistribution, while the band edge remains unchanged. A minimal two-level interaction model semi-quantitatively captures the data, revealing magnetic-field-tunable exciton-exciton coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether ultrahigh magnetic fields modify interaction-driven hybridization between two spin-orbital-entangled exciton peaks in the van der Waals crystal NiPS₃, which exhibits strong spin-dependent optical character.

Method: Systematic magneto-optical measurements of NiPS₃ in pulsed magnetic fields up to 178 T, combined with a minimal two-level interaction model compared semi-quantitatively against experimental data.

Result: Observation of pronounced mutual repulsion between the two sharp exciton peaks with redistribution of oscillator strength, no detectable band-edge shift, and successful semi-quantitative description by a two-level interaction model.

Conclusion: Magnetic-field-tunable exciton-exciton coupling dominates the high-field response of NiPS₃, establishing it as a new experimental platform for strongly correlated exciton physics in magnetic van der Waals insulators.

Abstract: Two ultra-narrow absorption peaks around 1.5 eV, which are widely believed to originate from a transition from a spin-orbital entangled triplet to a singlet state, in the two-dimensional van der Waals crystal NiPS$_3$, have attracted considerable attention because of their pronounced spin-dependent character. An interesting question is whether ultrahigh magnetic fields modify an interaction-driven hybridization between those two peaks. In this work we perform systematic magneto-optical measurements of NiPS$_3$ in pulsed magnetic fields of up to 178 T and observe a pronounced mutual repulsion between the two sharp exciton peaks accompanied by a redistribution of oscillator strength, while the band edge shows no detectable field-induced shift within our experimental resolution. We construct a minimal two-level interaction model and compare it semi-quantitatively with the experimental data. Our results reveal a magnetic-field-tunable exciton-exciton coupling as the dominant high-field response of NiPS$_3$, and clarify this material as a new experimental platform for exploring strongly correlated exciton physics in magnetic van der Waals insulators.

</details>


### [117] [Emergent Pair Density Wave and Incoherent Metallic State in a Strongly Correlated Doped System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29742)
*Soham Maiti,Nandan Pakhira,A. Taraphder*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using a slave-rotor mean-field framework, the paper shows that pair-density-wave (PDW) order dynamically emerges in the coexistence region of antiferromagnetism (AFM) and d-wave superconductivity (SC). The AFM-SC coexistence region shifts significantly with strong correlations, and the analysis reveals a crossover from coherent to incoherent metal behavior characterized by the rotor condensate amplitude φ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the dynamical emergence of states driven by the interplay between antiferromagnetism and d-wave superconductivity, particularly capturing strong-correlation effects that go beyond conventional mean-field approaches.

Method: Slave-rotor mean-field framework in which the electron is decomposed into charge (rotor) and spin (spinon) degrees of freedom, enabling treatment of strong correlations beyond standard mean-field theory.

Result: (1) PDW order emerges dynamically in the AFM-SC coexistence region; (2) The coexistence region is significantly modified by correlations, shifting the tetracritical point systematically; (3) A crossover from coherent to incoherent metal is observed via the rotor condensate amplitude φ; (4) In the AFM+(φ≠0) regime, coherent quasiparticle bands coexist with incoherent Hubbard-like excitations, while only incoherent spectral features survive in the AFM+(φ=0) regime; (5) The SC phase exhibits nodal quasiparticles consistent with d-wave pairing.

Conclusion: The slave-rotor approach reveals that strong electron correlations fundamentally alter the AFM-SC phase diagram, leading to emergent PDW order and a rich crossover from coherent to incoherent metallic behavior, providing new insights into the interplay of competing orders in strongly correlated electron systems.

Abstract: We investigate the dynamical emergence of a state driven by the interplay between antiferromagnetism (AFM) and singlet $d$-wave superconductivity (SC) within a slave-rotor mean-field framework. By decomposing the electron into charge and spin degrees of freedom, the formalism captures strong-correlation effects beyond conventional mean-field approaches. A PDW order is found to emerge dynamically in the coexistence region of AFM and SC. The AFM-SC coexistence region is significantly modified with correlation, leading to a systematic shift of the tetra-critical point. The doping and temperature evolution of the AFM, SC, and PDW order parameters, together with the rotor condensate amplitude $φ$, which characterizes charge coherence, shows a crossover from a coherent to an incoherent metal with the suppression of coherent quasi-particle spectral weight. Moreover, in the AFM + $(φ\neq 0)$ region, coherent quasi-particle bands coexist with incoherent Hubbard-like excitations, whereas only incoherent spectral features survive in the AFM + $(φ= 0)$ regime. The SC phase exhibits nodal quasi-particles consistent with $d$-wave pairing.

</details>


### [118] [Thermal Hall Signatures of Distinct Schwinger-Boson Flux Sectors on the Honeycomb Lattice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29792)
*Daiki Sasamoto*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper uses finite-temperature Schwinger-boson mean-field theory and the Kubo formula to compute the intrinsic spinon thermal Hall conductivity in three distinct flux sectors (0-flux, π-flux, and chiral π/2-flux) of a frustrated J1-J2 honeycomb Heisenberg model with DM interaction and magnetic field. The results show that the sign and temperature dependence of κxy/T provide a flux-sensitive transport signature distinguishing different parton mean-field structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Thermal transport is a bulk probe of charge-neutral excitations in frustrated magnets, but it remains unclear whether such transport can distinguish different parton mean-field structures of the same spin system. This is particularly relevant on the honeycomb lattice where distinct flux sectors (0-flux, π-flux, chiral π/2-flux) have been proposed, and the question of whether transport measurements can discriminate between them is open.

Method: The authors compare self-consistent saddle-point solutions in three flux sectors for a frustrated J1-J2 Heisenberg model with next-nearest-neighbor Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction and perpendicular magnetic field. They employ finite-temperature Schwinger-boson mean-field theory combined with the Kubo formula for bosonic Bogoliubov-de Gennes systems to evaluate the intrinsic spinon thermal Hall conductivity.

Result: At a common parameter point where all three branches remain gapped, the 0-flux response is positive and the π-flux response is negative for D>0 and h>0 under the paper's sign conventions, while the π/2-flux response changes sign with increasing temperature. Additionally, a projective symmetry combining sixfold rotation with time reversal forces the zero-field response of a fixed chiral π/2-flux domain to vanish. Thus, the sign and temperature dependence of κxy/T provide a flux-sensitive transport signature within the mean-field regime.

Conclusion: Thermal Hall conductivity (specifically κxy/T) can distinguish between different flux sectors in the honeycomb frustrated magnet, as each sector exhibits a distinct sign and temperature dependence, making thermal transport a viable probe of parton mean-field structure.

Abstract: Thermal transport offers a bulk probe of charge-neutral excitations in frustrated magnets. It remains challenging, however, to determine whether such transport can distinguish different parton mean-field structures of the same spin system. This question is particularly relevant on the honeycomb lattice, where distinct $0$-flux, $π$-flux, and chiral $π/2$-flux sectors have been proposed. In this paper, we compare self-consistent saddle-point solutions in these three sectors for a frustrated $J_1-J_2$ Heisenberg model with a next-nearest-neighbor Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction and a perpendicular magnetic field. Using finite-temperature Schwinger-boson mean-field theory and the Kubo formula for bosonic Bogoliubov-de Gennes systems, we evaluate the intrinsic spinon thermal Hall conductivity. At a common parameter point where all three branches remain gapped, the $0$-flux response is positive and the $π$-flux response is negative for $D>0$ and $h>0$ under the sign conventions used in this paper, whereas the $π/2$-flux response changes sign with increasing temperature. We further show that a projective symmetry combining a sixfold rotation with time reversal forces the zero-field response of a fixed chiral $π/2$-flux domain to vanish. Within the mean-field regime examined in this paper, the sign and temperature dependence of $κ_{xy}/T$ therefore provide a flux-sensitive transport signature.

</details>


### [119] [Symmetry-Enforced Topological Structures in Quantum Phase Diagrams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29915)
*Linhao Li,Yuan Yao*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper studies the topological structure of quantum phase diagrams for gapped systems by classifying noncontractible loops (S^1-families) within gapped phases. It reveals how exotic symmetry interplays—nontrivial mixed anomalies, semi-direct product relations between broken/unbroken symmetries, and noninvertible symmetries—lead to novel S^1-family classifications inaccessible by earlier frameworks built on "independent" symmetries, and provides explicit lattice realizations demonstrating these algebraic structures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To go beyond earlier classifications of S^1-families (noncontractible loops in gapped phase diagrams) that assumed "independent" symmetries, and to understand how exotic symmetry structures such as mixed anomalies, semi-direct products, and noninvertible operators give rise to new topological classifications of quantum phases.

Method: The authors analyze the topological structure of gapped phase diagrams by identifying S^1-family classifications based on symmetry interplays. They consider three exotic symmetry scenarios: (i) nontrivial mixed anomalies, (ii) semi-direct product relations between spontaneously broken and unbroken symmetries, and (iii) noninvertible symmetry operators. They then construct explicit lattice models realizing these S^1-families to demonstrate the novel algebraic structures.

Result: The paper finds that the three exotic symmetry interplays—mixed anomalies, semi-direct product relations, and noninvertible operators—produce novel S^1-family classifications that cannot be captured by existing classifications relying on independent symmetries. Explicit lattice realizations demonstrate these new algebraic structures concretely.

Conclusion: The work extends the classification of gapped quantum phases by showing that the topological structure of phase diagrams, encoded in S^1-families, is enriched by nontrivial symmetry interplays beyond independent symmetries. These findings open new avenues for classifying gapped systems and understanding the role of generalized symmetries in phase diagram topology.

Abstract: We study the topological structure of the quantum phase diagram of gapped systems by identifying the noncontractibility of loops, so-called $S^1$-families, within the gapped phase diagram in which many-body Hamiltonians can have nontrivial ground-state degeneracy. We manifest the role of symmetries in such $S^1$-family classifications by the exotic symmetry interplay: (i) nontrivial mixed anomalies, (ii) semi-direct product relation between the spontaneously broken and the unbroken symmetries, and (iii) symmetry with noninvertible operators. We find that such structures lead to the novel $S^1$-family classifications inaccessible by earlier classifications based on ``independent'' symmetries. Furthermore, we construct lattice realizations of these $S^1$-families and explicitly demonstrate their novel algebraic structures.

</details>


### [120] [Holographic Representations of Topological Quantum Criticality: Emergent Symmetry Approach around the Bott Clock](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30335)
*Fan Yang,Fei Zhou*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper applies emergent symmetries to construct holographic representations of topological quantum critical points (tQCPs) in fermionic classes of the Bott clock, building (d+1)-dimensional lattice models whose boundaries reproduce the infrared dynamics of tQCPs in d dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a general framework for holographically representing topological quantum critical points across a broad class of fermionic topological phases, specifically leveraging emergent symmetries that appear at critical points between symmetry-protected gapped phases in the Bott clock classification.

Method: The authors show that at a tQCP between symmetry-protected gapped phases with protecting symmetry G_p in d spatial dimensions, an emergent symmetry U_EM exists. This emergent symmetry is used to construct a (d+1)-dimensional lattice model with upgraded symmetry (U_EM × G_p or U_EM ⋊ G_p). By doubling the degrees of freedom of the adjacent topological class, they construct lattice models whose enlarged symmetry groups belong to the adjacent topological classes around the Bott clock, and then study their d-dimensional boundaries.

Result: The constructed (d+1)-dimensional lattice models exhibit desired enlarged symmetry groups belonging to the adjacent topological classes (counterclockwise around the Bott clock). The d-dimensional boundaries of these gapped topological lattice models reproduce the identical infrared dynamics of the corresponding tQCPs in d-dimensional adjacent topological phases with lower symmetries.

Conclusion: Emergent symmetries at topological quantum critical points provide a systematic construction of holographic bulk models whose boundary dynamics faithfully encode the critical behavior of tQCPs across the entire Bott clock, unifying the description of these critical points with (d+1)-dimensional topological bulk-boundary correspondence.

Abstract: In this article, we apply the idea of emergent symmetries to construct holographic representations of a broad class of topological quantum critical points (tQCPs) that appear in fermionic classes in the Bott clock. We show explicitly that around the Bott clock, an emergent symmetry $U_{EM}$ exists at a tQCP between different symmetry protected gapped phases with protecting symmetry $G_p$ in $d$ spatial dimensions. This emergent symmetry can be used to construct a $(d+1)$ spatial dimensional lattice model with a properly upgraded symmetry such as $U_{EM}\times G_p$ or $U_{EM} \rtimes G_p$, etc. By doubling the degrees of freedom of the adjacent topological class in $d$-dimensions, we successfully show that the $(d+1)$-dimensional lattice models constructed in this way have the desired enlarged symmetry groups that precisely belong to the adjacent topological classes, counterclockwise around the Bott clock. Furthermore, $d$-dimensional boundaries of these $(d+1)$ dimensional lattice models of gapped topological classes are shown to exhibit identical infrared dynamics as the corresponding tQCPs in the $d$-dimensional adjacent topological phases in the Bott clock, with lower symmetries.

</details>


### [121] [Pseudospin Dynamics of Charge Order](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30381)
*Ping Tang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper derives an effective pseudospin model from an extended Hubbard model, mapping charge-ordered states onto staggered pseudospin order. The charge-order dynamics obeys Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert-like equations analogous to a bipartite antiferromagnet. An external electric field can control the staggered pseudospin order and, above a threshold, drive coherent reversal of charge-order polarity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While experimental capabilities to probe and manipulate nonequilibrium charge-order dynamics are growing, a microscopic framework for charge-order dynamics and its control under external driving remains largely underdeveloped, in contrast to the well-established framework for spin-ordered systems.

Method: Starting from an extended Hubbard model, the authors derive an effective pseudospin model where the charge-ordered state maps onto staggered pseudospin order. The dynamics is then formulated via Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert-like equations for the pseudospins.

Result: The study shows that an external electric field directly controls the staggered pseudospin order. Above a threshold field, coherent reversal of charge-order polarity is driven by destabilizing collective pseudospin excitations.

Conclusion: The charge pseudospin is established as a microscopic dynamical degree of freedom for coherent switching and control of charge order, providing a charge-sector analogue of the spin-order dynamics framework in spintronics.

Abstract: Charge order is conventionally viewed as a static modulation of the electronic density, despite growing experimental capabilities to probe and manipulate its nonequilibrium evolution. In contrast to spin-ordered systems, a microscopic framework for charge-order dynamics and its control under external driving remains largely underdeveloped. Here, starting from an extended Hubbard model, we derive an effective pseudospin model in which the charge-ordered state maps onto staggered pseudospin order. The resulting charge-order dynamics is governed by Landau--Lifshitz--Gilbert-like equations for the pseudospins, closely analogous to those of a bipartite antiferromagnet. We show that an external electric field directly controls the staggered pseudospin order and, above a threshold field, drives coherent reversal of the charge-order polarity by destabilizing collective pseudospin excitations. Our results establish the charge pseudospin as a microscopic dynamical degree of freedom for coherent switching and control of charge order, providing a charge-sector analogue of the well-established framework for spin-order dynamics in spintronics.

</details>


### [122] [Chiral Color Ice: Exact Local Handedness Constraints and Möbius Zero Modes in Frustrated Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30802)
*Péter Kránitz,Yasir Iqbal,Karlo Penc*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper introduces SU(2)-invariant local constraints that impose handedness (chirality) as an exact quantum constraint on frustrated spin systems, constructing parent Hamiltonians whose zero-energy spaces on tetrahedra are chiral singlets. The authors map these constraints to Möbius transformations for coherent states, derive constraint holonomies on lattices, and show that different lattice connectivities produce qualitatively distinct regimes—from exponentially large quantum ground-state kernels on corner-sharing lattices to subdimensional zero modes on edge-sharing lattices and complete Anderson towers on triangular lattices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frustrated magnetic systems are conventionally described by ice-type local constraints that constrain flux-like quantities and are insensitive to handedness (chirality). The authors aim to demonstrate that chirality itself can serve as an exact local constraint without selecting a preferred axis in spin space, creating a tractable framework to disentangle local handedness, nonlinear constraint geometry, and quantum degeneracy.

Method: The authors construct positive-semidefinite, SU(2)-invariant parent Hamiltonians whose zero-energy space on a tetrahedron has a prescribed chirality sign. For spin-1/2, the local term is a rank-one projector onto a chiral tetrahedral singlet; for arbitrary spin it factorizes as B†B through a singlet-annihilation operator with a kernel spanned by globally rotated chiral color-ice states. For coherent states, the zero-energy condition becomes an S-independent nonlinear constraint where three spin directions determine the fourth via a Möbius transformation; compositions define constraint holonomies on extended lattices.

Result: Corner-sharing lattices retain exponentially large quantum ground-state kernels with rigorous lower bounds exceeding conventional ice benchmarks. Edge-sharing lattices support subdimensional plane or line zero modes. Triangular constructions suppress nonuniform coherent deformations and contain the complete Anderson tower of tetrahedral magnetic order at exactly zero energy. Two inequivalent triangular coverings demonstrate that harmonic zero-mode counting does not determine the size of the quantum kernel.

Conclusion: Local handedness can be imposed as an exact SU(2)-invariant quantum constraint, and the resulting physics depends directly on the connectivity of the constraint network. This establishes a tractable setting where chirality constraints, nonlinear constraint geometry, and quantum degeneracy can be related in a precise and rigorous manner.

Abstract: Local constraints govern the low-energy physics of frustrated matter, but familiar ice-type rules constrain flux-like quantities and are insensitive to handedness. Here we show that handedness itself can be imposed as an exact local quantum constraint without selecting an axis in spin space. We construct positive-semidefinite, SU(2)-invariant parent Hamiltonians whose complete zero-energy space on a tetrahedron has a prescribed chirality sign, rather than selecting a particular chiral wave function. For spin-1/2 the local term is a rank-one projector onto a chiral tetrahedral singlet, while for arbitrary spin it factorizes as $B^\dagger B$ through a singlet-annihilation operator, with a completely characterized kernel given by the span of the globally rotated chiral color-ice states. For coherent states, the same zero-energy condition becomes an $S$-independent nonlinear constraint in which three spin directions determine the fourth through a Möbius transformation; compositions of these maps define constraint holonomies on extended lattices. Connecting the same local constraint in different ways produces qualitatively different collective regimes: corner-sharing lattices retain exponentially large quantum ground-state kernels, with rigorous lower bounds exceeding conventional ice benchmarks; edge-sharing lattices support subdimensional plane or line zero modes; while triangular constructions suppress nonuniform coherent deformations and contain the complete Anderson tower of tetrahedral magnetic order at exactly zero energy. Two inequivalent triangular coverings further show that harmonic zero-mode counting does not determine the size of the quantum kernel. These results establish a tractable setting in which local handedness, nonlinear constraint geometry, and quantum degeneracy can be disentangled and related directly to the connectivity of the constraint network.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mes-hall'></div>

# cond-mat.mes-hall [[Back]](#toc)

### [123] [Statistical Properties of a Fluctuation-Driven Nanomechanical Duffing Resonator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28731)
*Monan Ma,Nathan Welles,Johnathon Barbish,Ismet I. Kaya,M. Selim Hanay,Oleksiy Svitelskiy,Mark R. Paul,Kamil L. Ekinci*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates the fluctuating nonlinear dynamics of multiple modes in a nanomechanical doubly clamped beam resonator, driven by Gaussian force noise near resonance. As the force magnitude increases, the system transitions from harmonic to nonlinear Duffing behavior, characterized by a reduction in effective temperature and a crossover in response statistics from Gaussian to platykurtic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how nonlinearities affect the fluctuation dynamics and statistical properties of multimode nanomechanical resonators, which is relevant for their use as sensors and in fundamental studies of nonlinear stochastic dynamics in mesoscopic systems.

Method: Each mode of a doubly clamped nanobeam is driven by Gaussian force noise centered around resonance. The mode response is monitored while the driving force magnitude is increased, and an effective temperature is defined based on the mode fluctuation amplitude to quantify the transition from harmonic to nonlinear Duffing dynamics.

Result: At large amplitudes where nonlinearity becomes prominent, the effective thermal energy is reduced, and the response statistics cross over from Gaussian to platykurtic distributions, consistent with theoretical expectations for a Duffing potential.

Conclusion: The transition from harmonic to nonlinear Duffing dynamics in multimode nanomechanical resonators manifests as a measurable suppression of effective thermal energy and a characteristic change in the statistics toward platykurtic distributions, validating the Duffing potential description of nonlinear fluctuation dynamics.

Abstract: We investigate the fluctuating nonlinear dynamics of multiple modes of a nanomechanical doubly clamped beam resonator. Each mode is driven by Gaussian force noise centered around the resonance; the mode response is monitored while the force magnitude is increased, inducing a transition from harmonic to the nonlinear Duffing regime. To characterize the dynamics, we introduce an effective temperature based on the mode fluctuation amplitude. As the nonlinearity becomes prominent at large amplitudes, we observe a reduction in the effective thermal energy and a crossover in response statistics from Gaussian to platykurtic, consistent with the dynamics expected in a Duffing potential.

</details>


### [124] [Domain walls with alternating magnetic order in a model with dipolar coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28831)
*G. M. Wysin*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper analyzes static domain walls in a one-dimensional chain of elongated nano-scale magnetic islands with alternating order, using both numerical relaxation simulations and a two-sublattice continuum theory, finding that domain wall width diverges as the uniaxial anisotropy approaches the nearest-neighbor dipolar coupling, and revealing the walls carry a large longitudinal magnetic moment with opposite-sublattice rotation senses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the static properties of domain walls in a chain of dipole-interacting nano-scale magnetic islands exhibiting transverse alternating order, particularly the behavior as material parameters approach critical values.

Method: Numerical relaxation simulations for the lattice model, complemented by a two-sublattice continuum theory assuming nearest-neighbor dipole interactions only.

Result: The domain wall width diverges as the uniaxial anisotropy constant K1 approaches the NN dipolar coupling constant D from above. The applied field causes slight canting of dipoles toward the field, maximal at the wall center. Dipoles on the two sublattices rotate in opposite senses, producing a large longitudinal wall moment; a smaller transverse moment has a topological contribution depending on chain length parity.

Conclusion: The domain wall structure in the alternating-order magnetic island chain exhibits critical broadening near the anisotropy-dipolar coupling threshold, with distinctive magnetic moment properties arising from the opposite-sublattice rotation.

Abstract: A model for a one-dimensional chain of elongated nano-scale magnetic islands with dipole interactions is analyzed here for the properties of its static domain walls with site-by-site alternating order. The anisotropic magnetic islands on a nonmagnetic substrate have their longer axes oriented transverse ($y$-direction) to the chain direction ($x$-direction), in a transverse applied magnetic field. The islands' magnetic dipoles $\vecμ_n$ are represented as macrospins of fixed length $μ$. The nearest-neighbor (NN) dipole interactions drive transverse alternating order, allowing for doubly-degenerate, uniform, static, $y$-alternating states, where the dipoles alternately point transverse to the chain direction, as in $\vecμ_n = \pm(-1)^n μ\hat{y}$. Assuming only NN interactions, the domain walls connecting these two alternating states are found with numerical relaxation simulations and analyzed in a two-sublattice continuum theory. As the uniaxial anisotropy constant $K_1$ descends from larger values until it closely approaches the NN dipolar coupling constant $D$, the domain wall width grows indefinitely, and the large-$x$ continuum solutions closely approach the lattice numerical solutions. The applied field produces a very slight canting of the dipoles towards the field, maximum in the center of the domain wall. The dipoles on the two sublattices are found to rotate in {\em opposite senses} as one scans along the lattice, resulting in a large longitudinal magnetic moment of the domain wall. A much smaller transverse magnetic moment has a topological contribution that depends on whether the chain length is odd or even.

</details>


### [125] [Electrochemical impedance spectroscopy of graphene nanogaps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28836)
*Patrick A. McKee,Chris S. DeMellier,Robin N. Schipper,Henk W. Ch. Postma*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper characterizes the electrochemical interfacial behavior of graphene nanogaps in aqueous environments using electrochemical impedance spectroscopy, revealing a Warburg-type diffusion response in an adsorbed conductive surface film. A quantitative equivalent-circuit model extracts nanogap length scales from impedance spectra, complementing tunneling-based measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphene nanogaps are promising for nanoscale electrochemical and biomolecular sequencing devices, but their interfacial behavior in aqueous environments—especially frequency-dependent impedance and charge transport at graphene edges—remains poorly understood.

Method: Graphene nanogaps were fabricated via controlled electrical breakdown in an inert atmosphere. Their electrochemical response was studied using electrochemical impedance spectroscopy across different liquid pH values, and a quantitative equivalent-circuit model (including a Warburg element) was developed to fit the impedance spectra and extract nanogap length scales.

Result: A surface contamination layer forms an ultrathin conductive film supporting electrochemical activity between graphene edges. Impedance spectra show Warburg-type diffusion behavior, with systematic pH-dependent evolution of the electrochemical reaction-diffusion processes. The equivalent-circuit model successfully captures these effects and extracts effective nanogap length scales.

Conclusion: Electrochemical impedance spectroscopy provides complementary information to tunneling measurements for characterizing graphene nanogaps, enabling quantitative extraction of nanogap dimensions from frequency-dependent impedance data.

Abstract: Graphene nanogaps represent an emerging platform for nanoscale electrochemical and sensing devices, with potential applications in next-generation biomolecular sequencing. However, their interfacial behavior in aqueous environments remains poorly characterized, particularly with respect to frequency-dependent impedance and charge transport mechanisms at the graphene edge. We fabricate graphene nanogaps by controlled electrical breakdown in an inert atmosphere and study their electrochemical response. Upon exposure to ambient conditions, a surface contamination layer supports electrochemical activity within an adsorbed ultrathin conductive film between the graphene edges. Electrochemical impedance spectroscopy reveals distinct frequency-dependent responses consistent with a Warburg element associated with diffusion in this confined interfacial film. The impedance evolves systematically with liquid $\mathrm{p}H$, reflecting changes in electrochemical reaction-diffusion processes at the graphene edges. A quantitative equivalent-circuit model captures these effects and enables extraction of an effective nanogap length scale from impedance spectra, providing information complementary to that obtained from tunneling measurements.

</details>


### [126] [Spin-textured orbitals in altermagnetic artificial atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29861)
*Yue Mao,Yu-Chen Zhuang,Cheng-Ming Miao,Yu-Fei Sun,Qing-Feng Sun*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper introduces altermagnetic artificial atoms—quantum-confined electron systems with momentum-dependent spin splitting—where confined orbitals become spin-textured with spatially separated opposite spin components, preserving a twofold degeneracy via C₄z𝒯 symmetry, and shows that strain can lift this degeneracy into spin-polarized states with tunable energy splitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To realize artificial atoms where spin is not a passive degree of freedom but actively participates in shaping confined orbital wavefunctions.

Method: The authors theoretically analyze electrons confined in altermagnetic systems, examining how momentum-dependent spin splitting reconstructs conventional confined orbitals into spin-textured orbitals, and study the effect of strain on degenerate orbital pairs.

Result: Altermagnetic confinement produces spin-textured orbitals with spatially distinct spin components; the spectrum retains twofold degeneracy protected by C₄z𝒯 symmetry; higher-energy states combine radial and angular spin structures; strain splits degenerate pairs into spin-polarized states with tunable splitting.

Conclusion: Altermagnetic artificial atoms provide a route to engineering spin-dependent orbital structures in quantum-confined systems, enabling controllable spin-orbit physics at the orbital level.

Abstract: Artificial atoms provide a versatile platform for engineering atomic-like orbitals, yet spin generally remains a passive degree of freedom in their orbital structure. Here, we introduce the concept of altermagnetic artificial atoms formed by confining electrons with momentum-dependent spin splitting. We show that altermagnetism reconstructs conventional confined orbitals into spin-textured orbitals, with spatially distinct distributions of opposite spin components. The resulting confined spectrum retains a twofold degeneracy protected by the combined $C_{4z}\mathcal{T}$ symmetry. These spin textures persist in higher-energy states, where additional radial structures combine with the characteristic angular spin pattern. Furthermore, strain resolves the degenerate orbital pairs into spin-polarized states, and continuously tunes their energy splitting. Our results establish altermagnetic artificial atoms as a route to engineering spin-dependent orbital structures in quantum-confined systems.

</details>


### [127] [Design and Modeling of the Charge Readout of a SiMOS Quantum Dot with a Single Electron Transistor and CryoCMOS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28906)
*Adam Quinn,Troy England,Andrew Li,Sharmila Mustari Nandita*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper presents design techniques and simulation results for an optimized qubit-single electron transistor (SET) cryogenic CMOS readout interface, culminating in the QNDR1 ASIC—the first cryogenic readout ASIC under the Quandarum project, targeting many-channel spin qubit-based detectors for high energy physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single electron spin qubits in SiMOS quantum dots show promise for scaling to large qubit systems, but require a readout system that avoids bulky RF components and room-temperature interconnects. A cryogenic CMOS amplified readout chain integrated with a single electron transistor offers key advantages for scalable, many-channel readout.

Method: The work presents design techniques and simulation results for an optimized qubit-SET cryoCMOS interface, combining a single electron transistor with a custom cryogenic CMOS amplification and digitization chain.

Result: The optimization culminates in the design of the QNDR1 ASIC, the first cryogenic readout ASIC designed under the Quandarum project for a many-channel spin qubit-based detector.

Conclusion: The QNDR1 ASIC demonstrates a viable path toward scalable, many-channel qubit readout using cryoCMOS technology, leveraging CMOS manufacturing compatibility and avoiding the use of RF components or room-temperature interconnects, with applications in high energy physics detection.

Abstract: Single electron spin qubits trapped in SiMOS quantum dots are a promising technology for scaling to thousand- or million-qubit systems due to their compatibility with mature CMOS manufacturing processes. A readout system that combines a single electron transistor with a custom cryogenic CMOS amplification and digitization chain offers key advantages by avoiding the use of bulky RF components or room-temperature interconnects. We present design techniques and simulation results for an optimized qubit-SET cryoCMOS interface, culminating in the design of the QNDR1 ASIC, the first cryogenic readout ASIC designed under the Quandarum project, which targets the development of a many-channel spin qubit based detector for use in high energy physics.

</details>


### [128] [Chiral Tubular Magnonic Crystals with Periodic Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28928)
*P. Contreras-Gallardo,J. Flores-Farias,B. Mimica-Figari,V. Puliafito,P. Landeros,R. A. Gallardo*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper introduces a chiral tubular magnonic crystal formed by a ferromagnetic nanotube with periodically modulated interfacial Dzyaloshinskii–Moriya interaction (DMI). Using a plane-wave formalism adapted to cylindrical geometry, the authors show that tubular curvature converts part of the interfacial DMI into an effective anisotropy-like internal field, creating sign-dependent frequency landscapes. This leads to flat and weakly dispersive bands in both Damon–Eshbach-like and backward-volume-like configurations, offering new routes for controlling nonreciprocal spin waves, localized modes, and flat magnonic bands in curved architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how periodic modulation of interfacial DMI in a tubular (curved) geometry alters the magnonic band structure compared to planar chiral magnonic crystals, and to identify new phenomena enabled by curvature that could control spin-wave propagation.

Method: A plane-wave formalism adapted to cylindrical geometry is developed to calculate spin-wave band structures, incorporating exchange interactions, nonlocal dipolar coupling, and interfacial DMI in a ferromagnetic nanotube with periodically modulated DMI.

Result: Curvature converts part of the interfacial DMI into an effective anisotropy-like field, creating a sign-dependent frequency landscape where DMI-covered regions act as potential wells or barriers. Flat, weakly dispersive bands emerge in both Damon–Eshbach-like and backward-volume-like configurations, with the latter having no planar counterpart.

Conclusion: Periodically engineered DMI in nanotubes provides a promising route for controlling nonreciprocal spin waves, localized modes, and flat magnonic bands in curved magnetic architectures.

Abstract: We introduce a chiral tubular magnonic crystal formed by a ferromagnetic nanotube with a periodically modulated interfacial Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya interaction (DMI). Using a plane-wave formalism adapted to the cylindrical geometry, we calculate the spin-wave band structure including exchange, nonlocal dipolar coupling, and interfacial DMI. We show that the tubular geometry qualitatively modifies the role of periodic DMI compared with planar chiral magnonic crystals. Besides producing the usual nonreciprocal frequency shift, curvature converts part of the interfacial DMI into an effective anisotropy-like internal field. This creates a sign-dependent frequency landscape along the tube, so that DMI-covered regions can act either as potential wells or barriers for the lowest-frequency modes. As a result, flat and weakly dispersive bands appear in both Damon--Eshbach-like and backward-volume-like configurations, with the latter having no direct counterpart in planar systems. Our results identify periodically engineered DMI in nanotubes as a route for controlling nonreciprocal spin waves, localized modes, and flat magnonic bands in curved architectures.

</details>


### [129] [Coherent phononic frequency combs in ferroelectric CMOS oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28956)
*Jinghan Gao,Shruti Mishra,Yilin Kou,S M Enamul Hoque Yousuf,Hanna Cho,Roozbeh Tabrizian*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper reports broadband phononic frequency combs in ferroelectric hafnia-zirconia nanoelectromechanical resonators built from CMOS oxides. Two generation mechanisms are demonstrated: two-tone-seeded wave mixing producing >170 lines over two octaves, and autonomous Hopf routes yielding hierarchical combs of >200 lines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern electronic systems require tens of clock and carrier frequencies, each generated by a dedicated phase-locked loop, creating routing, power, and synchronization burdens. While optical frequency combs solved multi-frequency generation in photonics, electronics lacks an equivalent broadband source in its native radiofrequency domain.

Method: Lithographically defined detuning of a 2:1 internal resonance selects the comb generation mechanism. Two-tone-seeded wave mixing produces >170 lines over two octaves with verified mutual coherence, while an autonomous Hopf route yields >200-line hierarchical combs via torus and period-doubling dynamics, consistent with slow-flow bifurcation theory. Geometric scaling extends generation from 0.44-2.1 GHz, and heterodyne measurements with modified Allan deviation (MDEV) characterize stability.

Result: The two-timescale stability law shows seeded combs inherit white-phase-noise scaling of their pumps, while autonomous combs exhibit phase diffusion of a free-running oscillator, with one-second MDEV increasing from 1e-11 to 1e-8. The temperature-compensated material stack suppresses random-walk drift in air without active thermal control, dominating uncompensated resonators.

Conclusion: The results establish mechanism- and material-level design rules for operating a single resonator as chip-scale frequency infrastructure, enabling applications from multi-clock generation to radiofrequency parallel processing.

Abstract: Modern electronic systems require tens of clock and carrier frequencies, each synthesized by a dedicated phase-locked loop from a shared reference, imposing routing, power and synchronization burdens that grow with every domain. Optical frequency combs solved this problem in photonics, whereas electronics has lacked an equivalent source in its native radiofrequency domain. Here we report broadband phononic frequency combs in ferroelectric hafnia-zirconia nanoelectromechanical resonators built from complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor (CMOS) oxides. Lithographically defined detuning of a 2:1 internal resonance selects the generation mechanism: two-tone-seeded wave mixing yields more than 170 lines distributed over two octaves, with mutual coherence verified for representative pump and generated lines, whereas an autonomous Hopf route yields hierarchical combs of more than 200 lines through torus and period-doubling dynamics, in agreement with slow-flow bifurcation theory. Geometric scaling extends comb generation across 0.44 GHz to 2.1 GHz. Heterodyne measurements, analyzed using the modified Allan deviation (MDEV), establish a two-timescale law. The mechanism governs short-term stability: seeded combs inherit the white-phase-noise scaling of their pumps, whereas autonomous combs acquire the phase diffusion of a free-running oscillator, with the one-second MDEV increasing from 1e-11 to 1e-8. The material governs long-term stability: in air and without active thermal control, the temperature-compensated stack suppresses the random-walk drift that dominates uncompensated resonators. These results establish mechanism- and material-level design rules for operating a single resonator as chip-scale frequency infrastructure, from multi-clock generation to radiofrequency parallel processing.

</details>


### [130] [Harnessing the skyrmion Hall effect for low-power skyrmion transport in a tubular synthetic antiferromagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29724)
*Ivan P. Miranda,Grzegorz J. Kwiatkowski,Jacob J. Mankenberg,Cecilia M. Holmqvist,Igor S. Lobanov,Valery M. Uzdin,Pavel F. Bessarab*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that the skyrmion Hall effect, typically a source of unwanted transverse motion, can be harnessed via optimal control to reduce Ohmic losses in skyrmion transport. Using a pair of antiferromagnetically coupled skyrmions in a tubular geometry, the authors show that relative transverse displacement acts as a periodic internal coordinate driven by the skyrmion Hall effect, creating a nonlinear current-velocity relation that allows different current protocols to achieve the same average velocity. They identify the protocol minimizing Joule heating, which is either constant or time-dependent depending on interlayer coupling and spin-transfer-torque parameters, and show that this internal degree of freedom always enables lower-power transport than the uncoupled limit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Skyrmion transport typically suffers from the skyrmion Hall effect causing unwanted transverse motion. The authors aim to turn this apparent disadvantage into a beneficial mechanism by exploiting the additional internal degrees of freedom created by skyrmion Hall effect and interlayer coupling to reduce Ohmic losses during transport.

Method: The authors consider a pair of antiferromagnetically coupled skyrmions confined to a tubular geometry, where relative transverse displacement becomes a periodic internal coordinate. They analyze the nonlinear relation between applied current and longitudinal velocity arising from the skyrmion Hall effect and finite interlayer coupling, then apply optimal control theory to determine which current protocols minimize Joule heating while producing the same prescribed average velocity.

Result: Depending on interlayer coupling and spin-transfer-torque parameters, the optimal current is either constant (with the skyrmion pair maintaining a fixed relative position) or time-dependent (with the pair undergoing periodic relative motion around the tube). In both cases, the additional internal degree of freedom created by the skyrmion Hall effect and interlayer coupling enables skyrmion transport at lower power than in the uncoupled limit.

Conclusion: The skyrmion Hall effect, combined with interlayer coupling in antiferromagnetically coupled skyrmion pairs, can be repurposed as a tool for energy-efficient skyrmion transport. This work opens a pathway to design more efficient skyrmion-based devices by leveraging internal degrees of freedom for power optimization.

Abstract: We show that optimal control can turn the skyrmion Hall effect from a source of typically unwanted transverse motion into a mechanism for reducing Ohmic losses in skyrmion transport. We consider a pair of antiferromagnetically coupled skyrmions confined to a tubular geometry, where their relative transverse displacement becomes a periodic internal coordinate. The skyrmion Hall effect drives this coordinate, while finite interlayer coupling makes the resulting relation between applied current and longitudinal velocity nonlinear, allowing different current protocols to produce the same prescribed average velocity. We determine which of these protocols minimizes Joule heating. Depending on the interlayer coupling and spin-transfer-torque parameters, the optimal current is either constant, with the skyrmion pair maintaining a fixed relative position, or time dependent, with the pair undergoing periodic relative motion around the tube. In either case, the additional internal degree of freedom created by the skyrmion Hall effect and interlayer coupling enables skyrmion transport at a lower power than in the uncoupled limit.

</details>


### [131] [Surface-mediated frequency aging beyond quality-factor saturation in an AlScN-on-silicon resonator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28959)
*S M Enamul Hoque Yousuf,Mahmudul Hasan,Shruti Mishra,Sourav Mukherjee,Jinghan Gao,Roozbeh Tabrizian*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper shows that quality factor (Q) saturation in a 64.21 MHz Al0.7Sc0.3N-on-silicon Lamé-mode resonator is insufficient to certify vacuum packaging for frequency references, because Q probes gas damping while frequency (f0) tracks surface-state aging with much slower, history-dependent stretched-exponential kinetics down to 1e-5 Torr.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional vacuum package integrity assessment relies on Q, which only reflects residual-gas damping. For precision mechanical frequency references, surface-state evolution governs frequency aging, so Q saturation alone cannot verify package quality for frequency stability.

Method: Pressure-controlled measurements (0.01 to 760 Torr) of a 64.21 MHz Al0.7Sc0.3N-on-silicon cross-sectional Lamé-mode resonator at 25 °C and at the 68.8°C frequency turnover. Q and f0 responses to pressure steps were compared, with transient kinetics modeled using stretched exponentials and short/long-term stability assessed via ten-hour phase-locked measurements.

Result: Q reaches a reversible, history-independent steady ceiling of 8.5e4 below ~1 Torr on the pressure-control timescale, whereas f0 responds down to 1e-5 Torr, remains history-dependent, relaxes for hours with stretched-exponential kinetics, and shows fractional shifts up to 1.9 ppm per pressure step. Short-term time deviation floor is ~1e-10 s from 0.01 to 100 Torr; gas damping degrades tracking at 760 Torr.

Conclusion: Gas-damping equilibrium and frequency equilibrium are distinct states. Q saturation cannot qualify vacuum packaging for precision frequency references; package specifications must also constrain surface-mediated frequency aging.

Abstract: Vacuum package integrity in micro- and nanoelectromechanical resonators is commonly assessed through the quality factor Q, although Q probes residual-gas damping rather than the surface-state evolution that can govern frequency aging. Here, we disentangle the pressure responses of Q and the resonance frequency f0 in a 64.21 MHz Al0.7Sc0.3N-on-silicon cross-sectional Lamé-mode resonator between 0.01 to 760 Torr. Measurements at 25 °C and at the 68.8°C frequency turnover, where first-order thermal sensitivity is suppressed, reveal widely separated equilibration timescales. Following each pressure step, Q reaches a reversible, history-independent steady value on the pressure-control timescale and approaches a fitted pressure-independent ceiling of 8.5e4 below approximately 1 Torr. By contrast, f0 responds measurably down to 1e-5 Torr and remains history-dependent, relaxing for hours at fixed pressure. The transients follow stretched-exponential kinetics, consistent with a broad distribution of surface relaxation rates, and individual pressure steps produce fractional frequency shifts as large as 1.9 ppm. Ten-hour phase-locked measurements show a common short-term time-deviation floor near 1e-10 s from 0.01 to 100 Torr, whereas residual deterministic relaxation dominates at long averaging times; at 760 Torr, gas damping degrades short-term tracking through the reduced Q. These results establish gas-damping equilibrium and frequency equilibrium as distinct states. Quality-factor saturation alone is therefore insufficient to qualify vacuum packaging for precision mechanical frequency references; package specifications must also constrain surface-mediated frequency aging.

</details>


### [132] [Fractional Impurity Entropy from Potential Scattering in a Free Electron Gas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29007)
*Syeda Neha Zaidi,Jukka I. Väyrynen*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that a fractional entropy change of k_B ln√2 can occur in a simple system of free electrons on a ring scattering off a repulsive point-like impurity, without requiring strong electron correlations or non-Abelian anyons. The effect arises from a shift of single-particle momenta from integer to half-integer quantization induced by the impurity, and is shown to be universal across a broad class of single-particle dispersions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to challenge the conventional association between fractional entropy changes and strong electron correlations or non-Abelian anyons in topological systems, showing that such signatures can arise from a much simpler mechanism in non-interacting systems.

Method: The authors analyze a system of free electrons on a ring with a repulsive point-like impurity. They demonstrate that the impurity shifts the allowed single-particle momentum quantization from integer to half-integer values, and compute the resulting entropy change as impurity strength is increased. They then verify universality of this effect across various single-particle dispersion relations.

Result: A repulsive point-like impurity on a ring of free electrons produces a fractional entropy change of k_B ln√2, arising purely from a quantization shift of single-particle momenta. This effect is shown to be universal across a broad class of single-particle dispersions, independent of electron correlation strength or topological order.

Conclusion: Fractional entropy signatures do not necessarily imply strong correlations or non-Abelian anyons; they can emerge from a simple boundary-condition change (integer to half-integer quantization) in non-interacting systems on a ring with an impurity.

Abstract: Fractional changes in entropy are often associated with strong electron correlations or non-Abelian anyons in topological systems. We show that neither condition is necessary -- a system of free electrons on a ring scattering off a repulsive point-like impurity undergoes a $k_B\ln\sqrt 2$ entropy change as the impurity strength is increased. This effect arises from a shift of allowed single-particle momenta from integer quantization to half-integer quantization, induced by the impurity. We show that this fractional entropy change is universal across a broad class of single-particle dispersions, demonstrating that such signatures can arise without strong correlations or topological order.

</details>


### [133] [Probing spin order via magnon transmission across quantum Hall ferromagnet heterojunctions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29015)
*Seung Hwan Lee,Shaowen Chen,Andrew T. Pierce,Patrick R. Forrester,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Amir Yacoby*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates, for the first time, magnon transmission across a monolayer-bilayer graphene quantum Hall ferromagnet heterojunction, using monolayer graphene as a magnon source to probe spin order in bilayer graphene symmetry-broken quantum Hall states via nonlocal voltage measurements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Probing magnetic order in two-dimensional material platforms hosting exotic correlated phases is essential, but dilute spin densities render conventional magnetic probes ineffective. While spin waves in quantum Hall ferromagnets have proven effective for probing magnetic order in graphene, previous work was limited to homojunction configurations within a single material.

Method: The authors generate magnons in monolayer graphene at filling factor ν=1 and detect their transmission through bilayer graphene via nonlocal voltage measurements. This cross-material approach uses one material as a magnon source to probe magnetic order in a distinct material at a quantum Hall ferromagnet heterojunction.

Result: Magnon transmission through bilayer graphene reveals spin order in its symmetry-broken quantum Hall states, exhibiting hallmark magnon signatures including a sharp onset at the Zeeman energy and systematic variation with Landau level filling, including suppression at ν=4 and 8 where spin polarization vanishes.

Conclusion: Heterojunction magnon transmission is established as a powerful, modular probe of magnetic order, opening new avenues for investigating exotic quantum states across the expanding family of two-dimensional materials.

Abstract: Two-dimensional material platforms now host a remarkable array of exotic correlated phases, from unconventional superconductivity to fractional Chern insulators. Probing magnetic order in these systems is essential for understanding their underlying physics, yet dilute spin densities render conventional magnetic probes ineffective. Spin waves, or magnons, in quantum Hall ferromagnets (QHFM) have proven effective for probing the magnetic order in various symmetry-broken quantum Hall (QH) phases in graphene systems, but previous works have been limited to homojunction configurations within a single material. Here, we demonstrate magnon transmission across a monolayer-bilayer graphene quantum Hall ferromagnet heterojunction - the first magnon transmission across quantum Hall ferromagnet heterojunctions, using one material as a magnon source to probe magnetic order in a distinct material. Generating magnons in monolayer graphene (MLG) at $ν$ = 1, we detect their transmission through bilayer graphene (BLG) via nonlocal voltage measurements, revealing spin order in BLG symmetry-broken quantum Hall states. The transmission exhibits hallmark magnon signatures: a sharp onset at the Zeeman energy and systematic variation with Landau level filling, including suppression at $ν$ = 4 and 8 where spin polarization vanishes. Our findings establish heterojunction magnon transmission as a powerful, modular probe of magnetic order, opening new avenues for investigating exotic quantum states across the rapidly expanding family of two-dimensional materials.

</details>


### [134] [Quantitative Disentanglement of Terahertz Spin and Orbital Pumping in 3d Ferromagnetic Heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29165)
*Tongyang Guan,Jiahao Liu,Yuxiao Mo,Liangliang Zhu,Yizheng Wu,Zhensheng Tao*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper combines spintronic terahertz emission spectroscopy (STES) with wedge-sample thickness control to quantitatively decompose spin and orbital pumping contributions in ferromagnet/nonmagnetic-metal heterostructures (Fe, Co, Ni with Ta, W, Nb). The results reveal that orbital-pumping contributions increase progressively toward Ni (reaching tens of percent of spin current) and exceed theoretical predictions, with Fe also generating non-negligible orbital currents essential in the thin-nonmagnetic-layer regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Microscopic theory predicts orbital-pumping efficiency increases from Fe to Ni across the 3d series, but it is unclear whether these predictions hold under ultrafast excitation. The central challenge is that spin and orbital currents are generated simultaneously and contribute additively to the same terahertz emission, preventing their quantitative separation.

Method: The authors combine STES with wedge-sample thickness control in heterostructures whose nonmagnetic layers (Ta, W, Nb) have spin Hall and orbital Hall angles of opposite signs. This yields distinct emission polarities and thickness dependences for the two angular-momentum channels, enabling quantitative decomposition of spin and orbital pumping contributions.

Result: Systematic measurements on Fe, Co, and Ni heterostructures show that the orbital-pumping contribution increases progressively toward Ni, reaching several tens of percent of the spin-current contribution—far exceeding theoretical predictions. Even Fe generates a non-negligible orbital current that becomes essential in the thin-nonmagnetic-layer regime. Extracted orbital diffusion lengths are consistently shorter than spin diffusion lengths and increase with decreasing spin-orbit coupling strength of the nonmagnetic layer.

Conclusion: This work establishes a quantitative framework for ultrafast spin and orbital pumping in magnetic heterostructures, enabling quantitative separation of the two angular-momentum channels and revealing that orbital contributions are much larger than theoretically predicted across the 3d series.

Abstract: Spin and orbital pumping - the injection of spin and orbital angular momentum from a driven ferromagnet into an adjacent nonmagnetic layer - are fundamental processes underlying angular-momentum generation and transport in magnetic heterostructures. Femtosecond optical excitation extends these phenomena into the ultrafast regime, where spintronic terahertz emission spectroscopy (STES) detects picosecond angular-momentum currents in a contact-free manner through spin-to-charge and orbital-to-charge conversion. Microscopic theory predicts that orbital-pumping efficiency increases from Fe to Ni across the 3d series, yet whether these predictions hold under ultrafast excitation remains unclear. A central challenge is that spin and orbital currents are generated simultaneously and contribute additively to the same terahertz emission, preventing their quantitative separation. Here, we overcome this limitation by combining STES with wedge-sample thickness control in heterostructures whose nonmagnetic layers (Ta, W, and Nb) have spin Hall and orbital Hall angles of opposite signs. The two angular-momentum channels therefore exhibit distinct emission polarities and thickness dependences, enabling their quantitative decomposition. Systematic measurements on Fe, Co, and Ni heterostructures reveal that the orbital-pumping contribution increases progressively toward Ni, reaching several tens of percent of the spin-current contribution - far exceeding theoretical predictions. Even Fe generates a non-negligible orbital current that becomes essential in the thin-nonmagnetic-layer regime. The extracted orbital diffusion lengths are consistently shorter than spin diffusion lengths and increase with decreasing spin-orbit coupling strength of the nonmagnetic layer. These results establish a quantitative framework for ultrafast spin and orbital pumping in magnetic heterostructures.

</details>


### [135] [Heavy-Hole--Light-Hole Mixing and Spin--Photon Coupling in Germanium Flopping-Mode Qubits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29325)
*Jose Reina-Gálvez,Guido Burkard*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper studies intrinsic spin-orbit interaction in germanium, enabling electrically active flopping-mode qubits without magnetic-field gradients. Using a multiband Luttinger-Kohn framework for double quantum dots that treats heavy-hole (HH) and light-hole (LH) states equally, the authors show that finite vertical confinement induces HH-LH mixing, which activates new electric-dipole spin-coupling channels (LH-LH and LH-HH transitions). These additional channels can yield larger spin-photon figures of merit than the conventional HH-HH transition, making HH-LH mixing a tunable resource for qubit-cavity coupling in germanium hole-spin qubits and extending circuit-QED operating regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work aims to leverage intrinsic spin-orbit coupling in germanium to realize electrically active flopping-mode qubits without requiring external magnetic-field gradients, and to explore how heavy-hole/light-hole mixing can enhance spin-charge hybridization and spin-photon coupling in circuit-QED architectures.

Method: The authors employ a multiband Luttinger-Kohn framework that retains both heavy-hole (HH) and light-hole (LH) states on equal footing for modeling double quantum dots (DQDs). Vertical confinement is modeled with a finite confinement potential along the growth direction, bringing selected subbands into a regime of appreciable HH-LH mixing. The analysis examines electric-dipole spin resonance channels activated by this mixing, including LH-LH and LH-HH transitions, alongside the conventional HH-HH pseudospin-flip resonance, and evaluates spin-photon figures of merit for each channel.

Result: For the parameter sets considered, the HH-LH mixing-activated channels (LH-LH and LH-HH transitions) can produce larger spin-photon figures of merit than the conventional HH-HH transition, demonstrating that the additional coupling channels notably enhance qubit-cavity coupling strength.

Conclusion: HH-LH mixing provides a tunable ingredient for qubit-cavity coupling in germanium hole-spin qubits, extending the operating regimes available to circuit-QED architectures beyond what conventional HH-HH transitions alone can achieve.

Abstract: Intrinsic spin--orbit interaction in germanium provides electrically active flopping-mode qubits without requiring magnetic-field gradients to generate spin--charge hybridization. We study double quantum dots (DQDs) with a multiband Luttinger--Kohn framework that retains heavy-hole (HH) and light-hole (LH) states on equal footing. Modeling vertical confinement with a finite confinement potential along the growth direction brings selected subbands into a regime of appreciable HH--LH mixing. This admixture activates electric-dipole spin-coupling through LH--LH and LH--HH transitions, complementing the conventional HH--HH pseudospin-flip resonance. For the parameter sets considered, these additional channels can produce larger spin--photon figures of merit than the HH--HH transition. HH--LH mixing thus supplies a tunable ingredient for qubit--cavity coupling in germanium hole-spin qubits and extends the operating regimes available to circuit-QED architectures.

</details>


### [136] [Extreme Polarization of the Optical Gap and High-Energy Exciton Landscape in CrSBr](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29566)
*Sayantan Patra,Sourabh Jain,Bhumika Chauhan,Marie-Christin Heißenbüttel,Abhisek Saidarsan,Ranjuna M. K.,Kseniia Mosina,Zdeněk Sofer,Michael Rohlfing,Thorsten Deilmann,Ashish Arora*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper reveals a strongly anisotropic excitonic landscape in CrSBr using optical absorption spectroscopy and GW-BSE ab initio calculations, reporting an in-plane optical gap anisotropy of 470 ± 15 meV — the highest observed for any material in the near-infrared-to-visible range. Multiple strongly polarized excitons spanning 1.25–3.1 eV were identified, along with a coupled trion/exciton pair, establishing CrSBr as a promising polarization-selective excitonic system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the anisotropic excitonic properties of CrSBr, a material of interest for polarization-selective optoelectronics, and to establish the extent of its in-plane optical gap anisotropy through combined experimental and theoretical approaches.

Method: Optical absorption spectroscopy combined with GW-Bethe-Salpeter equation (GW-BSE) ab initio calculations on monolayer and bulk-like CrSBr. Polarization-resolved measurements were performed to access excitons along the two in-plane eigenaxes, and broadband dielectric functions were extracted.

Result: The lowest bright optical onsets X0a and X0b yielded an in-plane optical gap anisotropy of 470 ± 15 meV, the highest recorded for any material in the near-IR to visible range. Multiple strongly polarized excitons were identified between 1.25 eV and 3.1 eV. A trion resonance X- located 24 meV below X0b shows oscillator strength transfer consistent with a coupled trion (Fermi-polaron)/exciton pair. Polarization-resolved broadband dielectric functions were also obtained.

Conclusion: CrSBr is established as a strongly polarization-selective excitonic system with exceptional in-plane optical gap anisotropy, highlighting its potential for polarization-selective optoelectronic applications.

Abstract: We reveal a strongly anisotropic excitonic landscape in monolayer and bulk-like CrSBr using optical absorption spectroscopy and $GW$-Bethe-Salpeter equation $\textit{ab initio}$ calculations. The direct absorptive determination of the lowest bright optical onsets i.e. $X_0^a$ and $X_0^b$ excitons for the two in-plane polarization eigenaxes yield an in-plane optical gap anisotropy of $470 \pm 15$ meV. This is the highest observed value for any material in the near-infrared-to-visible spectral region to the best of our knowledge. Energetically above, we identify multiple strongly polarized excitons spanning $1.25$ eV to $3.1$ eV selectively aligned along the two orthogonal axes. A resonance $X^-$, located $24$ meV below the $X_0^b$ progressively transfers oscillator strength to $X_b^0$, a behavior consistent with a coupled trion (Fermi-polarion)/exciton pair. Our experiments also provide polarization-resolved broadband dielectric functions of CrSBr. These results establish CrSBr as a strongly polarization-selective excitonic system and highlight its potential for polarization-selective optoelectronics enabled with its large optical-gap anisotropy.

</details>


### [137] [Localized orbitals and tunnel couplings from general confinement potentials in gate-defined quantum-dot arrays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29766)
*Bohdan Khromets,William Chow,Jonathan Baugh*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents a numerical localization procedure that rotates the eigenbasis of a one-particle effective orbital Hamiltonian into s-, p-, d-,...-shells of localized orbital wavefunctions for individual quantum dots in dense gate-defined multi-quantum-dot arrays. The method computes pairwise tunnel couplings directly as Hamiltonian matrix elements, and is demonstrated on a 2D triangular Si-MOS triple-quantum-dot array, including voltage dependencies and disorder effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Efficient and accurate simulation of dense gate-defined multi-quantum-dot arrays requires scalable modeling methods that can handle asymmetries and imperfections in realistic voltage-controlled confinement potentials, where existing approaches struggle with realistic device conditions.

Method: A numerical localization procedure that rotates the eigenbasis of a one-particle effective orbital Hamiltonian into s-, p-, d-,...-shells of localized orbital wavefunctions associated with individual quantum dots. Tunnel couplings are computed directly as matrix elements of the Hamiltonian in this localized basis.

Result: The procedure is demonstrated on a 2D triangular Si-MOS triple-quantum-dot array, obtaining the voltage dependencies of tunnel couplings and their distributions in the presence of disorder.

Conclusion: The method provides a scalable approach for evaluating tunnel couplings in realistic multi-quantum-dot systems, with implications for many-body calculations and relates to experimental tunnel coupling measurements.

Abstract: Efficient simulation of dense gate-defined multi-quantum-dot arrays requires accurate and scalable modeling methods, compatible with asymmetries and imperfections of realistic voltage-controlled confinement potentials. We present a numerical localization procedure that rotates the eigenbasis of a general one-particle effective orbital Hamiltonian into $s$-, $p$-, $d$-, $\ldots$-shells of localized orbital wavefunctions associated with individual quantum dots. The pairwise tunnel couplings between such states are computed directly as matrix elements of the Hamiltonian. We demonstrate this procedure on a 2D triangular Si-MOS triple-quantum-dot array by obtaining the voltage dependencies of the tunnel couplings and their distributions in the presence of disorder.
