We discuss the implications of this evaluation method on the many-body calculations, and relate it to the experimental tunnel coupling measurements.

</details>


### [138] [Josephson-Phase Reversal of Non-Bloch Andreev Propagation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29787)
*X. Z. Zhang,Hechen Ren*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A spin-orbit-coupled planar Josephson junction enables phase-controlled reversal of non-Bloch Andreev band propagation and switching of boundary accumulation between edges, achieved by reshaping the band's spin and electron-hole composition so a fixed dissipation reservoir unequally attenuates counterpropagating modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Hermitian control is typically challenging in solid-state systems because dissipation is fixed and not easily tunable, limiting the ability to manipulate non-Bloch propagation and boundary effects.

Method: The authors show that in a spin-orbit-coupled planar Josephson junction, varying only the Josephson phase reverses non-Bloch propagation of a low-energy Andreev band and switches its boundary accumulation between junction edges. The phase modifies the band's spin and electron-hole composition, leading a fixed reservoir to attenuate counterpropagating modes unequally. A weak-loss theory is used to connect this phase-controlled loss imbalance to boundary-selected complex momenta.

Result: The Josephson phase provides an in situ control knob for non-Hermitian transport, enabling reconfigurable reversal of propagation and boundary accumulation in a superconducting platform without changing the physical dissipation reservoir.

Conclusion: This work offers a practical route to reconfigurable non-Hermitian transport in superconducting systems, where loss engineering can be effectively realized through phase control rather than modifying dissipation itself.

Abstract: Non-Hermitian control is difficult in solid-state systems due to fixed dissipation. Here, we show that in a spin-orbit-coupled planar Josephson junction, changing only the Josephson phase reverses the non-Bloch propagation of a low-energy Andreev band and switches its boundary accumulation between junction edges. The phase reshapes the band's spin and electron-hole composition, causing a fixed reservoir to unequally attenuate counterpropagating modes. Weak-loss theory links this phase-controlled loss imbalance to boundary-selected complex momenta, offering an in situ route to reconfigurable non-Hermitian transport in superconducting platforms.

</details>


### [139] [Chiral superconductors and competing states across a Lifshitz transition in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29873)
*Chuanqi Zheng,Cheng Xu,Chushan Li,Chenyu Zhang,Zijing Zhang,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Hao Yang,Dandan Guan,Liang Liu,Shiyong Wang,Yaoyi Li,Hao Zheng,Canhua Liu,Jinfeng Jia,Shengwei Jiang,Zhiwen Shi,Guorui Chen,Fengcheng Wu,Yang Zhang,Tingxin Li,Xiaoxue Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper maps the normal-state fermiology around chiral superconductivity in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene using quantum oscillation measurements, revealing an electrically controlled Lifshitz transition between circular and annular quarter-metal Fermi surfaces. The study shows that Fermi-surface topology serves as a key control parameter for chiral superconductivity, with the annular-side state suppressed by a smaller perpendicular magnetic field despite comparable transition temperatures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent experiments reported signatures of chiral superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral multilayer graphene within the spin- and valley-polarized regime, but the normal-state fermiology surrounding this exotic state was not well understood. Understanding the Fermi-surface topology and electronic structure near the superconducting phases is crucial for identifying the mechanism and control parameters of chiral superconductivity.

Method: The authors performed quantum oscillation measurements to map the normal-state fermiology of rhombohedral pentalayer graphene. They tracked the Fermi surface evolution under electrical gating, extracted the electron effective mass, and measured how the Lifshitz transition boundary shifts with perpendicular magnetic field. They also performed calculations to compare chiral pairing tendencies on the two parent Fermi surfaces and computed the orbital-Zeeman pair-breaking scale for the annular state.

Result: They found an electrically controlled Lifshitz transition between a simply-connected circular quarter-metal Fermi surface and an annular (ring-shaped) quarter-metal Fermi surface. The Lifshitz boundary shifts with perpendicular magnetic field, consistent with a strongly momentum-dependent orbital magnetic moment. Approaching the transition, the electron effective mass becomes strongly enhanced, implying a nearly dispersionless band bottom. Two superconducting regions with orbital time-reversal-symmetry-breaking signatures lie on opposite sides of the Lifshitz boundary with comparable transition temperatures, but the annular-side state is suppressed by a substantially smaller perpendicular magnetic field. Calculations show comparable chiral pairing tendencies on both Fermi surfaces but a much lower orbital-Zeeman pair-breaking scale and an additional finite-momentum pairing tendency for the annular state.

Conclusion: Fermi-surface topology is identified as a key control parameter for chiral superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene. The Lifshitz transition creates a regime of exceptionally strong instability where chiral superconductivity competes with Wigner crystalline phases and reentrant quantum Hall states, and the distinct magnetic-field responses of the two superconducting regions arise from differences in their parent Fermi-surface topology.

Abstract: Rhombohedral multilayer graphene hosts a distinctive low-energy electronic structure in which strong Coulomb interactions and nontrivial quantum geometry intertwine to generate exotic quantum states. Recent experiments reported signatures of chiral superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral multilayer graphene within the spin- and valley-polarized regime. Here we map the normal-state fermiology surrounding chiral superconductivity in rhombohedral pentalayer graphene. Quantum oscillation measurements reveal an electrically controlled Lifshitz transition between a simply-connected circular quarter-metal Fermi surface and an annular quarter-metal Fermi surface. The Lifshitz boundary itself shifts with perpendicular magnetic field, consistent with the strongly momentum-dependent orbital magnetic moment of the low-energy band. Approaching the transition from either side, the electron effective mass becomes strongly enhanced, implying the formation of a nearly dispersionless band bottom and a strongly reduced kinetic-energy scale. This singular electronic structure produces a regime of exceptionally strong instability in which chiral superconductivity competes with Wigner crystalline phases and reentrant quantum Hall states. In particular, two superconducting regions with signatures of orbital time-reversal-symmetry breaking lie on opposite sides of the Lifshitz boundary and have comparable transition temperatures, yet the annular-side state is suppressed by a substantially smaller perpendicular magnetic field. Our calculation finds comparable chiral pairing tendencies on the two parent Fermi surfaces while producing a much lower orbital-Zeeman pair-breaking scale and an additional finite-momentum pairing tendency for the annular state. These results identify Fermi-surface topology as a key control parameter for chiral superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene.

</details>


### [140] [Universal tuning of Förster resonance energy transfer in gate-programmable conductor-dielectric-conductor heterostructures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30150)
*Alexis J. Agosto,Daniel Gunlycke,Michael N. Leuenberger*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a quantum-electrodynamical (QED) theory for tuning spontaneous emission and FRET in a conductor-dielectric-conductor heterostructure, where the dielectric spacer thickness W and gate-tunable 2D conductors enable universal control of near-field electromagnetic coupling via reflection phase branches (Dirichlet/PEC vs Neumann/PMC-like), yielding exponentially screened or logarithmically enhanced FRET depending on the branch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a material-agnostic platform and theoretical framework for universally tuning spontaneous emission and Förster resonance energy transfer (FRET) in nanophotonic systems, using gate-tunable two-dimensional conductors separated by a dielectric spacer, with only the reflection amplitudes of the surrounding sheets as microscopic input.

Method: Starting from the QED photon propagator, the authors derive the retarded Maxwell dyadic, vacuum/Hadamard field propagator, and time-ordered Feynman propagator in the conductor-dielectric-conductor geometry. The FRET rate is formulated via the retarded/advanced product G_R * Im(α_A) * G_A, and the theory is analyzed in transparent, Dirichlet/PEC (r_TM→-1), and Neumann/PMC-like (r_TM→1⁻) limits, with graphene-Er implementations separating dissipative (absorptive) from dispersive (reactive) coupling channels.

Result: The near-field FRET recovers the bulk x_ρ⁻⁶ law in the transparent limit; in the Dirichlet/PEC branch it becomes exponentially screened as exp(-2πx_ρ/W); in the Neumann/PMC-like branch a nearly gapless mode produces a quasi-2D logarithmic propagator enhancing coupling over a gate-programmable range x_ρ,* ~ W/(1-r_TM). For graphene-Er, a plasmonic band gap can suppress dissipative Er-to-graphene decay while preserving dispersive Er-Er virtual-plasmon coupling.

Conclusion: A universal QED framework for a material-agnostic conductor-dielectric-conductor heterostructure enables programmable control of spontaneous emission and FRET across distinct reflection branches, offering both exponential screening and long-range logarithmic enhancement of donor-acceptor coupling, as well as selective suppression of dissipative losses in realistic graphene-Er systems.

Abstract: We develop a quantum-electrodynamical theory for universal tuning of spontaneous emission and Förster resonance energy transfer (FRET) in a material-agnostic conductor-dielectric-conductor heterostructure. The platform consists of a dielectric spacer of thickness $W$ bounded by two gate-tunable two-dimensional conductors. The only microscopic input from the surrounding materials is the transverse-magnetic and transverse-electric reflection amplitudes $r_{\TM/\TE}(q_ρ,ω)$ of the two sheets. Starting from the QED photon propagator, we derive the retarded Maxwell dyadic, the vacuum/Hadamard field propagator, and the time-ordered Feynman propagator in the same geometry. The spontaneous-emission rate is controlled by the local vacuum spectral density, while FRET is controlled by the nonlocal retarded/advanced product $\obG_{\text R}(\bx_D,\bx_A;ω_D)\Im\,\balpha_A(ω_D)\obG_{\text A}(\bx_A,\bx_D;ω_D)$. In the transparent limit $r_{\TM}\to 0$, the near-field FRET rate recovers the bulk $x_ρ^{-6}$ law. In the Dirichlet/PEC branch $r_{\TM}\to -1$, the gapless transverse mode is removed and the donor-acceptor coupling acquires a Bessel-$K$ envelope, giving an exponentially screened FRET rate $Γ_{D\to A}\propto\exp(-2πx_ρ/W)$ at large lateral separation. In the opposite Neumann/PMC-like branch $r_{\TM}\to 1^{-}$, a nearly gapless transverse mode survives and produces a wide quasi-two-dimensional logarithmic propagator, enhancing the nonlocal electromagnetic coupling over a gate-programmable range $x_{ρ,*}\sim W/(1-r_{\TM})$. For graphene-Er implementations, the same retarded Green tensor also separates dissipative on-shell Er-to-graphene decay, governed by its absorptive part, from dispersive virtual-plasmon-mediated Er-Er coupling, governed by its reactive part; a plasmonic band gap can suppress the former while retaining the latter.

</details>


### [141] [Switchable Magnetoelectric Transport in Graphene via a Van der Waals Multiferroic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30170)
*Miuko Tanaka,Shunta Aoki,Ikoi Sato,Hao Ou,Itishree Pradhan,Ngoc Han Tu,Yangsong Chen,Tomohiro Ishii,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Michihisa Yamamoto,Masayuki Hashisaka,Jiang Pu,Naoki Ogawa,Toshiya Ideue*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates the first realization of gate-switchable magnetoelectric transport in a van der Waals heterostructure combining graphene with the multiferroic CuCrP2S6. The device exhibits polarization-dependent hysteresis in the charge-neutrality resistance peak of graphene, magnetic-field-induced polarization modulation, and electric-field-controlled domain switching, establishing interfacial magnetoelectric transport in van der Waals heterostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electric and magnetic control of transport at atomic interfaces is essential for next-generation electronics and spintronics. While van der Waals multiferroics—which host simultaneous dielectric and magnetic orders down to monolayer thickness—offer a promising platform, electronic functionalities exploiting their unique attributes had not been realized prior to this work.

Method: The authors construct a van der Waals heterostructure of graphene on the multiferroic CuCrP2S6. They measure magnetoelectric transport in graphene, observing the charge-neutrality resistance peak hysteresis from ferroelectric polarization flip. They apply in-plane magnetic fields to detect magnetic-field-induced polarization changes (magnetoelectric coupling), and use field cooling to control multiferroic domain orientation, enabling reversible switching of interfacial transport.

Result: Gate-switchable magnetoelectric transport is demonstrated in graphene mediated by the multiferroic layer. The resistance peak shows hysteresis from polarization switching; an in-plane magnetic field shifts this peak polarization-dependently, evidencing the magnetoelectric effect. Field-cooling enables domain control and reversible switching of interfacial magnetoelectric transport.

Conclusion: This work provides the first demonstration of interfacial magnetoelectric transport in a van der Waals heterostructure and establishes a pathway for engineering 2D van der Waals interfaces for functional device applications in electronics and spintronics.

Abstract: Electric and magnetic control of transport properties at atomic interfaces is central to the development of next generation electronics and spintronics. Van der Waals multiferroics materials that simultaneously host dielectric and magnetic orders down to the monolayer limit offer a promising platform for such interfacial control, yet the realization of electronic functionalities that exploit the unique attributes of van der Waals multiferroics has largely remained elusive. Here, we realize a van der Waals heterostructure comprising graphene and the multiferroic CuCrP2S6, enabling gate-switchable magnetoelectric transport in graphene, mediated by the multiferroic layer. The charge-neutrality resistance peak of graphene exhibits pronounced hysteresis arising from polarization flip in the multiferroic state. Application of an in-plane magnetic field shifts this peak in a polarization-dependent manner, revealing magnetic-field-induced polarization modulation a direct signature of the magnetoelectric effect. Furthermore, cooling the device under an applied electric field enables domain control of the multiferroic order, allowing reversible switching of the interfacial magnetoelectric transport. These results provide the first demonstration of interfacial magnetoelectric transport in a vdW heterostructure, and establish a pathway for engineering two-dimensional van der Waals interfaces for functional device applications.

</details>


### [142] [Singlet-doublet transitions and Josephson currents in a superconducting ring with a quantum dot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30306)
*Guo-Hui Ding,Fei Ye,Bing Dong*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates a superconducting ring with an embedded quantum dot using a variational wave-function approach, revealing singlet-doublet ground state transitions in the mixed valence regime, with supercurrents showing flux-dependent oscillations and abrupt jumps at phase transition points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the ground state properties of a finite-U Anderson impurity coupled to a superconducting ring, particularly the quantum phase transitions in the mixed valence regime and their effect on supercurrent behavior.

Method: A variational wave-function approach is used to formulate a theoretical treatment of the finite-U Anderson impurity coupled with a superconducting ring.

Result: The ground state exhibits singlet-doublet transitions in the mixed valence regime. The supercurrent in the superconducting ring oscillates with the external magnetic flux and shows abrupt jumps at the singlet-doublet phase transition points.

Conclusion: The variational wave-function approach successfully captures singlet-doublet ground state transitions in a quantum dot embedded superconducting ring, and these transitions are manifested as discontinuities in the magnetic flux-dependent supercurrent oscillations.

Abstract: We investigate the ground state properties of a superconducting ring embedded with a quantum dot (QD) by using a variational wave-function approach. A theoretical formulation for the treatment of the finite-U Anderson impurity coupled with a superconducting ring are presented. We demonstrate singlet-doublet transitions of the ground state for this system with the QD in the mixed valence regime. It is shown that the supercurrent in the superconductor ring shows oscillations with the external enclosed magnetic flux and exhibits abrupt jumps at the singlet-doublet phase transition points.

</details>


### [143] [Scalar Spin Chirality from Dissipative Pumping and Lamb Shift Precession](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30324)
*YuanDong Wang,JianHua Wei*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper explains the previously unclear origin of repeatedly reversing circulating currents in triangular triple quantum dots under bias even at zero magnetic flux and for a real Hamiltonian. The authors show that dissipative tunneling prepares an orbital pseudospin while virtual charge fluctuations generate a noncollinear Lamb field that rotates it chirally, and bias changes their relative orientation causing current reversal. This "orbital Hanle effect" converts nonchiral orbital polarization into scalar spin chirality, linked to the circulating current via an exact ground-multiplet identity. Hierarchical-equations-of-motion calculations confirm the low-bias reversal survives beyond weak-coupling theory, and the quadratic growth of chirality after a sudden voltage switch confirms precession dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The phenomenon of bias-reversing circulating currents in triangular triple quantum dots at zero magnetic flux and with a real Hamiltonian has remained unexplained despite being observed. The authors aim to clarify the physical origin of this counterintuitive time-reversal-odd response emerging in a seemingly time-reversal-symmetric open quantum system.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework involving an orbital pseudospin description, where dissipative tunneling prepares the pseudospin and virtual charge fluctuations generate a noncollinear Lamb field. They connect the resulting orbital Hanle effect to scalar spin chirality via an exact ground-multiplet identity, and validate their theory using hierarchical equations of motion (HEOM) calculations beyond second-order weak-coupling perturbation theory, including analysis of the transient dynamics after a sudden voltage switch.

Result: The mechanism correctly reproduces the repeated current reversal with bias. The low-bias reversal persists in HEOM calculations beyond the weak-coupling limit. The quadratic temporal growth of chirality following a voltage switch confirms the precessional (Hanle-type) origin of the effect. The orbital Hanle effect converts a nonchiral orbital polarization into scalar spin chirality that drives the circulating current.

Conclusion: Time-reversal-odd responses can arise in open quantum systems from the interplay between dissipative state preparation and coherent precession, offering a general principle for emergent chiral behavior without external magnetic fields. This reservoir-induced orbital Hanle effect provides a broadly applicable mechanism for generating such responses.

Abstract: Circulating currents in triangular triple quantum dots reverse repeatedly with bias even at zero magnetic flux and for a real Hamiltonian, a phenomenon whose origin has remained unclear. We show that dissipative tunneling prepares an orbital pseudospin, while virtual charge fluctuations generate a noncollinear Lamb field that rotates it toward the chiral direction. Bias changes their relative orientation and thereby reverses the current. This reservoir-induced orbital Hanle effect converts a nonchiral orbital polarization into scalar spin chirality, which an exact ground-multiplet identity links to the circulating current. Hierarchical-equations-of-motion calculations show that the low-bias reversal survives beyond the second-order weak-coupling description, while the quadratic growth of chirality after a sudden voltage switch identifies precession. More broadly, time-reversal-odd responses can emerge in open quantum systems from the interplay between dissipative state preparation and coherent precession.

</details>


### [144] [Evanescent-mode Casimir-Josephson force and gate-controlled resonances in ballistic graphene Josephson junctions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30438)
*Shahrukh Salim*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a microscopic scattering theory for the phase-dependent equilibrium mechanical response (force correction) of a ballistic superconductor-graphene-superconductor Josephson junction, using an energy-dependent Dirac scattering matrix embedded in a normalized Matsubara determinant. It derives a closed-form evanescent-mode result at charge neutrality, analyzes gate-induced propagating channels and Fabry-Pérot oscillations, and distinguishes interband/intraband from evanescent/propagating crossovers without treating Andreev state labels as separate thermodynamic forces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a complete and microscopic description of the phase-dependent mechanical force in a graphene-based Josephson junction, capturing the full Bogoliubov-de Gennes spectrum, and to clarify the distinction between various crossover regimes and approximation schemes in this Dirac-material system.

Method: The calculation uses an energy-dependent Dirac scattering matrix embedded in a normalized Matsubara determinant that contains the complete Bogoliubov-de Gennes spectrum. Exact real-energy subgap poles from the same scattering matrix are used to diagnose specular/interband and retro/intraband character. The complete determinant result is compared against the frozen-scattering short-junction approximation.

Result: At charge neutrality, the force correction approaches a closed-form evanescent-mode result proportional to ΔW/L², reaching 2ln2 ΔW/(πL²) at phase π in the zero-temperature short-junction limit. Gate doping introduces propagating channels and Fabry-Pérot structure, producing large oscillations and sign reversals of δF. The interband-to-intraband and evanescent-to-propagating crossovers are controlled by |μ|/Δ and |μ|L/(ℏvF), respectively.

Conclusion: The phase- and gate-dependent mechanical force provides a Dirac-material extension of earlier superconductivity-induced mechanical-force proposals, with clear crossover physics and a quantitative distinction between the full determinant result and the frozen-scattering short-junction approximation.

Abstract: We develop a microscopic scattering theory of the phase-dependent equilibrium mechanical response of a ballistic superconductor-graphene-superconductor Josephson junction. The calculation is based on an energy-dependent Dirac scattering matrix embedded in a normalized Matsubara determinant, so the reported force correction contains the complete Bogoliubov-de Gennes spectrum of the stated ideal model. At charge neutrality the full determinant approaches a closed-form evanescent-mode result proportional to $ΔW/L^2$, reaching $2\ln 2\,ΔW/(πL^2)$ at phase difference $π$ in the zero-temperature short-junction limit. Gate doping produces propagating channels and Fabry-Pérot structure, causing large oscillations and sign reversals of the phase-dependent force correction $δF=F(φ)-F(0)$. We distinguish the interband-to-intraband Andreev crossover, controlled by $|μ|/Δ$, from the evanescent-to-propagating crossover, controlled by $|μ|L/(\hbar v_F)$. Exact real-energy subgap poles obtained from the same energy-dependent scattering matrix are used to diagnose specular/interband and retro/intraband character; these labels are not treated as separately measurable thermodynamic forces in the mixed regime. We also quantify the difference between the complete determinant result and the frozen-scattering short-junction approximation without identifying that difference with a pure continuum force. The resulting gate- and phase-dependent mechanical signal provides a Dirac-material extension of earlier superconductivity-induced mechanical-force proposals.

</details>


### [145] [Dynamics of a nanoscale ferromagnetic vortex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30511)
*Jun Seok Seo,Se Kwon Kim*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a ferromagnetic vortex stabilized by interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (iDMI), demonstrating via theory and micromagnetic simulations that this vortex remains stable in nanoscale ferromagnetic disks with radii below 5 nm—far smaller than conventional nanodot vortices (~1 μm). The authors analytically solve the nonlinear equation of motion for anharmonic vortex oscillation, identify the critical frequency for driven oscillation stability, and show the vortex exhibits both conventional properties (gyrotropic oscillation, resonance shift under out-of-plane field) and unconventional behaviors (strongly anharmonic potential, nonlinear oscillations, Duffing-oscillator-like response).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional magnetic vortices in nanodots require relatively large disks (~1 μm radius), limiting their application in miniaturized spintronic devices. The authors seek to create a vortex structure that can remain stable at the nanoscale by leveraging interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction to stabilize a much smaller vortex, enabling new possibilities for high-density magnetic storage and nanoscale microwave devices.

Method: The work combines theoretical analysis with micromagnetic simulations. The authors analytically solve the nonlinear equation of motion describing anharmonic vortex oscillation and derive the critical frequency governing the stability of driven oscillation. They investigate both linear (gyrotropic oscillation, frequency shifts under out-of-plane fields) and nonlinear (anharmonic potential, Duffing response under AC bias) behaviors of the iDMI-stabilized nanoscale vortex.

Result: The iDMI-stabilized vortex remains stable in nanoscale ferromagnetic disks with radii below 5 nm, orders of magnitude smaller than conventional vortices (~1 μm). The vortex shows conventional gyrotropic oscillation and out-of-plane field-induced resonance frequency shifts, while also displaying unconventional strongly anharmonic potential, nonlinear oscillations, and Duffing-oscillator-like response under AC excitation. The critical frequency for driven oscillation stability is identified analytically.

Conclusion: The interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction enables stable magnetic vortices at the nanoscale, dramatically shrinking the required device size while preserving useful vortex properties and adding nonlinear dynamics that could be exploited in nanoscale spintronic and microwave applications.

Abstract: We propose a ferromagnetic vortex stabilized by the interfacial Dzyaloshinskii--Moriya interaction (iDMI) and investigate its properties through theoretical analysis and micromagnetic simulations. Our results demonstrate that this vortex can remain stable even in nanoscale ferromagnetic disks with radii below $5\,\text{nm}$---far smaller than those of conventional nanodot vortices having about $1\,μ\text{m}$ radius. We analytically solve the nonlinear equation of motion describing the anharmonic vortex oscillation, and identify the critical frequency that determines the stability of the driven oscillation of the vortex. This nanoscale vortex exhibits conventional properties of microscale vortices, including gyrotropic oscillation and resonance frequency shift under an out-of-plane magnetic field. It also exhibits unconventional behaviors, such as a strongly anharmonic potential, nonlinear oscillations, and a Duffing-oscillator-like response under the external AC bias.

</details>


### [146] [Moiré-induced lattice reconstruction at buried atomic interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30531)
*Nicholas Clark,Frederick Allars,Isaac Soltero,Wendong Wang,David Hopkinson,Hugo de Latour,James G. McHugh,William Talbott,Sam Sullivan-Allsop,Rongsheng Cai,Astrid Weston,Xiao Li,Gareth Tainton,Casey K. Cheung,Francisco Selles,Alex Summerfield,Andrey Kretinin,Christopher S. Allen,Vladimir Falko,Sarah J. Haigh,Roman Gorbachev*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper uses multi-slice electron ptychography to non-invasively recover the three-dimensional atomic structure at marginally twisted rhombohedral interfaces between thick transition-metal dichalcogenide crystals, revealing strong in-plane interfacial reconstruction suppressed of out-of-plane bending, and extracting strain tensor evolution during reconstruction decay into the bulk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While lattice reconstruction in suspended twisted bilayers is well understood, structural changes at van der Waals heterointerfaces between multilayer crystals remain largely unexplored, despite their importance for emergent properties like sliding ferroelectricity.

Method: Multi-slice electron ptychography is used to non-invasively recover the three-dimensional atomic structure at marginally twisted rhombohedral interfaces between thick transition-metal dichalcogenide crystals, achieving ~3 pm position precision and ~1 nm depth resolution, with three-dimensional modeling for comparison.

Result: The method resolves twist-induced lattice reconstruction fields per layer and the resulting dislocation network at the buried interface. Markedly strong in-plane interfacial reconstruction is observed due to suppression of out-of-plane bending by outer layers, exceeding model predictions. The strain tensor evolution during reconstruction decay into the bulk is also extracted.

Conclusion: The findings provide a structural foundation for understanding multi-layer moiré systems and reveal that bulk-like environments can enhance interfacial reconstruction by suppressing out-of-plane relaxation.

Abstract: Atomic reconstruction at twisted two-dimensional interfaces governs many of their emergent optical, electronic, and mechanical properties, including sliding ferroelectricity. Despite recent progress in understanding lattice reconstruction in suspended twisted bilayers, structural changes at van der Waals heterointerfaces between multilayer crystals remain largely unexplored. Here we use multi-slice electron ptychography to non-invasively recover the three-dimensional atomic structure at marginally twisted rhombohedral interfaces between thick transition-metal dichalcogenide crystals. With a position precision of ~3 pm and a depth resolution ~1 nm, we resolve the twist-induced lattice reconstruction field per layer, and the resulting dislocation network at the buried interface. Despite the bulk nature, we observe markedly strong in-plane interfacial reconstruction due to suppression of the out-of-plane bending by outer layers, exceeding predictions from our three-dimensional modelling. Furthermore, we extract the strain tensor evolution during the decay of the reconstruction into the bulk, providing a structural foundation for understanding multi-layer moiré systems.

</details>


### [147] [Exact fluctuation relations in voltage- and temperature-biased Laughlin-edge constrictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30535)
*Gu Zhang,Gabriele Campagnano,Domenico Giuliano,Igor Gornyi,In`es Safi*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper presents a comprehensive analysis of non-equilibrium fluctuation-dissipation relations connecting chiral-current auto- and cross-correlations to tunneling-current noise and conductance in Laughlin edge states coupled through a quantum point contact (QPC). The relations remain exact for arbitrary tunneling strength, voltage bias, and edge-state temperatures, and are generalized to spatially extended QPCs and voltage-dependent tunneling amplitudes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish that experimentally accessible chiral-current correlations can reliably reconstruct tunneling-current noise at a QPC in Laughlin edge states, providing a robust route to characterize non-equilibrium transport in chiral edge systems.

Method: The authors derive and analyze non-equilibrium fluctuation-dissipation relations for chiral Laughlin edges coupled through a QPC, considering edges at different temperatures and voltages, and generalize to spatially extended QPCs and explicitly voltage-dependent tunneling amplitudes.

Result: The fluctuation-dissipation relations remain exact for arbitrary tunneling strength, voltage bias, and edge-state temperatures. The relations hold for the general cases considered, with conditions and limitations of the generalizations discussed.

Conclusion: The local tunneling-current noise at a QPC can be reliably reconstructed from experimentally accessible downstream auto- and cross-correlations, establishing a robust methodology for characterizing non-equilibrium transport in chiral edge states.

Abstract: We present a comprehensive analysis of non-equilibrium fluctuation-dissipation relations connect- ing experimentally accessible chiral-current auto- and cross-correlations to the tunneling-current noise and conductance in Laughlin edge states coupled through a quantum point contact (QPC). We examine their validity for two chiral Laughlin edges held at different temperatures and voltages and show that the relations remain exact for arbitrary tunneling strength, voltage bias, and edge- state temperatures. We further generalize them to spatially extended QPCs and to tunneling am- plitudes with an explicit voltage dependence, and discuss the conditions and limitations associated with these generalizations. Our results establish that the local tunneling-current noise generated at the QPC can be reliably reconstructed from experimentally accessible auto- and cross-correlations measured downstream, providing a robust route to characterize non-equilibrium transport in chiral edge states.

</details>


### [148] [Predictive wavelength tailoring of uniform GaSb-based quantum dots for emission at 1.55 um](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30628)
*Markus Peil,Maja Wasiluk,Ziemowit Olinkiewicz,Tymon Przychodni,Robert Matysiak,Teemu Taskinen,Joona Salonen,Abhiroop Chellu,Metin Patli,Joonas Hilska,Anna Musiał,Michał Gawełczyk,Mircea Guina,Teemu Hakkarainen*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper presents a detailed study of emission wavelength tailoring in GaSb-based quantum dots (QDs) formed by InGaSb-filling of droplet-etched nanoholes in AlGaSb. The emission wavelength can be tuned from 1.48 μm to the telecom C-band (1.55 μm) by independently varying QD composition and size, with experimental energy shifts of -4.4 meV/In-percentage and -2.0 meV/ML, well-matched by simulations. The QDs exhibit excellent uniformity (inhomogeneous broadening <7 meV), narrow single-QD excitonic lines (13.8±6.7 μeV), and low fine-structure splitting (<10 μeV), positioning them as a tunable telecom platform for scalable quantum-photonic applications over long-haul optical fiber networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop and characterize a tunable GaSb-based droplet-etched quantum dot platform operating in the telecom C-band, addressing the need for scalable quantum light sources with precise wavelength control for long-haul quantum communication networks.

Method: GaSb-based QDs are fabricated by filling droplet-etched nanoholes in AlGaSb with InGaSb. The emission wavelength is tailored by independently varying the In-content of the QD material and the number of monolayers used for filling. Experimental photoluminescence measurements are compared with simulations that assume uniform In composition, low intermixing, and microscopically measured QD geometries. Ensemble uniformity and single-QD optical properties are characterized via photoluminescence spectroscopy, including inhomogeneous broadening, excitonic linewidths, and fine-structure splitting.

Result: Emission wavelength was tuned from 1.48 μm to 1.55 μm (telecom C-band). Optical transition energy shifts linearly at -4.4 meV/In-percentage and -2.0 meV/ML experimentally, matching simulated rates of -4.3 meV/In-percentage and -2.1 meV/ML. All samples showed excellent ensemble uniformity with inhomogeneous broadening below 7 meV. Single-QD measurements revealed narrow excitonic emission lines of 13.8±6.7 μeV and fine-structure splitting values below 10 μeV.

Conclusion: GaSb-based local droplet epitaxy (LDE) QDs are identified as a tunable telecom platform offering precise wavelength control, exceptional uniformity, and excellent single-photon optical quality, making them suitable for scaling quantum-photonic applications over long-haul optical fiber networks.

Abstract: A detailed study of emission wavelength tailoring of GaSb-based QDs formed by InGaSb-filling of droplet-etched nanoholes in AlGaSb is presented. The study shows that the emission wavelength can be modified from 1.48 um to the center of the telecom C-band at 1.55 mm by independently varying the QD composition and size. More specifically, the optical transition energy shifts linearly as a function of In-content of the QD material at a rate of -4.4 meV/In-percentage, and with the number of monolayers (ML) of material used for filling the nanoholes, at -2.0 meV/ML. These experimentally observed energy shifts are well predicted by simulations yielding rates of -4.3 meV/In-percentage and -2.1 meV/ML, respectively. For the simulation, a uniform In composition, low intermixing, and microscopically measured QD geometry is considered. Additionally, excellent ensemble QD uniformity, with unprecedented inhomogeneous broadening well-below 7 meV across all samples is demonstrated. Finally, photoluminescence of single-QDs reveals narrow excitonic emission lines of 13.8+/-6.7 ueV and low fine-structure splitting values reaching <10 ueV. These results identify GaSb-based LDE QDs as a tunable telecom platform for scaling quantum-photonic applications over long-haul optical fiber networks.

</details>


### [149] [Coexistence of polariton bound states in the continuum and 2D radiative excitons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30691)
*Simone Zanotti,Marco Liscidini,Dario Gerace,Lucio C. Andreani*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates that a non-Hermitian Hopfield formalism that incorporates both photonic and excitonic radiative losses can correctly describe genuine k=0 polariton bound states in the continuum (BICs) with diverging radiative lifetimes, providing a unified microscopic framework for radiative excitons and polariton BICs in periodically patterned planar waveguides coupled to 2D excitonic materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whether the Hopfield formalism can consistently account for intrinsic wavevector-dependent radiative decay of 2D excitons within the light cone while preserving polariton BICs has remained an open question, since 2D excitons undergo intrinsic radiative decay that could potentially disrupt BIC formation.

Method: The authors generalize the non-Hermitian Hopfield matrix to incorporate independent photonic and excitonic losses, including wavevector-dependent radiative decay of 2D excitons, and diagonalize it to study polaritonic states in periodically patterned planar waveguides embedding 2D excitonic layers.

Result: The non-Hermitian Hopfield formalism incorporating excitonic radiative losses correctly captures the existence of genuine k=0 polariton BICs with diverging radiative lifetimes.

Conclusion: The theory provides a unified microscopic framework for radiative excitons and polariton BICs, serving as an efficient predictive tool for designing photonic-crystal platforms coupled to quantum wells, transition-metal dichalcogenides, and other 2D excitonic materials.

Abstract: Bound states in the continuum (BICs) enable optical modes with ideally infinite radiative lifetimes despite lying within the radiation continuum. Radiation-matter interaction in periodically patterned planar waveguides embedding two-dimensional (2D) optically active excitations can be described quantum mechanically by diagonalizing a non-Hermitian operator, known as the Hopfield matrix, which can be generalized to incorporate independent photonic and excitonic losses into the polaritonic states. However, since 2D excitons undergo intrinsic wavevector-dependent radiative decay within the light cone, whether the Hopfield formalism can consistently account for this process while preserving polariton BICs has remained an open question. Here we show that a non-Hermitian Hopfield formalism incorporating excitonic radiative losses correctly captures the existence of genuine $k=0$ polariton BICs with diverging radiative lifetimes. The theory provides a unified microscopic framework for radiative excitons and polariton BICs, and an efficient predictive tool for designing photonic-crystal platforms coupled to quantum wells, transition-metal dichalcogenides, and other 2D excitonic materials.

</details>


### [150] [Supermoiré Reconstruction and Topological Mosaics in Twisted Trilayer WSe$_2$ and MoTe$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30813)
*Hai Meng,Yang Xu,Fengcheng Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates lattice relaxation and band structures of helical and alternating twisted trilayer WSe2 and MoTe2 using machine-learning force fields and large-scale ab initio calculations, revealing that supermoiré lattices reconstruct into dominant domain types with distinct valley Chern numbers and topologically inequivalent domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how interference between two bilayer moiré lattices in twisted trilayer transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) affects lattice relaxation and electronic band structure, particularly in systems lacking C2z symmetry where symmetric domains become energetically distinct.

Method: Uses machine-learning force fields and large-scale ab initio calculations to investigate lattice relaxation and band structures of helical and alternating twisted trilayer WSe2 and MoTe2.

Result: Supermoiré lattices reconstruct into a few dominant domain types with locally commensurate bilayer moiré lattices. Domains otherwise related by C2z symmetry become energetically and topologically distinct (unlike in twisted trilayer graphene). The topmost valence bands originate from K/K' valleys and carry domain-dependent valley Chern numbers, forming a mosaic of topologically inequivalent domains.

Conclusion: The resulting supermoiré lattice hosts a mosaic of topologically inequivalent domains, offering a platform for exploring correlated and topological physics in twisted trilayer TMD systems.

Abstract: We investigate lattice relaxation and band structures of helical and alternating twisted trilayer WSe$_2$ and MoTe$_2$ using machine-learning force fields and large-scale ab initio calculations. Interference between the two bilayer moiré lattices generates a supermoiré lattice that, upon relaxation, reconstructs into a few dominant domain types with locally commensurate bilayer moiré lattices. Because the systems lack $C_{2z}$ symmetry, domains otherwise related by this symmetry become energetically and topologically distinct, unlike in twisted trilayer graphene. Band structure calculations show that the topmost valence bands originate from the $K$ ($K'$) valleys and carry domain-dependent valley Chern numbers. The resulting supermoiré lattice hosts a mosaic of topologically inequivalent domains, offering a platform for exploring correlated and topological physics.

</details>


### [151] [Local phase-space Berry curvature and Hall transport in textured twisted bilayer graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30882)
*Tohid Farajollahpour*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a local analytic framework for textured moiré Dirac materials, showing that a purely geometric tetrad/shift sector alone does not produce mixed Berry curvature once spin connection is treated consistently, but a projected textured mini-Dirac cone in twisted bilayer graphene does acquire genuine mixed phase-space curvature. In a Hall-bar limit with 1D texture and mirror symmetry, pseudogauge gradients renormalize longitudinal conductivity only, while a dc nonlinear Hall response requires an additional valley-odd tilt generating a Berry-curvature dipole. Both responses peak at the same local filling √2·m and all parameters are extracted from a heterostrained Bistritzer-MacDonald model at 1.3°.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how slow twist-angle and heterostrain textures in twisted bilayer graphene generate phase-space Berry geometry, and to provide a clean separation of geometric, texture-induced, and transport-level ingredients relevant to realistic TBG devices.

Method: Analytical treatment of a textured mini-Dirac cone in TBG with consistent spin connection for the tetrad/shift sector; analysis of a controlled local Hall-bar limit with one-dimensional texture and mirror Mx symmetry; numerical parameter extraction from a heterostrained Bistritzer-MacDonald model at θ=1.3°.

Result: A purely geometric tetrad/shift sector does not generate mixed Berry curvature with consistent spin connection, while a projected textured mini-Dirac cone does acquire genuine mixed phase-space curvature. Pseudogauge gradient renormalizes only the longitudinal conductivity along the textured direction; dc nonlinear Hall response appears only with an additional valley-odd tilt creating a Berry-curvature dipole. Both responses peak at local filling √2·m. The required tilt corresponds to heterostrain of only 0.03%-0.2%, below values routinely imaged in devices.

Conclusion: The theory provides a local analytic framework for textured moiré Dirac materials, cleanly separating geometric, texture-induced, and transport-level contributions, with a gate-tunable fingerprint (peak at μ_loc = √2·m) identifying the common origin of the geometric responses in realistic TBG.

Abstract: Slow twist-angle and heterostrain textures in twisted bilayer graphene provide a natural route to phase-space Berry geometry. We show that a purely geometric tetrad/shift sector does not generate mixed Berry curvature once the spin connection is treated consistently. By contrast, a projected textured mini-Dirac cone in twisted bilayer graphene acquires genuine mixed phase-space curvature. We analyze a controlled local Hall-bar limit with a one-dimensional texture and mirror $M_x$. In that limit the pseudogauge gradient renormalizes only the longitudinal conductivity along the textured direction, while a dc nonlinear Hall response appears only when an additional valley-odd tilt generates a Berry-curvature dipole. Both geometric responses peak at the same local filling, $μ_{\rm loc}=\sqrt{2}\,m$, providing a gate-tunable fingerprint of their common origin. All local-cone parameters and their texture susceptibilities are extracted from a heterostrained Bistritzer-MacDonald model at $θ=1.3^\circ$. The tilt invoked in the transport estimates corresponds to heterostrain of only $0.03\%-0.2\%$, below values routinely imaged in devices. The resulting theory provides a local analytic framework for textured moiré Dirac materials and cleanly separates geometric, texture-induced, and transport-level ingredients relevant to realistic TBG.

</details>


### [152] [Signatures of inter-sideband coherence in the resonance fluorescence spectrum of an acoustically-modulated quantum dot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30886)
*Rafał A. Bogaczewicz,Hubert J. Krenner,Paweł Machnikowski*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper theoretically investigates phase coherence between spectral sidebands in the resonance fluorescence of an acoustically modulated quantum dot, using a filtered-field formalism for a Mach-Zehnder interferometer. It shows that the geometric slant of interferograms is a robust indicator of phase coherence that remains invariant under environmental white noise, even though noise reduces overall fringe intensity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a reliable framework for verifying single-photon coherence between spectral sidebands, which is essential for frequency-bin encoding and scalable quantum networking in realistic solid-state systems that suffer from environmental noise.

Method: The authors employ a filtered-field formalism within a Mach-Zehnder interferometric configuration to analyze the resonance fluorescence spectrum of an acoustically modulated quantum dot, examining how geometric properties of interferograms relate to inter-sideband phase coherence under white-noise environmental conditions.

Result: The geometric slant (inclination) of interferograms serves as a robust and invariant indicator of inter-sideband phase coherence, unaffected by noise-induced spectral diffusion. While environmental white noise globally suppresses fringe intensity, the characteristic inclination remains strictly preserved.

Conclusion: The framework provides a noise-resilient method for verifying single-photon coherence between spectral sidebands, offering a practical path toward frequency-bin encoding and scalable quantum networking in realistic, noisy solid-state architectures.

Abstract: We theoretically investigate the inter-sideband phase coherence within the resonance fluorescence spectrum of an acoustically modulated quantum dot using a filtered-field formalism for a Mach-Zehnder configuration. We demonstrate that geometric slant of the interferograms provides an indicator of phase coherence that is resilient to environmental white noise. Specifically, noise-induced spectral diffusion reduces the global fringe intensity, while leaving the characteristic inclination strictly invariant. Our findings establish a framework for verifying single-photon coherence between spectral sidebands, essential for frequency-bin encoding and scalable quantum networking in realistic, noisy solid-state architectures.

</details>


### [153] [Breakdown of Charge-Conjugation Symmetry of Disclinations in 2D Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30921)
*Ruslan Yamaletdinov,Mikhail I. Katsnelson,Oleg V. Yazyev*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper uses atomistic simulations to study disclinations (topological defects) in free-standing graphene, revealing that negative disclinations break charge-conjugation symmetry with sublinear energy scaling, non-saddle equilibrium shapes, and long-range attractive interactions, unlike the repulsive behavior of positive disclinations—effects further amplified by self-adhesion in folded membranes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disclinations are fundamental topological defects in 2D crystalline membranes, but their elastic properties—particularly for negative-charge defects—remain largely unexplored despite being critical for understanding defect-driven morphology and stability of graphene and other 2D materials.

Method: Atomistic simulations of free-standing graphene are used to characterize the energetics, equilibrium morphology, and pairwise interactions of both positive and negative disclinations, including systems with self-adhesion to probe folded membrane configurations.

Result: Positive disclinations behave as expected (repulsive interactions), while negative disclinations exhibit sublinear energy scaling with topological charge, equilibrium shapes deviating from the conventional saddle ansatz, and robust long-range attraction. These charge-conjugation symmetry-breaking effects are amplified in self-adhesive folded membranes.

Conclusion: The work uncovers a fundamentally distinct energetic landscape for negative-curvature defects, providing a basis for understanding stability, self-folding behavior, and defect-driven morphology of graphene and other 2D membranes.

Abstract: Disclinations are elementary topological defects in two-dimensional (2D) crystalline membranes, yet their elastic properties remain largely unexplored. Using atomistic simulations, we address the energetics, morphology, and interactions of disclinations in free-standing graphene, a prototypical 2D crystal. We find that while disclinations with positive topological charges follow an expected behavior, negative disclinations exhibit sublinear energy scaling with charge as well as equilibrium shape that deviates sharply from the conventional saddle ansatz. This breakdown of charge-conjugation symmetry leads to qualitatively distinct interactions: positive disclinations repel, whereas negative disclinations display a robust long-range attraction. These trends are shown to be further amplified by self-adhesion in folded membranes. Our results uncover a fundamentally different energetic landscape for negative curvature defects and provide a basis for understanding the stability, self-folding behavior, and defect-driven morphology of graphene and other 2D membranes.

</details>


### [154] [Interfacial Spin-to-Charge Conversion in Sputtered MoTe2 Heterostructures Probed by Spin Pumping and Spin-Torque Ferromagnetic Resonance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30953)
*J. L. Costa,E. Santos,E. L. T. França,J. B. S. Mendes,A. Azevedo*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates spin-charge interconversion in MoTe2 heterostructures using SP-FMR and ST-FMR, finding that spin-to-charge conversion and spin-torque efficiencies are essentially independent of MoTe2 thickness. This indicates the conversion is dominated by the Rashba-Edelstein effect at the Py/MoTe2 interface rather than bulk spin transport, contrasting with Pt heterostructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve whether the efficient charge-to-spin conversion in TMD Weyl semimetal phases like MoTe2 originates predominantly from the bulk or the interface, which is critical for optimizing spin-orbit torque applications.

Method: Thickness-dependent spin-pumping FMR and spin-torque FMR measurements on MoTe2/Py heterostructures, complemented by bidirectional SP-FMR and interface-separation measurements for verification.

Result: The spin-to-charge conversion and spin-torque efficiencies are large and essentially independent of MoTe2 thickness, demonstrating that the conversion is predominantly governed by the Rashba-Edelstein effect at the Py/MoTe2 interface, in contrast to the characteristic thickness dependence seen in Pt heterostructures.

Conclusion: The Py/MoTe2 interface plays a dominant role in enabling efficient spin-charge conversion and spin-orbit torques, highlighting sputtered MoTe2/Py heterostructures as promising for low-power spintronic applications such as magnetic memory and logic devices.

Abstract: Transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) and their Weyl semimetal phases, such as MoTe$_2$, have attracted significant attention for spin-orbit torque applications due to their efficient charge-to-spin conversion. However, whether this conversion originates predominantly from the bulk or the interface remains unclear. Here, we investigate spin-charge interconversion in MoTe$_2$ using spin-pumping and spin-torque ferromagnetic resonance (SP-FMR and ST-FMR). Thickness-dependent measurements reveal large spin-to-charge conversion and spin-torque efficiencies that are essentially independent of MoTe$_2$ thickness, indicating that the conversion is predominantly governed by the Rashba-Edelstein effect at the Py/MoTe$_2$ interface rather than by bulk spin transport. This behavior contrasts with the characteristic thickness dependence observed in Pt heterostructures and is further supported by bidirectional SP-FMR and interface-separation measurements. Our results highlight the dominant role of the Py/MoTe$_2$ interface in enabling efficient spin-charge conversion and spin-orbit torques in TMD-based spintronic devices. These findings highlight the potential of sputtered MoTe$_2$/Py heterostructures for low-power spintronic applications, including magnetic memory and logic devices.

</details>


### [155] [Device characterization of Si$/$SiGe double quantum dots using exchange oscillations in Earth's magnetic field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31093)
*Holly G. Stemp,Harry Hanlim Kang,Chih Hwan Yang,Gabriel D. Cutter,Frederike Brockmeyer,Patrick J. Strohbeen,Max Hays,Jeffrey A. Grover,William D. Oliver*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates a zero-applied-field diagnostic for exchange-based semiconductor qubits by using the intrinsic magnetic-field gradient from residual nuclear spins in isotopically enriched Si/SiGe to perform exchange oscillations between two quantum dots, without requiring micromagnets, dynamic nuclear polarization, or multi-axis calibration. Using CPMG sequences, singlet coherence is extended from T2*=1.17±0.02 μs to T2_CPMG=74.8±1.8 μs with 70 refocusing pulses, and residual exchange in the tens-of-kilohertz regime is resolved and mapped across the (1,1) charge cell.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exchange-based semiconductor qubits require characterization of environmental noise, residual idle interactions, and exchange-dependent decay, but existing characterization methods rely on multi-axis control calibration or deliberately engineered magnetic-field gradients. A simpler zero-applied-field diagnostic is especially valuable for hybrid semiconductor-superconductor systems where magnetic fields degrade superconducting components.

Method: The authors use the intrinsic magnetic-field gradient produced by residual nuclear spins in isotopically enriched Si/SiGe to drive exchange oscillations between two quantum dots as a characterization tool. They employ Carr-Purcell-Meiboom-Gill (CPMG) exchange sequences to extend singlet coherence and use the oscillation phase to resolve and map residual exchange across the (1,1) charge cell, all without micromagnets, dynamic nuclear polarization, or prior multi-axis calibration.

Result: CPMG exchange sequences extended singlet coherence from T2*=1.17±0.02 μs to T2_CPMG=74.8±1.8 μs with N=70 refocusing pulses. The oscillation phase resolved residual exchange in the tens-of-kilohertz regime and enabled its mapping across the (1,1) charge cell.

Conclusion: Intrinsic-gradient exchange oscillations establish a simple, more relevant zero-field diagnostic for exchange-only and related semiconductor qubit encodings, amenable to rapid, high-throughput device characterization.

Abstract: Exchange-based semiconductor qubits encompass a broad family of encodings constructed from singlet- and triplet-like spin states, several of which are compatible with operation at zero applied magnetic field. Their reliable operation requires characterization of environmental noise, residual idle interactions, and exchange-dependent decay, but this characterization often relies on multi-axis control calibration or deliberately engineered magnetic-field gradients. A simpler zero-applied-field diagnostic is particularly valuable for hybrid semiconductor-superconductor systems, in which magnetic fields can degrade superconducting components. Here, we use the intrinsic magnetic-field gradient produced by residual nuclear spins in isotopically enriched Si/SiGe to implement exchange oscillations between two quantum dots as a characterization tool without a micromagnet, dynamic nuclear polarization, or prior multi-axis calibration. Using Carr-Purcell-Meiboom-Gill exchange sequences, we extend the singlet coherence from $T_2^*=1.17\pm0.02~μ$s to $T_2^{\mathrm{CPMG}}=74.8\pm1.8~μ$s with $N=70$ refocusing pulses. The oscillation phase resolves residual exchange in the tens-of-kilohertz regime and enables it to be mapped across the $(1,1)$ charge cell. These results establish intrinsic-gradient exchange oscillations as a simple, more relevant zero-field diagnostic for exchange-only and related semiconductor qubit encodings that is amenable to rapid, high-throughput device characterization.

</details>


### [156] [Carrier-Resolved Attosecond Valley Polarimetry of Monolayer MoS$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25121)
*Navdeep Rana,Lun Yue,Mette B. Gaarde*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes using attosecond transient absorption spectroscopy at the Mo 4p semicore edge as a quantitative valley polarimeter in monolayer MoS₂, enabling carrier-by-carrier readout of valley polarization on femtosecond/attosecond timescales that was previously impossible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While all-optical writing of valley polarization in 2D semiconductors has been demonstrated on femtosecond timescales, reading out this polarization on the same timescale, carrier by carrier, has remained an unsolved challenge. The authors aim to close this gap by providing a carrier-sensitive, quantitative readout tool.

Method: Semiconductor Bloch equation simulations of monolayer MoS₂ are employed to investigate attosecond transient absorption at the Mo 4p semicore edge. The approach exploits two distinct spectral channels: pump-enabled core-to-valence absorption to probe holes, and core-to-conduction bleaching to probe electrons, so each carrier species is identified by its photoabsorption energy. Key metrics tested include circular dichroism sign reversal with pump helicity and the normalized dichroism magnitude proportional to valley polarization, as well as delay-scanning for real-time clocking of the write process.

Result: The hole channel emerges background-free since it is Pauli-blocked in equilibrium. The circular dichroism reverses sign with pump helicity, and its normalized magnitude is proportional to valley polarization. Scanning the probe delay enables real-time observation of the few-femtosecond write process. Thus, a single transient absorption spectrum identifies both electrons and holes by energy and quantifies valley polarization.

Conclusion: Attosecond core-level dichroism at the semicore edge constitutes a carrier-sensitive, quantitative probe of the creation and evolution of valley polarization, overcoming the previous limitation of not being able to read valley polarization on the same ultrafast timescale it is written.

Abstract: In recent years, all-optical writing and switching of valley polarization in two-dimensional semiconductors has been demonstrated on femtosecond timescales. Reading this polarization out on the same timescale, carrier by carrier, has so far remained out of reach. Employing semiconductor Bloch equation simulations of monolayer MoS$_2$, we show that attosecond transient absorption closes this gap by turning the Mo $4p$ semicore edge into a quantitative valley polarimeter. We find that pump-enabled core-to-valence absorption probes the holes while core-to-conduction bleaching probes the electrons, so that a single spectrum identifies each carrier by its photoabsorption energy. The hole channel, Pauli-blocked in equilibrium, emerges background-free. Its circular dichroism reverses sign with the pump helicity, and its normalized magnitude is proportional to the valley polarization. Finally, we show that scanning the probe delay clocks the few-femtosecond write in real time. Thus, attosecond core-level dichroism is a carrier-sensitive, quantitative probe of the creation and evolution of valley polarization.

</details>
